0.97
  A STI-0111 [stis] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle is an emergency. It can be testicular torsion, where the testicle twists and loses its blood supply, which can destroy the testicle within hours. Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.
  B WEL-0134 [wellbeing] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, severe pain in a testicle, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, can mean the testicle has twisted (testicular torsion). This is an emergency because the blood supply is cut off and the testicle can be lost within hours. Go to the nearest hospital or health facility immediately or call 112. Do not wait, and do not feel embarrassed; health workers see this often.

  A PUB-0125 [puberty] Sudden pain in a testicle: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle or the scrotum, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, is an emergency. It can mean the testicle has twisted and lost its blood supply. Go to a health facility now, or call 912 or 112. Treatment within a few hours can save the testicle. Do not wait to see if it goes away, and do not feel shy; health workers see this often.

Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.

0.93
  A PUB-0115 [puberty] Curved penis: It is common for an erect penis to curve a little to the left, right, up or down. This is usually normal and does not affect urinating or sex. See a health worker if the bend is strong, gets worse over time, is painful, or you feel a hard lump in the shaft. Masturbation does not cause a curve.
  B WEL-0106 [wellbeing] A curved penis: It is common for an erect penis to curve a little up, down or to one side. Most penises are not perfectly straight, and a slight curve is normal and does not affect sex or fertility. See a health worker if the curve is new, getting worse, painful, if you feel a hard lump in the penis, or if it makes sex difficult.

0.92
  A PUB-0129 [puberty] Testicle ache after being sexually excited: After being sexually excited for a long time without ejaculating, some boys feel a dull ache or heaviness in the testicles. This happens because extra blood flows to the area. It is uncomfortable but harmless, and it goes away by itself, usually within an hour or so. It is never a reason to pressure anyone into sex. Strong or lasting pain should be checked.
  B WEL-0118 [wellbeing] Testicle ache after arousal: Some boys and men feel a dull ache or heaviness in the testicles after being sexually aroused for a long time without ejaculating. This happens because extra blood flows to the area. It is uncomfortable but harmless and goes away on its own within a short time. It is never a reason to pressure anyone into sex. Severe or sudden pain, swelling or pain that lasts needs a health worker.

0.92
  A HIV-0027 [hiv] Sharing razors and blades: Sharing razors, blades or other sharp tools that may have blood on them carries a small risk of HIV and a bigger risk of other infections like hepatitis B. Small cuts while shaving can let blood pass. It is best to have your own razor and not to share. At the barber or salon, make sure tools are cleaned and disinfected or a new blade is used for you.
  B PUB-0054 [puberty] Sharing razors and blades: Do not share razors, blades or clippers with anyone, even family or friends. Shaving can cause tiny cuts, and a shared blade can carry blood from one person to another, which can spread infections such as HIV or hepatitis B and C. Use your own razor, keep it clean and dry, and throw away old blades safely. The same applies to tools used for cutting or piercing the skin.

0.92
  A PUB-0099 [puberty] Wiping from front to back: After using the toilet, girls should wipe from the front towards the back, away from the vagina and urethra. This stops germs from the anus moving forward, which can cause urinary or vaginal infections. Use clean toilet paper or water, and wash your hands with soap afterwards. Teaching young girls this habit early is very helpful.
  B WEL-0123 [wellbeing] Wiping front to back: Girls and women should wipe from front to back after using the toilet, from the vagina towards the anus. Wiping the other way can carry germs from the bottom to the urinary opening and vagina, causing urine infections and other infections. Use clean toilet paper or water and wash hands afterwards. Signs of a urine infection include burning when urinating and needing to go often; see a health worker if this happens.

0.92
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B SRV-0070 [services_rights] Age to test for HIV without a parent: According to Rwanda's HIV Guidelines, young people aged 12 and above can agree to an HIV test themselves, without needing a parent's permission. You will get counselling before and after the test, and your result is kept confidential. HIV testing is offered at health centres and some youth centres. Knowing your status is a smart, brave step for your health.

0.91
  A PUB-0068 [puberty] Skin lightening creams: Skin lightening or bleaching creams can be dangerous. Some contain mercury, strong steroids or other chemicals that can damage the skin, cause rashes, thin skin, dark patches, and harm the kidneys and nerves. Your natural skin colour is healthy and beautiful. If you have dark spots or uneven skin and want help, see a health worker instead of buying creams from the market.
  B WEL-0034 [wellbeing] Skin-lightening creams dangers: Many skin-lightening or bleaching creams contain harmful substances such as mercury or strong steroids. They can cause rashes, thin and damaged skin, dark patches, infections, and harm to the kidneys and nervous system. Some are sold without clear labels. Your natural skin colour is beautiful and healthy. For skin problems like acne or dark spots, ask a health worker for safe advice instead of using bleaching products.

0.89
  A PUB-0126 [puberty] Lump or swelling in a testicle: A new lump, hardness, or swelling in a testicle or the scrotum should be checked by a health worker soon, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as fluid collections or cysts, but some need treatment, and a few can be serious. Finding problems early makes treatment easier. If the swelling comes with sudden strong pain, go to a health facility immediately.
  B WEL-0133 [wellbeing] A lump on the testicle: A new lump, hard area or swelling on a testicle should always be checked by a health worker, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as cysts or fluid, but testicular cancer, though rare, is most common in young men and is very treatable when found early. Do not be shy or wait to see if it goes away. The check is quick and private.

0.89
  A HIV-0061 [hiv] What a reactive self-test result means: A reactive result on an HIV self-test means you might have HIV, but it is not a final diagnosis. You must go to a health facility for further testing to confirm the result. Try to go soon, and consider bringing someone you trust. If the result is confirmed, you can start free treatment right away and live a long, healthy life. You can call 114 for support and information.
  B HIV-0062 [hiv] What a non-reactive self-test result means: A non-reactive self-test result usually means HIV was not found. But if you had a possible exposure in the last few weeks or months, the test may not yet detect it, so you may need to test again later; a health worker can tell you when. If you are at ongoing risk, keep using condoms, test regularly, and ask about PrEP.

0.88
  A STI-0065 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in girls and women: Many girls and women with trichomoniasis have no signs. When signs appear, they may include a frothy yellow-green or grey vaginal discharge, often with a strong or fishy smell, itching or soreness around the vagina, burning when urinating, and discomfort during sex. These signs can also come from other infections such as bacterial vaginosis or yeast, so only a test at a health center can tell the cause.
  B STI-0066 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in boys and men: Most boys and men with trichomoniasis have no signs at all, which is why they can pass it on without knowing. Some notice itching or irritation inside the penis, a thin discharge, or burning after urinating or ejaculating. Signs may come and go. Because other infections cause similar signs, only a health worker can tell the cause. If your partner is diagnosed with trich, you need treatment too.

0.88
  A PUB-0139 [puberty] Myth: losing semen makes you weak: Losing semen through wet dreams, masturbation or sex does not make you weak, sick, thin, or less intelligent, and it does not drain your strength or blood. Semen is mostly water and the body makes new semen all the time. If you feel tired, it is more likely from lack of sleep, poor food, stress or illness. If you feel weak often, see a health worker.
  B WEL-0112 [wellbeing] Myth that losing semen makes you weak: Some people believe that ejaculating, wet dreams or masturbation drain a man's strength, blood or brain power. This is a myth. The body makes new sperm and semen all the time, and releasing semen does not cause weakness, illness, back pain or infertility. Feeling tired afterwards is normal and passes quickly. If you feel weak all the time, there is another cause, so see a health worker.

0.88
  A PUB-0071 [puberty] Why stretch marks appear: Stretch marks are lines on the skin that appear when the body grows quickly, as it often does in puberty. They are common on the hips, thighs, buttocks, breasts, back and arms, in both girls and boys. They may first look pink, purple or dark, and later fade. Stretch marks are harmless and very common. They do not mean you are unhealthy.
  B WEL-0038 [wellbeing] Stretch marks: Stretch marks are lines on the skin that appear when the body grows quickly, for example during puberty, weight gain or pregnancy. They are very common in both girls and boys and often appear on hips, thighs, breasts, belly or shoulders. They are not harmful and usually fade to a lighter colour with time. Creams cannot fully remove them, so avoid expensive or bleaching products. Many people have them; they are a normal sign of growth.

  A CSA-0035 [contraception] Talking to your partner about condoms: Talk about condoms before you are in a sexual moment, at a calm time when you are both relaxed. Be honest and simple: 'I care about us, and I want us both to be safe from pregnancy and infections.' Using condoms shows respect, not mistrust. Listen to your partner's worries and agree together. If you are not ready to have sex, it is also fine to say so.
  B R-004 [relationships] Talking to a partner about condoms: Talk about condoms before sex, when you are calm. You can say, "I want us both to be safe." Talking before sex makes it more likely that a condom will be used. If your partner refuses, you can say no to sex. A health worker can help you practise what to say.

  A PUB-0112 [puberty] Worry that the penis is too small: Many boys worry about penis size, but almost all penises are normal. Size varies a lot, and a soft penis can look very different from the same penis when erect. Looking down at your own body makes it look shorter than it is. Penises in pornography are not typical. The penis keeps growing through puberty. If you are still very worried, a health worker can check privately.
  B WEL-0105 [wellbeing] Penis size worries: Many boys and men worry their penis is too small, often because of jokes or pornography, which shows unusual sizes. Penises vary a lot in size when soft, but sizes are much more similar when erect. The penis keeps growing during puberty, often until the late teens. Size does not affect fertility, manhood or the ability to have a satisfying relationship. If you have real concerns, a health worker can check privately.

0.87
  A CSA-0030 [contraception] How effective male condoms are: When male condoms are used correctly every time, about 2 out of 100 women become pregnant in a year. In real life, when people sometimes forget or make mistakes, about 13 out of 100 become pregnant. So condoms work very well, but only if you use one correctly from start to finish every time. They are also the only method that protects against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs together.
  B CSA-0053 [contraception] How effective female condoms are: When the female condom is used correctly every time, about 5 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year. With typical use, when mistakes happen, about 21 out of 100 get pregnant. It works best when used every time, inserted properly, and the penis enters inside the condom. Using it with another method, like the pill, gives more pregnancy protection.

  A PUB-0127 [puberty] Getting hit in the testicles: Being hit or kicked in the testicles hurts a lot, and you may feel sick for a short time. Usually the pain fades within an hour. Lie down and use a cool cloth on the area. Go to a health facility if pain is severe or does not settle within an hour, if there is a lot of swelling or bruising, blood in the urine, or fever. Wear protective gear or supportive underwear during sport.
  B WEL-0136 [wellbeing] Injury to the testicles: Getting hit in the testicles, for example in football, is very painful, and mild pain usually settles within an hour. Rest, lie down and use a cool cloth wrapped around ice for short periods. Seek care quickly if pain is severe or does not improve, there is swelling, bruising, blood in urine, nausea or vomiting, as the testicle may be damaged. Wearing protection in contact sports helps prevent injury.

0.86
  A PUB-0114 [puberty] Products to make the penis bigger: No pill, cream, herb, exercise, pump or traditional remedy has been shown to make the penis permanently bigger, and some can injure the penis or contain harmful chemicals. Size depends mainly on genes and develops naturally in puberty. A normal penis works well for urinating, sex and having children. If you are worried, talk to a health worker instead of buying products.
  B WEL-0107 [wellbeing] Penis enlargement products: Pills, creams, herbs, oils, pumps or exercises sold to make the penis bigger do not work in a safe, lasting way. Some contain hidden medicines or chemicals that can be dangerous for the heart or skin, and some cause injury, swelling or infection. Sellers profit from people's worries. Penis size is decided by genes. If you are worried, talk to a health worker instead of risking your health.

  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.

  A CSA-0031 [contraception] Condoms and protection from HIV: Yes. Male and female condoms, used correctly every time, are very good at preventing HIV because they stop semen, vaginal fluids and blood from passing between partners. They protect against HIV during vaginal and anal sex. To be protected, use a condom for the whole act of sex, every time. You can also ask a health center about HIV testing and other ways to prevent HIV.
  B HIV-0041 [hiv] How well condoms protect against HIV: When used correctly every time, from start to finish, male and female condoms are very effective at preventing HIV and many other STIs, and they also prevent pregnancy. Most failures happen because condoms are not used every time, are put on late, are torn by nails or teeth, or are used with oil-based lubricants. Check the expiry date, open the packet carefully, and use a new condom for each act of sex. Free condoms are available at many health centers.

  A STI-0012 [stis] Gonorrhoea signs in boys and men: Boys and men with gonorrhoea often notice signs a few days after sex. These can include a thick white, yellow or green discharge from the penis, burning or pain when urinating, and sometimes pain or swelling in a testicle. Some men have no signs at all. Other infections can look the same, so only a test at a health center can tell. Please go soon, and avoid sex until you are checked.
  B STI-0013 [stis] Gonorrhoea signs in girls and women: Many girls and women with gonorrhoea have no signs, or the signs are mild and easy to confuse with other problems. Possible signs include more vaginal discharge than usual, burning when urinating, bleeding between periods or after sex, and pain in the lower belly. If it spreads to the womb and tubes, it can cause fever and strong belly pain. Only a test at a health center can tell what the cause is.

  A PUB-0009 [puberty] First sign of puberty in girls: For most girls the first sign of puberty is breast budding: a small, firm bump under one or both nipples, which can feel a bit sore. Soon after, hair may start to grow around the genitals and under the arms. A growth spurt follows, and the first period usually comes about two years after breasts start growing. Some girls notice pubic hair first, which is also normal.
  B PUB-0010 [puberty] First sign of puberty in boys: For most boys the first sign of puberty is that the testicles get bigger and the skin of the scrotum becomes thinner, a bit darker and hangs lower. Many boys do not notice this at first. Next, pubic hair starts to grow and the penis gets longer and wider. The growth spurt, voice change and facial hair usually come later.

  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B SRV-0070 [services_rights] Age to test for HIV without a parent: According to Rwanda's HIV Guidelines, young people aged 12 and above can agree to an HIV test themselves, without needing a parent's permission. You will get counselling before and after the test, and your result is kept confidential. HIV testing is offered at health centres and some youth centres. Knowing your status is a smart, brave step for your health.

  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0059 [hiv] How to get an HIV self-test kit in Rwanda: If you are 16 or older, you can ask for an HIV self-test kit at a health facility, and some places such as youth-friendly services may also offer them. Availability can differ from place to place. To find out where kits are available near you, ask a nurse at your health center or a community health worker, or call the free line 114.

0.85
  A HIV-0055 [hiv] Is HIV testing free in Rwanda: HIV testing is offered free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay to know your status. If anyone asks you for money for an HIV test at a public facility, you can ask the health center management or call 114 for information. Private clinics may have their own fees, so ask before testing there.
  B HIV-0141 [hiv] Is HIV treatment free in Rwanda: Yes. HIV treatment (ART), viral load tests and routine HIV care are provided free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay for your ARVs. If you face any problem getting your medicine, talk to the health facility or call 114.

  A PUB-0110 [puberty] Testicles of different sizes: Having one testicle slightly bigger than the other is common and usually normal, especially during puberty when they are growing. A testicle that is clearly much smaller, suddenly gets bigger, feels hard or lumpy, or is painful should be checked by a health worker. Checking your testicles from time to time helps you know what is normal for you.
  B WEL-0108 [wellbeing] Testicle size worries: Testicles grow during puberty and come in different sizes. It is normal for one testicle to be a little larger than the other. Size does not usually tell you about fertility or manhood. See a health worker if a testicle seems to be shrinking, one is missing from the scrotum, there is a lump or swelling, or there is pain. Steroid use can also shrink testicles.

0.84
  A STI-0097 [stis] What is bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis (BV) is a very common vaginal condition. It happens when the normal balance of bacteria in the vagina changes and some bacteria grow too much. It is not caused by poor hygiene. Many women with BV have no signs, while others notice a fishy smell. BV is usually not dangerous, but it can raise the risk of some infections, so it is worth getting checked.
  B STI-0099 [stis] What causes bacterial vaginosis: The exact cause of bacterial vaginosis is not fully known. It happens when the healthy bacteria in the vagina decrease and other bacteria increase. Things that make it more likely include washing or douching inside the vagina, using scented soaps, having a new sex partner or several partners, and not using condoms. Girls who have never had sex can also get it.

  A PUB-0026 [puberty] Lump under the nipple in a young girl: A small, firm, coin-sized lump just under the nipple in a girl starting puberty is usually a breast bud, the first stage of breast growth. It can be on one side first and be tender. This is normal and not cancer. The other side often follows within months. If a lump is somewhere else in the breast, grows quickly, is red and hot, or you are worried, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0039 [puberty] Painful lump under a boy's nipple: A tender, firm lump under one or both nipples is common in boys during puberty. It is usually normal breast tissue growing because of hormones and is not cancer. It often gets softer and disappears with time. Avoid squeezing or pressing it. See a health worker if the lump is hard, not under the nipple, growing fast, very painful, or has fluid or blood coming out.

0.83
  A MEN-0124 [menstruation] Stress and periods: Stress affects the part of the brain that controls your period hormones. During times of worry, such as exams, family problems, moving home or grief, your period may come late, early, be lighter, heavier, or even skip a month. Usually, periods return to normal when stress eases. Sleep, exercise, talking with someone you trust and making time to relax can help. If periods stay irregular or stress feels too much, talk to a health worker or call 114.
  B WEL-0008 [wellbeing] Stress delaying your period: Yes, strong stress can affect the hormones that control your cycle, so a period may come late or be skipped. Worry, family problems, illness, big changes in weight or poor sleep can all do this. But if you have had sex, a late period can also mean pregnancy, so a pregnancy test is the safest way to know. If periods stay irregular for several months, see a nurse at the health center.

  A CSA-0031 [contraception] Condoms and protection from HIV: Yes. Male and female condoms, used correctly every time, are very good at preventing HIV because they stop semen, vaginal fluids and blood from passing between partners. They protect against HIV during vaginal and anal sex. To be protected, use a condom for the whole act of sex, every time. You can also ask a health center about HIV testing and other ways to prevent HIV.
  B CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.

0.83
  A CSA-0001 [contraception] How to put on a male condom: First check the expiry date and make sure the packet is not torn. Open it gently with your fingers, never with teeth, scissors or a blade. Put the condom on when the penis is hard and before it touches your partner's genitals. Check that the rim is on the outside so it rolls down easily. Pinch the tip to squeeze out air, then roll it all the way down to the base. If you start putting it on the wrong way, throw it away and use a new one.
  B CSA-0050 [contraception] How to insert a female condom: Check the expiry date and open the packet carefully. Find a comfortable position, like squatting or lifting one leg. Squeeze the inner ring at the closed end and push it into the vagina as far as it will go. Put a finger inside the condom and push the inner ring up. The outer ring stays outside, covering the opening. During sex, guide the penis into the condom.

  A CSB-0037 [contraception] Late for the injection: If you are late for your injection, go to the health center as soon as you can. With DMPA, you can usually still get the injection if you are up to 4 weeks late. If you are later than that, the provider will check whether you might be pregnant before giving it. Until you get your injection, use condoms or avoid sex. Do not stay away because you feel embarrassed; providers want to help.
  B CSB-0038 [contraception] Getting the injection early: Yes. You can get your DMPA injection up to 2 weeks before your due date. This is useful if you will be travelling, going to boarding school or busy on the due date. Getting it a little early is safe and does not reduce protection. Talk to your provider and they will write the new date for your next injection on your card.

  A STI-0003 [stis] Chlamydia signs in girls and women: Most girls and women with chlamydia have no signs at all. When signs do appear, they can include unusual vaginal discharge, a burning feeling when urinating, bleeding between periods or after sex, pain during sex, or pain in the lower belly. These signs can also be caused by other infections, so only a test at a health center can tell what is going on. If you notice any of them, please see a nurse soon.
  B STI-0004 [stis] Chlamydia signs in boys and men: Many boys and men with chlamydia notice nothing. Some get a clear or cloudy discharge from the tip of the penis, burning or pain when urinating, itching inside the penis, or pain and swelling in one testicle. Infection in the anus or throat often has no signs. Other infections can cause the same problems, so the only way to know is a test at a health center. Testicle pain should always be checked quickly.

0.82
  A CON-0124 [consent_safety] What is Isange One Stop Centre: Isange One Stop Centres are places in Rwanda where survivors of gender-based violence and child abuse can get several services in one place: medical care, psychosocial support (counselling), legal advice and police/investigation services. This means survivors do not have to go from office to office telling their story many times. Isange centres are found in hospitals across the country. The toll-free number is 3029. Children, young people, women and men can all go there.
  B SRV-0095 [services_rights] What an Isange One Stop Centre is: Isange One Stop Centres are places, usually at hospitals, that support people who have experienced gender-based violence or child abuse. In one place you can get medical care, counselling, help with collecting evidence, and support from police and legal staff. Services are free, and staff are trained to be kind and respectful. You can call 3029 to find the nearest centre.

0.82
  A CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.
  B CSA-0148 [contraception] Sex again after taking EC: Emergency pills only protect against sex that happened before you took them. They do not protect you during the rest of the month. If you have sex again after taking EC, use a condom or start a regular method. Because EC can delay ovulation, you might release an egg later than usual and get pregnant.

  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0055 [hiv] Is HIV testing free in Rwanda: HIV testing is offered free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay to know your status. If anyone asks you for money for an HIV test at a public facility, you can ask the health center management or call 114 for information. Private clinics may have their own fees, so ask before testing there.

0.82
  A CSA-0032 [contraception] STIs that condoms protect against less well: Condoms protect very well against infections spread through fluids, like HIV, gonorrhoea and chlamydia. They give less protection against infections spread by skin-to-skin contact, such as herpes, genital warts (HPV) and syphilis sores, if the sore or wart is in an area the condom does not cover. Condoms still lower the risk a lot. If you see sores or warts, avoid sex and visit a health center.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.82
  A CSA-0025 [contraception] Reusing a condom: A condom can only be used once. After sex, a used condom has semen on it and may be stretched or have tiny tears, so it will not protect you again. Never wash, turn over or reuse a condom, even with the same partner. Use a new condom for every act of sex. Condoms are free at health centers and youth corners, so you do not need to save them.
  B CSA-0058 [contraception] Reusing a female condom: Female condoms are made to be used only once. Washing or reusing them is not recommended, because they may tear or carry germs and sperm. Use a new female condom each time you have sex, and throw the old one in a bin or pit latrine.

0.82
  A HIV-0066 [hiv] Is my HIV test result confidential: Yes. Your HIV test and result are confidential. Health workers must not tell your parents, teachers, partner, friends or anyone else without your permission, except other health workers caring for you. If you are worried about privacy, ask the counsellor how your information will be kept. You decide whom to tell and when.
  B SRV-0064 [services_rights] Privacy of HIV test results: Your HIV test result belongs to you. Health workers must keep it confidential and should not share it with your family, school, partner or anyone else without your agreement. If your result is positive, a counsellor can help you decide whom to tell and how, at your own pace. Your status should never be used to treat you badly at school, at work or in the community.

0.82
  A CSA-0019 [contraception] Lubricants that are safe with latex condoms: Lubricant (lube) makes sex more comfortable and helps stop condoms from tearing. With latex condoms, only use water-based or silicone-based lubricants, or clean water. Some condoms already come with lubricant on them. Many pharmacies sell water-based lube. Check the label, and if you are not sure, ask the pharmacist which lubricant is safe with condoms.
  B WEL-0102 [wellbeing] Choosing a lubricant with condoms: If you use a lubricant with latex condoms, choose a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Oil-based products like petroleum jelly, body lotion, cooking oil, shea butter or baby oil can weaken latex and make condoms break. Saliva is not a good lubricant and can carry infections. Some condoms already come lubricated. You can ask at a health center or pharmacy for a lubricant that is safe with condoms.

0.82
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0010 [puberty] First sign of puberty in boys: For most boys the first sign of puberty is that the testicles get bigger and the skin of the scrotum becomes thinner, a bit darker and hangs lower. Many boys do not notice this at first. Next, pubic hair starts to grow and the penis gets longer and wider. The growth spurt, voice change and facial hair usually come later.

0.82
  A CSB-0106 [contraception] Switching from pills to implant or injection: If you have been taking pills correctly, you can usually get an implant or injection any day, and you are protected right away. If you missed pills recently, your provider may ask you to use condoms for 7 days. You can stop taking pills after the implant or injection is given. Bring your pill pack to the visit if you can.
  B CSB-0108 [contraception] Switching from an implant to pills: If you want to change from the implant to pills, start your first pill on the day the implant is removed, or a day before. This way you stay protected. Ask the provider for your pills at the removal visit and how to take them. If there is a gap, use condoms until you have taken pills for 7 days.

0.82
  A PUB-0028 [puberty] Worry about small breasts: Breasts come in many sizes, and small breasts are completely normal and healthy. Breast size depends mostly on your genes and body fat, and they can keep growing until your late teens. Small breasts work just as well, including for breastfeeding a baby in the future. Your worth is not about your breast size. If people tease you, that is their problem, not yours.
  B WEL-0035 [wellbeing] Worries about breast size: Breasts come in many sizes and shapes, and it is normal for one breast to be slightly bigger than the other. Breasts may keep growing until your late teens. Size does not affect your health, femininity or ability to breastfeed. Wearing a comfortable, well-fitting bra can help you feel better. If you notice a lump, pain that does not go away or very fast changes, see a health worker.

0.82
  A PRB-0005 [pregnancy] What happens at the first antenatal visit: At your first antenatal visit, the health worker will ask about your health, your last period and any past pregnancies. They will check your weight, height and blood pressure and may examine your belly. You will be offered blood and urine tests, including tests for HIV, syphilis and anaemia. You may get a tetanus vaccine and iron and folic acid. They will estimate your due date and explain danger signs. It is a good time to ask any question.
  B PRB-0006 [pregnancy] What happens at later antenatal visits: At later antenatal visits, the health worker checks your blood pressure and weight, measures your belly to see if the baby is growing well, and listens to the baby's heartbeat. Near the end of pregnancy they check the baby's position. They may repeat some tests, continue your supplements and ask how you feel. They also help you make a birth plan and remind you of danger signs. Tell them about any pain, bleeding or worries.

0.82
  A CSA-0174 [contraception] Washing or peeing after sex myth: Peeing, washing the vagina, jumping, or pushing after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm swim into the womb very quickly, within minutes, and urine comes out of a different opening. Washing inside the vagina can also cause infections. If you had unprotected sex, emergency contraception within 5 days is the right step.
  B WEL-0127 [wellbeing] Peeing and washing after sex: Urinating after sex can help flush out germs and may lower the chance of a urine infection, especially for women. Washing the outside of the genitals with clean water is fine. However, peeing or washing after sex does not prevent pregnancy, HIV or other STIs. Do not wash inside the vagina. Condoms remain the best protection against infections, and contraception protects against pregnancy.

0.81
  A PUB-0124 [puberty] Checking your testicles: It is good for boys to check their testicles once a month, so they know what is normal. The best time is after a warm bath, when the scrotum is relaxed. Gently roll each testicle between your thumb and fingers. It is normal to feel a soft tube at the back, called the epididymis. If you notice a new lump, swelling, hardness, heaviness or pain, see a health worker.
  B WEL-0132 [wellbeing] Testicular self-examination: Checking your testicles once a month helps you know what is normal. The best time is during or after a warm bath when the skin is relaxed. Gently roll each testicle between your thumb and fingers. It should feel smooth and firm. The soft tube at the back (the epididymis) is normal. Look for lumps, swelling, hardness or changes in size. If you notice anything new, see a health worker.

0.81
  A PUB-0013 [puberty] Growth spurt in girls: Girls usually have their fastest growth in height early in puberty, often around ages 10 to 14, soon after breasts start growing. During this time you may grow several centimetres a year. Your hips also widen and body shape changes. After the first period, growth slows down, and most girls reach close to their adult height a couple of years after that.
  B PUB-0014 [puberty] Growth spurt in boys: Boys usually have their growth spurt a bit later than girls, often around ages 12 to 16. During the spurt a boy can grow quickly in height, then his shoulders get broader and muscles get stronger. That is why girls are often taller than boys for a while at the start of secondary school. Most boys catch up and keep growing for longer.

0.81
  A CSA-0041 [contraception] Are there tiny holes in condoms: This is a myth. Good quality condoms are tested and have no holes that let HIV, other germs or sperm pass through. Laboratory tests show that latex blocks even very tiny germs. When condoms fail, it is usually because they break, slip or are not used every time. Use condoms correctly and consistently and they protect you very well.
  B HIV-0042 [hiv] Myth that HIV passes through condom holes: It is a myth that HIV can pass through tiny holes in condoms. Good quality latex and other approved condoms block HIV, which cannot get through when the condom is not broken. Condoms are tested before they are sold or given out. It is also not true that condoms contain HIV or worms. Store condoms away from heat and sharp objects, check the expiry date, and use them correctly every time.

0.81
  A PRA-0026 [pregnancy] Sperm in a swimming pool or bath water: You cannot get pregnant from sperm in a swimming pool, lake or shared bath water. Sperm die quickly in water, especially water with chlorine, soap or heat, and they become very spread out. For pregnancy to happen, sperm must be put directly into or right at the vagina. Being in the same water as a boy, or after him, will not make you pregnant.
  B PRA-0027 [pregnancy] Having sex in water: Yes, you can get pregnant from having sex in water, such as a lake, pool or bath. When the penis is in the vagina, sperm go straight into the body, just as they would on land. Water does not wash sperm away or kill them before they enter. Sex in water also carries the same risk of STIs.

0.80
  A CON-0129 [consent_safety] When to call 112: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police and urgent help. Call it when someone is in immediate danger: being attacked, threatened with a weapon, locked in, seriously hurt, or when an attacker is still nearby. Say clearly where you are and what is happening. If it is not an emergency but you need advice or to report abuse, you can call 3512 (GBV), 116 (children) or 3029 (Isange).
  B SRV-0089 [services_rights] What the 112 line is for: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police help. Call it if you or someone else is in danger right now, for example if someone is being attacked, threatened or hurt, or if there is a serious emergency. Tell them clearly where you are and what is happening. For medical emergencies you can also call 912 for an ambulance.

0.80
  A CSA-0027 [contraception] Allergy to latex condoms: A few people are allergic to latex, the rubber in most condoms. Signs can include itching, redness, rash or swelling of the genitals after using a condom. Similar signs can also come from an infection, so see a nurse or health worker to find the cause. If it is a latex allergy, you can still use condoms made of polyurethane or nitrile, including the female condom.
  B CSA-0059 [contraception] Female condoms and latex allergy: Most female condoms are made of nitrile or polyurethane, not latex. This makes them a good choice if you or your partner are allergic to latex. They protect against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs. If you still get itching, swelling or rash, see a health worker to find the cause.

0.80
  A MEN-0088 [menstruation] How often to change underwear during a period: Change into clean underwear at least once a day during your period, and change it sooner if blood leaks onto it or it feels damp. Cotton underwear is best because it lets air through and is easy to wash. Wash used underwear in cold water first to remove blood, then with soap. Dry it in the sun. Having a spare pair in your bag is helpful in case of leaks.
  B PUB-0081 [puberty] How often to change underwear: Change your underwear every day, and more often if it gets wet from sweat or sport. Wash underwear with soap and water, rinse it well and dry it fully, ideally in the sun, before wearing it again. Cotton underwear is best because it lets the skin breathe. Do not share underwear with others. Clean, dry underwear helps prevent smell, itching and infections.

0.80
  A CSA-0002 [contraception] How to take off a condom after sex: Right after ejaculation, while the penis is still hard, hold the rim of the condom against the base of the penis and pull out slowly so no semen leaks. Move away from your partner's genitals before you slide the condom off. Be careful not to spill anything on your partner. If you want to have sex again, always use a brand new condom. Wash your hands after handling a used condom.
  B CSA-0051 [contraception] How to remove a female condom: After sex, and before standing up, hold the outer ring and twist it gently to close the condom and keep the semen inside. Then pull it out slowly. Wrap it in paper or a bag and put it in a bin or pit latrine, not a flush toilet. Use a new female condom every time you have sex.

0.80
  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PUB-0009 [puberty] First sign of puberty in girls: For most girls the first sign of puberty is breast budding: a small, firm bump under one or both nipples, which can feel a bit sore. Soon after, hair may start to grow around the genitals and under the arms. A growth spurt follows, and the first period usually comes about two years after breasts start growing. Some girls notice pubic hair first, which is also normal.

0.79
  A PRA-0057 [pregnancy] Herbs or traditional medicine to prevent pregnancy: There is no proof that herbs, roots or traditional mixtures prevent pregnancy. Some can make you very sick or harm your liver or kidneys. Relying on them can lead to an unplanned pregnancy. Safe and effective contraception and emergency contraception are available at health centers. A nurse or community health worker can explain your choices privately.
  B PRB-0027 [pregnancy] Traditional herbs during pregnancy: Some people use traditional herbs or remedies during pregnancy, for example to make labour faster. Many herbs have not been tested for safety, and some can harm the baby, cause strong contractions or bleeding. It is safest not to take herbal mixtures while pregnant or in labour unless a health worker says it is safe. If family members pressure you, you can explain that the nurse asked you to check first.

0.79
  A HIV-0016 [hiv] HIV risk from vaginal sex: Vaginal sex without a condom is one of the most common ways HIV is passed, both from man to woman and from woman to man. The virus in semen, pre-cum or vaginal fluids can enter through the thin lining of the vagina or the penis. Risk is higher if there are sores, other STIs, bleeding, or if the partner with HIV is not on treatment. Using a condom correctly every time greatly lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0022 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between women: The risk of HIV passing between two women during sex is very low, but it is possible if blood, such as menstrual blood or blood from cuts, or vaginal fluids pass between partners, or if sex toys are shared without cleaning or a new condom. Women who have sex with women can also have male partners or other risks. Using barriers, not sharing toys, and regular testing help keep you healthy.

0.79
  A STI-0048 [stis] HPV vaccine in Rwandan schools: Rwanda offers the HPV vaccine free to girls, usually around age 12, mainly through schools, with health workers coming to give it. It protects against the HPV types that cause most cervical cancer. Girls who are not in school can get it at a health center. If you are unsure whether you were vaccinated or how many doses you need, ask the school nurse, a health center or a community health worker, or call 114.
  B STI-0052 [stis] HPV vaccine and boys: Some countries vaccinate boys against HPV too, but in many countries, including Rwanda, programmes focus on girls because cervical cancer causes the most deaths. When most girls are vaccinated, boys are also indirectly protected because less HPV spreads in the community. Boys can protect themselves and partners by using condoms and limiting partners. Ask a health worker if you want to know about vaccine options for boys.

0.79
  A PRA-0050 [pregnancy] Peeing after sex does not prevent pregnancy: Peeing after sex does not stop pregnancy. Urine comes out of a different opening, the urethra, not the vagina. So peeing cannot wash sperm out of the vagina or womb. Peeing after sex can help lower the chance of a bladder infection, but it is not a way to avoid pregnancy.
  B WEL-0127 [wellbeing] Peeing and washing after sex: Urinating after sex can help flush out germs and may lower the chance of a urine infection, especially for women. Washing the outside of the genitals with clean water is fine. However, peeing or washing after sex does not prevent pregnancy, HIV or other STIs. Do not wash inside the vagina. Condoms remain the best protection against infections, and contraception protects against pregnancy.

0.79
  A PRA-0009 [pregnancy] When a boy becomes able to cause pregnancy: A boy can make a girl pregnant once his body starts making sperm during puberty. This often begins somewhere between about 11 and 15 years old, but it differs for each boy. A sign is having wet dreams or ejaculating, but sperm may be present even before he notices this. So a young boy who has started puberty can cause a pregnancy, even the first time he has sex.
  B PUB-0138 [puberty] When a boy can make a girl pregnant: Once a boy's body starts making sperm in puberty, he can make a girl pregnant, even if he is young, small, has no beard yet, or has only just had his first ejaculation. Sperm can be present even before he notices semen. So any sex where the penis enters or touches the vagina can lead to pregnancy. Talk to a health worker or call 114 for advice on protection.

0.79
  A MEN-0022 [menstruation] Discharge before the period starts: In the days before your period, discharge is often thicker, white or creamy, and sometimes slightly sticky. This is caused by the hormone progesterone and is normal. Some girls also see a little brown discharge just before bleeding starts. If the discharge is lumpy and itchy, green or grey, smells bad, or comes with pain, see a nurse at your health center.
  B PUB-0096 [puberty] White discharge before the first period: Many girls notice a white or clear, slightly sticky fluid in their underwear several months before their first period. This is normal vaginal discharge caused by rising hormones. It keeps the vagina clean and healthy. It may change a little in amount through the month. See a health worker if it smells bad, is green, grey or like cottage cheese, or comes with itching, pain or sores.

0.79
  A CON-0045 [consent_safety] What is sexual abuse: Sexual abuse is any sexual act or contact that a person does not agree to, cannot agree to, or is forced, tricked or pressured into. It includes unwanted touching, rape, forcing someone to touch another person, showing sexual images, sexual comments or messages, and taking sexual photos. Any sexual activity with a child is abuse. Abuse can be done by strangers, but most often by someone the person knows. It is never the victim's fault. Help: 116, 3512 or Isange on 3029.
  B CON-0046 [consent_safety] What is child sexual abuse: Child sexual abuse is when an adult, or an older or more powerful young person, involves a child (anyone under 18) in sexual activity. A child cannot consent to sex, so it is always abuse, even if the child seemed to agree or did not resist. It includes touching, rape, showing sexual images, making sexual photos or videos, and sexual talk. It is a serious crime in Rwanda. The child is never to blame. Report it to 116, 3512, Isange (3029) or police on 112.

0.79
  A PRB-0028 [pregnancy] Healthy eating in pregnancy: A healthy pregnancy diet includes a variety of foods every day: staples like sweet potatoes, cassava, maize or rice; beans, peas, groundnuts, eggs, fish or meat for protein; dark green vegetables like dodo (amaranth) and cassava leaves; fruits; and milk if you can. Eat an extra small meal or snack each day. Use iodised salt, limit sugary drinks and very salty food, and drink clean water. Good food helps your baby grow and keeps you strong.
  B PRB-0037 [pregnancy] Eating well on a small budget in pregnancy: You can eat well in pregnancy without expensive food. Beans, peas, groundnuts and small dried fish are cheap sources of protein and iron. Dark green leaves like amaranth and pumpkin or cassava leaves are nutritious and can be grown at home. Eggs, seasonal fruits, sweet potatoes and sorghum porridge are also good. If your family is struggling to get enough food, tell your community health worker or health center, as support programs may exist.

0.79
  A CON-0092 [consent_safety] Why gender-based violence happens: Gender-based violence happens mainly because of unequal power between men and women and harmful beliefs, such as men must control women, women must obey, or violence is a private family matter. Alcohol, poverty and stress can make it worse, but they are not excuses. The person who chooses violence is responsible, not the victim. GBV can be prevented through equality, respect, education and speaking out. Report violence to 3512 or 112.
  B CON-0095 [consent_safety] Health effects of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence can harm health in many ways: injuries, chronic pain, unwanted pregnancy, HIV and other infections, problems during pregnancy, depression, anxiety, trouble sleeping, alcohol or drug use, and thoughts of self-harm. Children who experience or see violence may struggle at school and with feelings. Getting care early protects health. Isange One Stop Centres (3029) and health centres offer medical and psychosocial care, and 3512 can guide survivors.

0.79
  A PRA-0022 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex during your period: Pregnancy from sex during a period is less likely, but it is possible. Sperm can live up to about 5 days inside the body. If your period is short or your cycle is short or irregular, you could ovulate soon after your period ends, and sperm from sex during your period could still be alive. So sex during a period is not a reliable way to avoid pregnancy.
  B PRA-0023 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex right after your period: Yes, you can get pregnant from sex right after your period ends. Some people, especially those with short or irregular cycles, ovulate only a few days after bleeding stops. Sperm can live up to about 5 days and wait for the egg. So the days just after a period are not safe days. If you had unprotected sex, see a health worker if you are worried.

0.79
  A STI-0021 [stis] First stage of syphilis: painless sore: The first sign of syphilis is often a single sore, called a chancre. It usually appears around 3 weeks after infection where the bacteria entered, such as the penis, vagina, anus or mouth. It is usually firm, round and painless, so many people do not notice it. The sore heals on its own in a few weeks, but the infection stays in the body. Any genital sore should be checked at a health center.
  B STI-0022 [stis] Second stage of syphilis: rash: If syphilis is not treated, a second stage can start weeks after the sore. The most common sign is a rash that does not usually itch, often on the palms of the hands and soles of the feet, but it can be anywhere. Some people also get fever, tiredness, swollen glands, sore throat, patchy hair loss or sores in the mouth. These signs go away by themselves, but the infection does not. Get tested at a health center.

0.79
  A PUB-0072 [puberty] Can stretch marks go away: Stretch marks usually fade over time, becoming lighter and less noticeable, but they often do not disappear completely. Creams and oils sold to remove them have little proven effect, though moisturising can keep skin soft. Most people have some stretch marks, and they are nothing to be ashamed of. If the marks are very wide, many, and appear with other changes, a health worker can check.
  B WEL-0038 [wellbeing] Stretch marks: Stretch marks are lines on the skin that appear when the body grows quickly, for example during puberty, weight gain or pregnancy. They are very common in both girls and boys and often appear on hips, thighs, breasts, belly or shoulders. They are not harmful and usually fade to a lighter colour with time. Creams cannot fully remove them, so avoid expensive or bleaching products. Many people have them; they are a normal sign of growth.

0.79
  A CSA-0175 [contraception] What fertility awareness methods are: Fertility awareness methods help a woman know which days of her cycle she could get pregnant. On those fertile days, the couple avoids sex or uses condoms. Examples are the Standard Days Method with CycleBeads, the calendar method, the TwoDay Method using vaginal secretions, and the body temperature method. They need regular cycles or careful daily attention, and partner cooperation.
  B CSA-0182 [contraception] How effective fertility awareness is: Fertility awareness methods can work well for couples who use them carefully, but mistakes are common. For example, with the Standard Days Method about 12 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year with typical use, and about 5 with correct use. Other methods have similar or wider ranges. They are less effective than pills, injections, implants or IUDs.

0.79
  A CSA-0136 [contraception] Copper IUD as emergency contraception: A copper IUD put into the womb within 5 days after unprotected sex is the most effective emergency contraception, preventing more than 99 out of 100 possible pregnancies. A trained provider at a health facility puts it in. It works even for people for whom pills may work less well. After that, it can stay in as a long-term method.
  B CSA-0156 [contraception] Keeping a copper IUD after using it as EC: If you get a copper IUD as emergency contraception, you can keep it as your regular method. It protects against pregnancy for many years and can be removed by a provider whenever you want. If you prefer another method, the IUD can be taken out at a later visit after the provider confirms you are not pregnant.

0.78
  A STI-0097 [stis] What is bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis (BV) is a very common vaginal condition. It happens when the normal balance of bacteria in the vagina changes and some bacteria grow too much. It is not caused by poor hygiene. Many women with BV have no signs, while others notice a fishy smell. BV is usually not dangerous, but it can raise the risk of some infections, so it is worth getting checked.
  B STI-0101 [stis] Treating bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis can be treated with antibiotics given by a health worker, as tablets or a gel. Finish all the medicine, even if the smell goes away quickly, and avoid alcohol if the health worker tells you to. BV sometimes comes back; if it does, go back to the health center. Avoid douching, scented soaps, or putting anything inside the vagina to remove the smell.

0.78
  A CSA-0025 [contraception] Reusing a condom: A condom can only be used once. After sex, a used condom has semen on it and may be stretched or have tiny tears, so it will not protect you again. Never wash, turn over or reuse a condom, even with the same partner. Use a new condom for every act of sex. Condoms are free at health centers and youth corners, so you do not need to save them.
  B CSA-0026 [contraception] Washing a condom to use again: Washing a condom does not make it safe to use again. Soap, water and rubbing can weaken it and cause tiny holes you cannot see, and it may still carry sperm or germs. Condoms are made for one use only. Throw it away after sex and use a new one next time.

0.78
  A HIV-0116 [hiv] PEP after rape: If you were raped, go as soon as possible to the nearest health facility or Isange One Stop Centre, or call 3029 (free). They can give PEP within 72 hours to prevent HIV, emergency contraception, treatment for STIs, counselling, and help with reporting if you choose. Try not to wash or change clothes if you want to keep evidence, but do not delay care. It was not your fault.
  B HIV-0123 [hiv] Care after rape besides PEP: After rape, PEP is only one part of care. At a health facility or Isange One Stop Centre (call 3029), you can also get emergency contraception to prevent pregnancy, tests and treatment for other STIs, care for injuries, counselling, and help with police and legal support if you choose. Follow-up visits are important. You deserve care and support, and what happened was not your fault.

0.78
  A HIV-0081 [hiv] Feeling scared to take an HIV test: It is very normal to feel afraid of an HIV test. Many people worry about the result or what others will think. Remember that knowing your status is a way of caring for yourself. If the result is negative, you can stay protected. If it is positive, free treatment lets you stay healthy, and starting early is best. You can bring a trusted friend or adult, and counsellors are there to support you.
  B WEL-0003 [wellbeing] Anxiety while waiting for HIV test results: Feeling anxious before or after an HIV test is normal. Many health centers give results the same day, and a counsellor will explain what they mean and support you whatever the result. If the result is positive, treatment today lets people live long, healthy lives. While you wait, breathe slowly, stay with a trusted friend or family member, and avoid alcohol. You can call 114 for free to talk about your worries and ask questions.

0.78
  A PRB-0001 [pregnancy] What antenatal care is: Antenatal care (ANC) is the regular health check-ups you get from a nurse, midwife or doctor while you are pregnant. At these visits the health worker checks your health and the baby's growth, offers tests, gives vaccines and supplements, and answers your questions. ANC also helps you plan where and how you will give birth. In Rwanda you can get antenatal care at your nearest health center. It is for every pregnant person, whatever your age or situation.
  B PRB-0002 [pregnancy] Why antenatal care matters: Antenatal care helps you and your baby stay healthy. Health workers can find problems early, like high blood pressure, anaemia, infections or a baby that is not growing well, and treat them before they become dangerous. You get vaccines, supplements and advice on food, rest and danger signs. Mothers who attend antenatal care regularly are more likely to have a safe birth and a healthy baby. Even if you feel fine, the check-ups are still important.

0.78
  A CSA-0047 [contraception] Using condoms when drinking alcohol: Alcohol and drugs make it harder to think clearly. People who are drunk are more likely to forget condoms, use them wrongly, or have sex they did not plan or agree to. If you drink, carry condoms and decide before you go out. Remember that a person who is very drunk cannot give consent to sex. If something happened that you did not agree to, you can get help at Isange One Stop Centre (3029).
  B WEL-0071 [wellbeing] Alcohol and condom mistakes: When people are drunk or high, they often forget condoms, put them on wrongly, or do not notice when a condom breaks or slips. This raises the risk of pregnancy, HIV and other infections. If you might have sex, decide before drinking and keep condoms with you. If a condom was not used or failed, go to a health center as soon as possible, ideally within 72 hours, for advice about emergency contraception and HIV prevention.

0.78
  A CSA-0060 [contraception] Lubricant with female condoms: Female condoms usually come with lubricant already on them. Because they are made of nitrile or polyurethane, you can add any type of lubricant, including water-based, silicone-based or oil-based ones, without damaging them. Extra lubricant can make sex more comfortable and reduce noise. Do not use a male condom at the same time.
  B WEL-0102 [wellbeing] Choosing a lubricant with condoms: If you use a lubricant with latex condoms, choose a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Oil-based products like petroleum jelly, body lotion, cooking oil, shea butter or baby oil can weaken latex and make condoms break. Saliva is not a good lubricant and can carry infections. Some condoms already come lubricated. You can ask at a health center or pharmacy for a lubricant that is safe with condoms.

0.78
  A CSB-0060 [contraception] Does IUD insertion hurt: Many people feel cramping or discomfort while the IUD is inserted, similar to period pain, and some feel more. It usually passes quickly. Breathing slowly and relaxing helps, and a provider can explain each step and stop if you need a moment. You can ask about pain relief. Some cramps for a day or two afterwards are normal. You can always ask questions before deciding.
  B CSB-0061 [contraception] Cramps after IUD insertion: Cramping and light bleeding are common for a few days after IUD insertion. Rest, a warm cloth on your belly, and simple pain relief recommended by a health worker or pharmacist can help. Cramps usually improve within days. If you have severe belly pain, fever, chills, or bad-smelling discharge in the weeks after insertion, go to a health center quickly, as these can be signs of infection.

0.78
  A PUB-0026 [puberty] Lump under the nipple in a young girl: A small, firm, coin-sized lump just under the nipple in a girl starting puberty is usually a breast bud, the first stage of breast growth. It can be on one side first and be tender. This is normal and not cancer. The other side often follows within months. If a lump is somewhere else in the breast, grows quickly, is red and hot, or you are worried, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0036 [puberty] Breast lump in a teenage girl: Breasts can feel lumpy during puberty because they are made of glands and tissue that are still growing. Most lumps in teenage girls are harmless, and breast cancer is very rare at this age. Still, if you find a new lump that does not go away after your next period, grows, is hard, or comes with skin or nipple changes, see a health worker so they can examine it.

0.78
  A CSA-0020 [contraception] Vaseline, oil or lotion with condoms: Do not use oil-based products with latex condoms. Things like petroleum jelly (Vaseline), cooking oil, butter, body lotion, baby oil and some creams can weaken latex within a short time and make it break, even if you cannot see damage. Use water-based or silicone-based lubricant instead. If you need an oil-based product, choose a non-latex condom made of polyurethane or nitrile.
  B WEL-0102 [wellbeing] Choosing a lubricant with condoms: If you use a lubricant with latex condoms, choose a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Oil-based products like petroleum jelly, body lotion, cooking oil, shea butter or baby oil can weaken latex and make condoms break. Saliva is not a good lubricant and can carry infections. Some condoms already come lubricated. You can ask at a health center or pharmacy for a lubricant that is safe with condoms.

0.78
  A CON-0129 [consent_safety] When to call 112: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police and urgent help. Call it when someone is in immediate danger: being attacked, threatened with a weapon, locked in, seriously hurt, or when an attacker is still nearby. Say clearly where you are and what is happening. If it is not an emergency but you need advice or to report abuse, you can call 3512 (GBV), 116 (children) or 3029 (Isange).
  B WEL-0069 [wellbeing] When to call 112 instead of 114: Call 112 when someone is in immediate danger and needs police or emergency help now, for example violence, someone unconscious, an overdose, severe injury or someone at risk of harming themselves. Call 114, the free RBC health line, for health questions, advice and support when it is not an emergency, such as stress, worries about sex or where to find services. If unsure and someone could be hurt, call 112.

0.78
  A STI-0030 [stis] How herpes spreads: Herpes spreads through skin-to-skin contact with the genitals, anus or mouth of someone who has the virus, during vaginal, anal or oral sex or close genital touching. It spreads most easily when sores are present, but can also pass when there are no sores. Oral herpes (cold sores) can be passed to the genitals through oral sex. Condoms lower the risk but do not cover all skin. Avoid sex when sores are present.
  B STI-0034 [stis] Herpes spreading without sores: Yes, herpes can spread even when no sores are visible. Sometimes the virus is active on the skin without causing signs, and it can pass during sex. This is how many people get herpes from a partner who did not know they had it. The risk is highest when sores are present, so avoid sex then. Using condoms and, for some people, medicine from a health worker lowers the risk at other times.

0.78
  A WEL-0113 [wellbeing] Blood after first sex: Some girls and women notice a little bleeding the first time they have vaginal sex, and many do not. A small amount of light bleeding that stops within a day or so is usually not serious. Heavy bleeding, bleeding that does not stop, strong pain, fever or bad-smelling discharge need care: go to a health center. If the sex was not your choice, you deserve care and support; please go to a health facility quickly.
  B WEL-0116 [wellbeing] Bleeding after sex: Bleeding after sex that is not your first time, or that happens again and again, should be checked. It can be caused by dryness, small tears, infections including STIs, changes on the cervix, or bleeding linked to contraception. Most causes can be treated, but some need early care. Please see a nurse or health worker at the health center, especially if you also have pain, unusual discharge or bleeding between periods.

0.78
  A CSA-0034 [contraception] Feeling shy to get condoms: Many people feel shy about getting condoms, but choosing to protect yourself is responsible and nothing to be ashamed of. Pharmacists and health workers give condoms every day and are there to help, not to judge. Youth corners are made for young people. You can go with a trusted friend, take them from a free condom box, or simply say 'condoms, please'.
  B SRV-0033 [services_rights] Buying condoms without feeling ashamed: Buying condoms is a responsible choice that protects your health and your partner's health. Pharmacists and shop workers sell condoms every day, and you do not need to explain why you want them. You can simply say: condoms, please. If buying feels hard, you can get condoms free at health centres, youth corners and some youth centres, or ask your community health worker. You have nothing to be ashamed of.

0.78
  A PUB-0007 [puberty] What causes puberty to start: Puberty starts when a part of your brain sends chemical messages, called hormones, to your body. In girls these messages tell the ovaries to make more estrogen. In boys they tell the testicles to make more testosterone. These hormones cause the changes you notice, like body hair, growth, breasts or a deeper voice. Nothing you do or eat switches it on. It happens naturally when your body is ready.
  B PUB-0010 [puberty] First sign of puberty in boys: For most boys the first sign of puberty is that the testicles get bigger and the skin of the scrotum becomes thinner, a bit darker and hangs lower. Many boys do not notice this at first. Next, pubic hair starts to grow and the penis gets longer and wider. The growth spurt, voice change and facial hair usually come later.

0.78
  A CSA-0050 [contraception] How to insert a female condom: Check the expiry date and open the packet carefully. Find a comfortable position, like squatting or lifting one leg. Squeeze the inner ring at the closed end and push it into the vagina as far as it will go. Put a finger inside the condom and push the inner ring up. The outer ring stays outside, covering the opening. During sex, guide the penis into the condom.
  B CSA-0051 [contraception] How to remove a female condom: After sex, and before standing up, hold the outer ring and twist it gently to close the condom and keep the semen inside. Then pull it out slowly. Wrap it in paper or a bag and put it in a bin or pit latrine, not a flush toilet. Use a new female condom every time you have sex.

0.77
  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B CSA-0055 [contraception] Where to get female condoms in Rwanda: Female condoms can be found at some health centers, youth corners and youth-friendly services, and in some pharmacies. They are less common than male condoms, so ask a nurse or pharmacist. If they do not have them, they can tell you where to find them. You can also call 114, the free RBC line, for information.

0.77
  A STI-0097 [stis] What is bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis (BV) is a very common vaginal condition. It happens when the normal balance of bacteria in the vagina changes and some bacteria grow too much. It is not caused by poor hygiene. Many women with BV have no signs, while others notice a fishy smell. BV is usually not dangerous, but it can raise the risk of some infections, so it is worth getting checked.
  B STI-0100 [stis] Is bacterial vaginosis an STI: Bacterial vaginosis is not counted as a typical STI, and it can happen to girls who have never had sex. But it is linked to sex: it is more common with new or multiple partners, and condoms can lower the risk. Having BV can also make it easier to catch STIs, including HIV. Male partners usually do not need treatment, but a health worker will advise you.

0.77
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B HIV-0080 [hiv] Can someone test me for HIV without my consent: HIV testing should be voluntary. Health workers should explain the test and get your agreement before testing you, and young people 12 and older in Rwanda can give their own consent. No one, including a partner, school or employer, should force you to test or test you secretly. If you feel your rights were not respected, you can talk to the health facility management or call 114.

0.77
  A HIV-0095 [hiv] Where to get PrEP in Rwanda: PrEP is offered at health facilities in Rwanda that provide HIV services. Ask a nurse at your health center or youth corner. They will talk with you about your risk, test you for HIV, and check if PrEP is right for you. Not every site may offer it, so if your health center does not, they can refer you. You can also call 114 to ask where PrEP is available.
  B HIV-0108 [hiv] Is PrEP free in Rwanda: PrEP is provided free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda that offer it to people who need it. You will not be asked to pay for the pills or HIV tests that come with PrEP at public facilities. Ask your health center or youth corner about PrEP, or call 114 for information.

0.77
  A CSA-0019 [contraception] Lubricants that are safe with latex condoms: Lubricant (lube) makes sex more comfortable and helps stop condoms from tearing. With latex condoms, only use water-based or silicone-based lubricants, or clean water. Some condoms already come with lubricant on them. Many pharmacies sell water-based lube. Check the label, and if you are not sure, ask the pharmacist which lubricant is safe with condoms.
  B CSA-0060 [contraception] Lubricant with female condoms: Female condoms usually come with lubricant already on them. Because they are made of nitrile or polyurethane, you can add any type of lubricant, including water-based, silicone-based or oil-based ones, without damaging them. Extra lubricant can make sex more comfortable and reduce noise. Do not use a male condom at the same time.

0.77
  A CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.77
  A PRB-0005 [pregnancy] What happens at the first antenatal visit: At your first antenatal visit, the health worker will ask about your health, your last period and any past pregnancies. They will check your weight, height and blood pressure and may examine your belly. You will be offered blood and urine tests, including tests for HIV, syphilis and anaemia. You may get a tetanus vaccine and iron and folic acid. They will estimate your due date and explain danger signs. It is a good time to ask any question.
  B PRB-0010 [pregnancy] Preparing for an antenatal visit: Before an antenatal visit, gather your antenatal booklet, your mutuelle card and any vaccination card you have. Write down questions or worries so you do not forget them, such as pains, discharge, sleep or food. Eat something before you go, as you may wait for a while, and bring water. You can bring your partner or a trusted person. Be honest with the health worker about your health, medicines and feelings; it helps them care for you.

0.77
  A CSA-0123 [contraception] How EC pills work: Emergency contraceptive pills work mainly by stopping or delaying the release of an egg from the ovary. If no egg is released while sperm are still alive in the body, fertilisation cannot happen. They do not work after an egg has been fertilised and they do not end a pregnancy that has already started.
  B CSA-0129 [contraception] How effective EC pills are: Emergency contraceptive pills greatly reduce the chance of pregnancy but do not remove it. For example, if 100 women had unprotected sex once in the middle of their cycle, about 8 might become pregnant without EC, but only about 1 or 2 with EC pills. They work best when taken as soon as possible. The copper IUD is even more effective as EC.

0.77
  A PUB-0030 [puberty] Creams and herbs to grow breasts: No cream, herb, pill, food or massage has been shown to make breasts grow bigger. Breast size is decided mostly by your genes and body weight. Products that promise bigger breasts can waste money, and some contain hormones or chemicals that may harm you. Your breasts may still be growing until your late teens. Your body is fine as it is. Talk to a health worker before using any product.
  B WEL-0036 [wellbeing] Breast enlargement creams and pills: Creams, pills, herbs or massages sold to make breasts bigger do not work in a safe or lasting way. Some contain hormones or unknown substances that can harm your health, cause irregular bleeding or skin problems. Breast size is decided mostly by your genes and body fat, and breasts can keep developing until your late teens. Please do not spend money or risk your health on these products.

0.77
  A CSA-0126 [contraception] Using EC more than once: Yes, you can use emergency contraceptive pills more than once, and every time you need them.
