Using EC again is safe and does not cause infertility, cancer or other long-term harm. But if you often need EC, a regular method like pills, an injection, an implant or an IUD will protect you better. A health worker can help you choose.
  B CSA-0127 [contraception] EC twice in the same cycle: If you have unprotected sex again after taking EC, even in the same month, you can take emergency contraceptive pills again. It is safe. Your next period may be more irregular. Remember that EC only protects against sex that happened before you took it, so start a regular method or use condoms to stay protected.

0.77
  A PUB-0142 [puberty] Never having a wet dream: Not every boy has wet dreams. Some have many, some have only a few, and some never have them at all. All of these are normal and do not mean anything is wrong with your body or your fertility. Wet dreams may also happen less often if you ejaculate in other ways. If you have no other signs of puberty by around 14, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0143 [puberty] How often wet dreams happen: How often wet dreams happen is different for everyone. Some boys have them several times a month, others only now and then, and they often become less frequent as boys get older or start ejaculating in other ways. There is no number that is too many or too few. Having many wet dreams does not cause weakness or illness.

0.77
  A STI-0103 [stis] Signs of a UTI: Signs of a urinary tract infection include burning or pain when urinating, needing to urinate often or urgently but passing only a little, cloudy or bad-smelling urine, blood in the urine, and pain or pressure in the lower belly. STIs can cause some of the same signs, so see a health worker, who may test your urine to find the cause.
  B STI-0106 [stis] UTI danger signs: A UTI can spread to the kidneys if not treated. Go to a health facility today if you have burning urine together with fever or chills, pain in your back or side under the ribs, nausea or vomiting, or feel very unwell. These can be signs of a kidney infection, which needs quick treatment. If someone is very weak or confused, call 112 or 912 for an ambulance.

0.77
  A STI-0044 [stis] HPV and cervical cancer: Almost all cervical cancer is caused by long-lasting infection with high-risk types of HPV. When these types stay in the cervix (the opening of the womb) for many years, they can slowly change the cells, and these changes can turn into cancer if not found and treated. This process usually takes 10 to 20 years or more. HPV vaccination before sexual activity and regular screening later in life can prevent most cervical cancer.
  B STI-0055 [stis] What is cervical cancer: Cervical cancer is a cancer that grows in the cervix, the lower part of the womb that opens into the vagina. It is one of the most common cancers in women in Africa, and almost all cases are caused by long-lasting HPV infection. It usually develops slowly over many years, which gives time to find and treat early changes. It can be prevented through HPV vaccination and regular screening, and treated if found early.

0.77
  A STI-0063 [stis] What is trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis, often called trich, is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by a tiny parasite. It mostly infects the vagina in women and the urethra (the tube inside the penis) in men. Many people have no signs, especially men. It can be easily cured with the right medicine from a health worker. Both partners need treatment at the same time to avoid passing it back and forth.
  B STI-0064 [stis] How trichomoniasis spreads: Trichomoniasis spreads mainly during vaginal sex without a condom, and through genital-to-genital contact, with someone who has the infection. It can pass from a man to a woman, a woman to a man, or between women. Because many people have no signs, they can pass it without knowing. It does not spread by hugging or sharing food. Using condoms every time lowers the risk.

0.77
  A CSA-0019 [contraception] Lubricants that are safe with latex condoms: Lubricant (lube) makes sex more comfortable and helps stop condoms from tearing. With latex condoms, only use water-based or silicone-based lubricants, or clean water. Some condoms already come with lubricant on them. Many pharmacies sell water-based lube. Check the label, and if you are not sure, ask the pharmacist which lubricant is safe with condoms.
  B CSA-0022 [contraception] Lubricated and non-lubricated condoms: Many male condoms come already lubricated, which makes them feel smoother and helps prevent tearing. Some are non-lubricated, which some people choose for oral sex. Both protect well when used correctly. If sex feels dry or rough with any condom, add a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Never add oil-based products to latex condoms.

0.77
  A CSA-0030 [contraception] How effective male condoms are: When male condoms are used correctly every time, about 2 out of 100 women become pregnant in a year. In real life, when people sometimes forget or make mistakes, about 13 out of 100 become pregnant. So condoms work very well, but only if you use one correctly from start to finish every time. They are also the only method that protects against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs together.
  B HIV-0041 [hiv] How well condoms protect against HIV: When used correctly every time, from start to finish, male and female condoms are very effective at preventing HIV and many other STIs, and they also prevent pregnancy. Most failures happen because condoms are not used every time, are put on late, are torn by nails or teeth, or are used with oil-based lubricants. Check the expiry date, open the packet carefully, and use a new condom for each act of sex. Free condoms are available at many health centers.

0.77
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B PRA-0083 [pregnancy] Where to get a pregnancy test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get a pregnancy test at a health center, and many health posts and youth corners can also help. Pharmacies sell urine pregnancy tests. A community health worker can tell you where to go near you. At a health center, a nurse can test you, explain the result and talk with you about next steps. You can also call 114 for free information.

0.76
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.

0.76
  A STI-0074 [stis] Is hepatitis B curable: When adults get hepatitis B, most clear the virus on their own within about six months. If it stays longer, it is called chronic hepatitis B. There is no complete cure for chronic hepatitis B yet, but medicine can control the virus, protect the liver and help people live long, healthy lives. Not everyone needs medicine; a health worker decides based on tests. Regular check-ups are important.
  B STI-0075 [stis] Long-term hepatitis B: Chronic hepatitis B means the virus stays in the body for more than six months. This is much more likely when a person is infected as a baby or young child than as an adult. Over many years, chronic hepatitis B can scar the liver (cirrhosis) or cause liver cancer, often without signs until late. Regular check-ups and treatment when needed greatly lower these risks.

0.76
  A CSB-0084 [contraception] Is sterilisation reversible: Female sterilisation and vasectomy should be thought of as permanent. Reversal surgery is difficult, expensive, often not available, and frequently does not work. That is why a person should only choose sterilisation when they are sure they never want children in the future. If you might want children later, long-acting reversible methods like the implant or IUD are better choices.
  B CSB-0093 [contraception] Can sterilisation fail: Sterilisation and vasectomy are very effective, but no method is 100 percent. Fewer than 1 in 100 people get pregnant after female sterilisation. A vasectomy only works after about 3 months. If a sterilised woman has signs of pregnancy, she should see a health worker quickly, because a pregnancy after sterilisation has a higher chance of being outside the womb.

0.76
  A WEL-0113 [wellbeing] Blood after first sex: Some girls and women notice a little bleeding the first time they have vaginal sex, and many do not. A small amount of light bleeding that stops within a day or so is usually not serious. Heavy bleeding, bleeding that does not stop, strong pain, fever or bad-smelling discharge need care: go to a health center. If the sex was not your choice, you deserve care and support; please go to a health facility quickly.
  B WEL-0114 [wellbeing] No bleeding after first sex: Many girls and women do not bleed the first time they have sex. The hymen is a thin, stretchy tissue that differs from person to person, and it can stretch through sport, daily activity or simply be naturally open. No bleeding does not mean someone has had sex before. Bleeding is not a test of virginity, and nobody can tell your sexual history by looking at your body.

0.76
  A HIV-0022 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between women: The risk of HIV passing between two women during sex is very low, but it is possible if blood, such as menstrual blood or blood from cuts, or vaginal fluids pass between partners, or if sex toys are shared without cleaning or a new condom. Women who have sex with women can also have male partners or other risks. Using barriers, not sharing toys, and regular testing help keep you healthy.
  B HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.

0.76
  A CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.
  B HIV-0041 [hiv] How well condoms protect against HIV: When used correctly every time, from start to finish, male and female condoms are very effective at preventing HIV and many other STIs, and they also prevent pregnancy. Most failures happen because condoms are not used every time, are put on late, are torn by nails or teeth, or are used with oil-based lubricants. Check the expiry date, open the packet carefully, and use a new condom for each act of sex. Free condoms are available at many health centers.

0.76
  A MEN-0035 [menstruation] Mood swings and crying before a period: Feeling happy one moment and sad or tearful the next is common in the days before a period. Hormone changes affect the brain chemicals that control mood. It does not mean something is wrong with you. Getting enough sleep, moving your body, eating regularly and talking with someone you trust can help. Knowing when your period is coming can also help you understand these feelings. If sadness is very strong or lasts beyond your period, talk to a health worker or call 114.
  B MEN-0037 [menstruation] Very low mood before periods (PMDD): For a small number of people, mood changes before a period are so strong that they make school, home or friendships very hard, with deep sadness, hopelessness, anxiety or anger that lifts once the period starts. This can be a condition sometimes called PMDD, and it can be treated. Please talk to a health worker at your health center or youth corner. If you ever have thoughts of hurting yourself, tell a trusted adult now, call 114, or go to the nearest health facility.

0.76
  A STI-0069 [stis] What is hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is an infection of the liver caused by a virus. It can cause a short illness, or it can stay in the body for life (chronic hepatitis B), slowly damaging the liver and sometimes leading to liver cancer. Many people have no signs for years. Hepatitis B can be prevented with a safe and effective vaccine, and chronic infection can be controlled with treatment from a health worker.
  B STI-0075 [stis] Long-term hepatitis B: Chronic hepatitis B means the virus stays in the body for more than six months. This is much more likely when a person is infected as a baby or young child than as an adult. Over many years, chronic hepatitis B can scar the liver (cirrhosis) or cause liver cancer, often without signs until late. Regular check-ups and treatment when needed greatly lower these risks.

0.76
  A HIV-0016 [hiv] HIV risk from vaginal sex: Vaginal sex without a condom is one of the most common ways HIV is passed, both from man to woman and from woman to man. The virus in semen, pre-cum or vaginal fluids can enter through the thin lining of the vagina or the penis. Risk is higher if there are sores, other STIs, bleeding, or if the partner with HIV is not on treatment. Using a condom correctly every time greatly lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.

0.76
  A SRV-0031 [services_rights] Why not to buy antibiotics without a prescription: Antibiotics only work for certain infections, and the right one depends on the cause. If you buy antibiotics without seeing a health worker, you may take the wrong medicine, not take enough, or hide a problem that needs real treatment. This can make you sicker and helps germs become resistant, so antibiotics stop working for everyone. If you think you have an infection, including an STI, go to a health centre to be checked and treated properly.
  B STI-0118 [stis] Buying antibiotics without a health worker: It is not safe to buy antibiotics for an STI on your own, from a shop, market, friend or online. You may get the wrong medicine, the wrong amount or fake drugs, which may not cure the infection and can make germs resistant. Many STIs also need testing for other infections, like HIV and syphilis. Go to a health center or youth corner, where you can be properly checked and treated.

0.76
  A STI-0063 [stis] What is trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis, often called trich, is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by a tiny parasite. It mostly infects the vagina in women and the urethra (the tube inside the penis) in men. Many people have no signs, especially men. It can be easily cured with the right medicine from a health worker. Both partners need treatment at the same time to avoid passing it back and forth.
  B STI-0067 [stis] Testing and cure for trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis can be found by testing a swab from the vagina or a urine sample, depending on what the health facility has. Sometimes the nurse treats based on signs. Trich is curable with medicine from a health worker. Take all the medicine, avoid alcohol if the nurse tells you to, avoid sex until you and your partner have both finished treatment, and go back if signs return.

0.76
  A CON-0086 [consent_safety] Main types of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence has four main types. Physical violence: hitting, slapping, kicking, burning or using weapons. Sexual violence: rape, unwanted touching, forced sex, sexual harassment and exploitation. Emotional or psychological violence: insults, threats, humiliation, control and isolation. Economic violence: taking someone's money, stopping them from working or studying, or refusing basic needs. A person may experience more than one type. All are harmful. Help: 3512 or Isange (3029).
  B CON-0092 [consent_safety] Why gender-based violence happens: Gender-based violence happens mainly because of unequal power between men and women and harmful beliefs, such as men must control women, women must obey, or violence is a private family matter. Alcohol, poverty and stress can make it worse, but they are not excuses. The person who chooses violence is responsible, not the victim. GBV can be prevented through equality, respect, education and speaking out. Report violence to 3512 or 112.

0.76
  A CSA-0072 [contraception] Vomiting or diarrhoea on the pill: If you vomit soon after taking your pill, or have very bad diarrhoea, the pill may not be absorbed properly. This can be like missing a pill. Follow the advice in your pack leaflet, keep taking your pills, and use condoms until you are well and have taken pills normally for 7 days. If the sickness lasts more than a day or two, see a health worker.
  B CSA-0075 [contraception] Nausea from the pill: Some people feel slightly sick in the first weeks of taking the pill. Taking it with food or at bedtime often helps. Nausea usually gets better after a few months. If you vomit soon after taking a pill, check your leaflet as it may count as a missed pill. If nausea continues or is strong, see a health worker; another pill type may suit you better.

0.76
  A CON-0085 [consent_safety] What is gender-based violence: Gender-based violence (GBV) is harm done to someone because of their gender, or because of unequal power between women and men. It includes physical, sexual, emotional and economic violence. It can happen at home, in relationships, at school, at work or online. Women and girls are affected most, but men and boys can also be victims. GBV is never acceptable and never the victim's fault. In Rwanda, help is available on 3512 (GBV line), 3029 (Isange), 116 and 112.
  B CON-0092 [consent_safety] Why gender-based violence happens: Gender-based violence happens mainly because of unequal power between men and women and harmful beliefs, such as men must control women, women must obey, or violence is a private family matter. Alcohol, poverty and stress can make it worse, but they are not excuses. The person who chooses violence is responsible, not the victim. GBV can be prevented through equality, respect, education and speaking out. Report violence to 3512 or 112.

0.76
  A C-004 [contraception] Male condoms: Condoms are worn on the penis and block sperm and infections. They are the only method that protects against both pregnancy and STIs, including HIV. Used correctly every time, about 2 in 100 women get pregnant in a year; as commonly used, about 13 in 100. Use a new condom each time and talk about it with your partner before sex. Using two condoms at once does not protect more and can make them break.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.76
  A PUB-0007 [puberty] What causes puberty to start: Puberty starts when a part of your brain sends chemical messages, called hormones, to your body. In girls these messages tell the ovaries to make more estrogen. In boys they tell the testicles to make more testosterone. These hormones cause the changes you notice, like body hair, growth, breasts or a deeper voice. Nothing you do or eat switches it on. It happens naturally when your body is ready.
  B PUB-0009 [puberty] First sign of puberty in girls: For most girls the first sign of puberty is breast budding: a small, firm bump under one or both nipples, which can feel a bit sore. Soon after, hair may start to grow around the genitals and under the arms. A growth spurt follows, and the first period usually comes about two years after breasts start growing. Some girls notice pubic hair first, which is also normal.

0.76
  A MEN-0053 [menstruation] Reusable versus disposable pads: Disposable pads are used once and thrown away. They are easy to use, good when you are away from water, but they cost money every month and create waste. Reusable pads cost more at first but can last a long time, saving money and making less rubbish. They need water, soap, a private place to wash, and sun to dry. Both are safe when used properly. Some girls use reusables at home and disposables at school.
  B MEN-0078 [menstruation] Reusing a disposable pad: Disposable pads are made to be used only once. Washing and reusing them is not safe because they do not get clean inside, they break apart, and they can grow germs that cause infections. If money is tight, reusable cloth pads or a menstrual cup are safer ways to save. You can also ask a teacher, school matron or health worker if they know of support for pads in your area.

0.76
  A MEN-0125 [menstruation] Being underweight and periods: Your body needs enough energy from food to have regular periods. If you lose a lot of weight, are very thin, do not eat enough, or skip meals often, your periods may become irregular or stop. This is the body protecting itself. Eating regular, balanced meals usually helps periods return. If you are trying to lose weight, feel afraid of eating, or your periods have stopped, please talk to a health worker.
  B MEN-0126 [menstruation] Weight gain and periods: Gaining a lot of weight can change hormone levels and sometimes make periods irregular, less frequent or heavier. Some conditions, like PCOS, can cause both weight gain and irregular periods. Eating balanced meals and being active are good for your whole body, including your cycle. If your periods have become irregular and you have gained weight, see a health worker for advice and a check, rather than trying extreme diets.

0.76
  A PUB-0125 [puberty] Sudden pain in a testicle: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle or the scrotum, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, is an emergency. It can mean the testicle has twisted and lost its blood supply. Go to a health facility now, or call 912 or 112. Treatment within a few hours can save the testicle. Do not wait to see if it goes away, and do not feel shy; health workers see this often.
  B PUB-0126 [puberty] Lump or swelling in a testicle: A new lump, hardness, or swelling in a testicle or the scrotum should be checked by a health worker soon, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as fluid collections or cysts, but some need treatment, and a few can be serious. Finding problems early makes treatment easier. If the swelling comes with sudden strong pain, go to a health facility immediately.

0.75
  A HIV-0101 [hiv] HIV testing while on PrEP: You need an HIV test before starting PrEP, because PrEP is only for people who are HIV negative. While on PrEP, you will have regular HIV tests, often every few months, when you collect your refills. This makes sure you are still negative and that PrEP is the right medicine for you. Keep all your clinic appointments.
  B HIV-0107 [hiv] PrEP when your partner has HIV: If your partner has HIV, PrEP can help protect you, especially while your partner is starting treatment or if their viral load is not yet undetectable. When your partner takes treatment well and is undetectable, HIV is not passed through sex. Talk with a health worker together about the best plan, which may include PrEP, condoms, and regular testing.

0.75
  A CSA-0174 [contraception] Washing or peeing after sex myth: Peeing, washing the vagina, jumping, or pushing after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm swim into the womb very quickly, within minutes, and urine comes out of a different opening. Washing inside the vagina can also cause infections. If you had unprotected sex, emergency contraception within 5 days is the right step.
  B PRA-0050 [pregnancy] Peeing after sex does not prevent pregnancy: Peeing after sex does not stop pregnancy. Urine comes out of a different opening, the urethra, not the vagina. So peeing cannot wash sperm out of the vagina or womb. Peeing after sex can help lower the chance of a bladder infection, but it is not a way to avoid pregnancy.

0.75
  A CSA-0122 [contraception] How long after sex EC pills work: Emergency contraceptive pills can be taken up to 5 days (120 hours) after unprotected sex. The sooner you take them, the better they work, so do not wait until the next morning if you can get them today. Even on day 4 or 5 they can still help. A copper IUD can also be fitted within 5 days.
  B CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.

0.75
  A CON-0107 [consent_safety] Helping a friend in an abusive relationship: If a friend is in an abusive relationship, listen without judging, tell them it is not their fault, and let them know you care. Avoid ordering them to leave or insulting their partner, because this can push them away. Help them think about safety, offer to go with them to get help, and share numbers like 3512 (GBV line), 3029 (Isange) and 116 if under 18. Respect their choices, stay in touch, and call 112 if they are in immediate danger.
  B CON-0148 [consent_safety] Supporting a friend who was abused: If a friend tells you they were abused, stay calm and listen. Say 'I believe you', 'It's not your fault' and 'Thank you for telling me'. Let them share at their own pace and do not push for details. Ask what they need, and help them reach a trusted adult or services like Isange (3029), 3512 or 116. If they are in danger or very hurt, call 112. Keep checking in, because support over time matters.

0.75
  A PR-001 [pregnancy] Signs of pregnancy: A missed period is a common first sign of pregnancy. Some people also feel sick, have tender breasts or feel very tired. A late period can have other causes, such as stress, so only a pregnancy test can tell you for sure.
  B PR-002 [pregnancy] How to know if you are pregnant: A pregnancy test can tell you. You can get one at a health center. If you took emergency pills and your period is more than 7 days late, take a pregnancy test. If it is positive, go to a health center for antenatal care as early as possible.

0.75
  A PUB-0036 [puberty] Breast lump in a teenage girl: Breasts can feel lumpy during puberty because they are made of glands and tissue that are still growing. Most lumps in teenage girls are harmless, and breast cancer is very rare at this age. Still, if you find a new lump that does not go away after your next period, grows, is hard, or comes with skin or nipple changes, see a health worker so they can examine it.
  B WEL-0130 [wellbeing] Breast cancer in young women: Breast cancer is rare in teenagers and uncommon in young women, though it can happen. Most breast lumps and pains at a young age are not cancer. Still, it is good to know how your breasts normally look and feel and to report changes, such as a new lump, skin dimpling, a nipple turning inward, bloody discharge or a sore that does not heal. Early checks save lives, so see a health worker if worried.

0.75
  A MEN-0035 [menstruation] Mood swings and crying before a period: Feeling happy one moment and sad or tearful the next is common in the days before a period. Hormone changes affect the brain chemicals that control mood. It does not mean something is wrong with you. Getting enough sleep, moving your body, eating regularly and talking with someone you trust can help. Knowing when your period is coming can also help you understand these feelings. If sadness is very strong or lasts beyond your period, talk to a health worker or call 114.
  B MEN-0036 [menstruation] Feeling angry or irritable before a period: Some girls feel short-tempered, impatient or easily annoyed before their period. This is a common part of PMS and is linked to hormone changes. You are still in charge of how you act, and these tips can help: take a short walk, breathe slowly, get enough sleep, and step away before an argument grows. It can help to explain to people close to you how you feel. If anger feels out of control, talk to a health worker or counsellor.

0.75
  A STI-0087 [stis] How scabies spreads: Scabies spreads through prolonged skin-to-skin contact with a person who has it, such as sharing a bed, caring for family members, or sexual contact. Brief contact like a handshake rarely spreads it. Less often, it spreads through shared bedding, clothes or towels. Because it spreads easily in homes and dormitories, everyone who has close contact should be treated at the same time.
  B STI-0089 [stis] Treating scabies: Scabies is treated with a cream, lotion or sometimes tablets recommended by a health worker. Everyone living closely with you or sexual partners should be treated at the same time, even if they do not itch yet. Wash clothes, bedding and towels in hot water and dry them in the sun. Itching may last a few weeks after treatment; if it continues longer, go back to the health center.

0.75
  A MEN-0160 [menstruation] Is period blood dirty: Period blood is not dirty, impure or dangerous. It is made of blood and womb lining and is as clean as blood from any other part of the body. It may smell when it meets air and bacteria outside the body, which is why we change pads and wash. A girl on her period is not unclean, and she can touch people, objects and food normally. Periods are a healthy part of life.
  B MEN-0188 [menstruation] Period as cleaning out bad blood myth: Some people believe periods remove dirty or bad blood from the body and that a girl who bleeds little is keeping poison inside. This is not true. Period blood is the womb lining that is no longer needed, not waste or toxins. Your kidneys and liver clean the body. A light period does not mean dirt is staying inside. If you have concerns about your flow, ask a health worker.

0.75
  A HIV-0093 [hiv] Who PrEP is for: PrEP is medicine taken by HIV-negative people to prevent HIV. It is for people at higher risk, for example someone whose partner has HIV and is not virally suppressed, someone with several partners or who often has sex without condoms, someone who recently had an STI, or someone who sells sex or injects drugs. A health worker will talk with you to see if PrEP is right for you.
  B H-006 [hiv] PrEP: PrEP is a daily pill that HIV-negative people at higher risk can take to prevent HIV. It is voluntary. You must test negative first and be tested again every 3 months. It reaches full protection after 7 daily doses. You should still use condoms for other protection. Ask a health worker if PrEP is right for you.

0.75
  A STI-0090 [stis] What is a vaginal yeast infection: A vaginal yeast infection, also called thrush or candida, happens when a yeast that normally lives in the body grows too much in the vagina. It is very common, and most women get it at least once. It is not usually passed through sex and is not a sign of being dirty. It causes itching and discharge but is not dangerous, and it can be treated.
  B STI-0092 [stis] What causes yeast infections: Yeast infections happen when the normal balance in the vagina changes and yeast grows too much. Things that can trigger this include taking antibiotics, pregnancy, uncontrolled diabetes, a weak immune system, hormonal changes, staying in wet or tight clothes, and washing inside the vagina or using scented soaps. Sometimes there is no clear reason. Yeast infections are not caused by poor morals or being unclean.

0.75
  A CSA-0175 [contraception] What fertility awareness methods are: Fertility awareness methods help a woman know which days of her cycle she could get pregnant. On those fertile days, the couple avoids sex or uses condoms. Examples are the Standard Days Method with CycleBeads, the calendar method, the TwoDay Method using vaginal secretions, and the body temperature method. They need regular cycles or careful daily attention, and partner cooperation.
  B CSA-0187 [contraception] Learning fertility awareness: To use fertility awareness methods well, it is best to learn from a trained health worker. They can check if a method suits your cycle, teach you how to track it, and show you how to use CycleBeads or record signs. Ask at your nearest health center or youth corner. Use condoms while you are learning.

0.75
  A MEN-0111 [menstruation] What counts as a heavy period: Your period may be heavy if you need to change a pad every one to two hours for several hours in a row, need to use two pads at once, bleed through at night often, pass clots bigger than a large coin, or bleed for more than seven days. Heavy periods can make you tired, weak or short of breath. If this sounds like you, see a health worker. If you soak a pad every hour and feel dizzy or faint, go to a health facility now.
  B MEN-0112 [menstruation] Causes of heavy periods: In the first years after periods start, heavy bleeding is often due to hormones that are not yet balanced, because ovulation does not happen every month. Other possible causes include bleeding disorders, thyroid problems, some contraceptive methods, growths in the womb such as fibroids or polyps, and infections. Only a health worker can find the cause. If your periods are heavy, visit your health center so you can be checked and helped.

0.75
  A STI-0075 [stis] Long-term hepatitis B: Chronic hepatitis B means the virus stays in the body for more than six months. This is much more likely when a person is infected as a baby or young child than as an adult. Over many years, chronic hepatitis B can scar the liver (cirrhosis) or cause liver cancer, often without signs until late. Regular check-ups and treatment when needed greatly lower these risks.
  B STI-0077 [stis] Living with hepatitis B: People with hepatitis B can live full lives. Go to regular check-ups, take any medicine exactly as advised, and avoid alcohol, which harms the liver. Do not take herbal or other remedies without asking a health worker, as some damage the liver. Protect others by using condoms, not sharing razors or toothbrushes, and covering cuts. Encourage partners and family to get tested and vaccinated. You can still eat, study and play with others.

0.75
  A STI-0097 [stis] What is bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis (BV) is a very common vaginal condition. It happens when the normal balance of bacteria in the vagina changes and some bacteria grow too much. It is not caused by poor hygiene. Many women with BV have no signs, while others notice a fishy smell. BV is usually not dangerous, but it can raise the risk of some infections, so it is worth getting checked.
  B STI-0098 [stis] Signs of bacterial vaginosis: Signs of bacterial vaginosis can include a thin white or grey vaginal discharge and a fishy smell, which may be stronger after sex or during your period. It does not usually cause much itching or soreness. Many women have no signs at all. Trichomoniasis and other infections can cause similar signs, so a health worker needs to check to know what is causing them.

0.75
  A CSA-0032 [contraception] STIs that condoms protect against less well: Condoms protect very well against infections spread through fluids, like HIV, gonorrhoea and chlamydia. They give less protection against infections spread by skin-to-skin contact, such as herpes, genital warts (HPV) and syphilis sores, if the sore or wart is in an area the condom does not cover. Condoms still lower the risk a lot. If you see sores or warts, avoid sex and visit a health center.
  B CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.

0.75
  A STI-0099 [stis] What causes bacterial vaginosis: The exact cause of bacterial vaginosis is not fully known. It happens when the healthy bacteria in the vagina decrease and other bacteria increase. Things that make it more likely include washing or douching inside the vagina, using scented soaps, having a new sex partner or several partners, and not using condoms. Girls who have never had sex can also get it.
  B STI-0101 [stis] Treating bacterial vaginosis: Bacterial vaginosis can be treated with antibiotics given by a health worker, as tablets or a gel. Finish all the medicine, even if the smell goes away quickly, and avoid alcohol if the health worker tells you to. BV sometimes comes back; if it does, go back to the health center. Avoid douching, scented soaps, or putting anything inside the vagina to remove the smell.

0.75
  A PUB-0130 [puberty] Tight underwear and the testicles: The testicles work best when slightly cooler than the body. Very tight or hot underwear worn all the time may make them too warm and uncomfortable, and can cause sweating and rashes. Loose, clean cotton underwear is a good everyday choice. Supportive underwear is useful during sport. Underwear choice is not a cause of serious problems, so do not worry if you sometimes wear tight pants.
  B WEL-0122 [wellbeing] Tight and synthetic underwear: Underwear that is very tight or made of synthetic material like nylon traps heat and sweat. This can cause rubbing, rashes, and make yeast and other infections more likely in girls. In boys, tight underwear and trousers keep the testicles warm, which may slightly lower sperm quality. Cotton underwear that fits comfortably lets the skin breathe. Change it daily and when wet with sweat.

0.75
  A CSA-0111 [contraception] What progestin-only pills are: Progestin-only pills, sometimes called mini-pills, contain only one hormone, a progestin, and no estrogen. They work mainly by thickening the mucus at the cervix so sperm cannot pass, and in some users by stopping eggs being released. They are taken every day with no break. They are often chosen by breastfeeding women and people who cannot use estrogen.
  B CSA-0113 [contraception] Who can use progestin-only pills: Almost all women and girls can use progestin-only pills, including those who are breastfeeding, those who smoke, those with high blood pressure that is controlled, and those who cannot take estrogen. A health worker will ask a few questions to check they are right for you, for example about current breast cancer or certain liver diseases.

0.75
  A CSA-0133 [contraception] Period after taking EC: After emergency pills, your next period may come on time, a few days early or a few days late. It may also be a bit lighter or heavier than usual. This is normal. If your period is more than a week late, take a pregnancy test at a pharmacy or health center.
  B CSA-0134 [contraception] Late period after EC: EC can make your next period a few days late. But if it is more than 7 days late, or you have signs of pregnancy, take a pregnancy test. You can get one at a pharmacy or health center. If the test is positive, or you are worried, talk to a nurse or health worker, or call 114 for free, confidential advice.

0.75
  A STI-0086 [stis] What is scabies: Scabies is a skin condition caused by tiny mites that burrow under the skin, causing very itchy spots. It is not only an STI: it spreads through any long, close skin contact, such as sleeping in the same bed, within families, schools and boarding schools, and also during sex. Anyone can get it, and it has nothing to do with being dirty. Scabies can be treated.
  B STI-0089 [stis] Treating scabies: Scabies is treated with a cream, lotion or sometimes tablets recommended by a health worker. Everyone living closely with you or sexual partners should be treated at the same time, even if they do not itch yet. Wash clothes, bedding and towels in hot water and dry them in the sun. Itching may last a few weeks after treatment; if it continues longer, go back to the health center.

0.75
  A SRV-0089 [services_rights] What the 112 line is for: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police help. Call it if you or someone else is in danger right now, for example if someone is being attacked, threatened or hurt, or if there is a serious emergency. Tell them clearly where you are and what is happening. For medical emergencies you can also call 912 for an ambulance.
  B SRV-0090 [services_rights] What the 912 line is for: 912 is the number to call for an ambulance in Rwanda. Use it for medical emergencies, such as very heavy bleeding, someone fainting or not waking, severe pain, trouble breathing, a serious injury, or a pregnant woman with danger signs. Stay calm, say where you are and what is happening, and follow their advice until help arrives.

0.74
  A CSB-0055 [contraception] How the copper IUD prevents pregnancy: The copper IUD is a small, flexible T-shaped device placed inside the womb by a trained provider. The copper changes the way sperm move so they cannot reach and fertilise an egg. It has no hormones, so it does not change your natural cycle, though periods may become heavier. It works for many years and can be removed whenever you want.
  B CSB-0058 [contraception] Copper IUD vs hormonal IUD: Both are placed in the womb and are very effective. The copper IUD has no hormones and lasts up to 12 years, but may make periods heavier or more painful, especially at first. The hormonal IUD releases a small amount of progestin, lasts several years, and usually makes periods lighter, sometimes stopping them. Neither protects against STIs. A health worker can help you compare them for your needs.

0.74
  A HIV-0046 [hiv] Having many sexual partners and HIV risk: Having more than one sexual partner, especially at the same time, increases the chance of HIV, because each partner may have other partners too, creating a network where HIV can spread quickly. You often cannot know your partners' status. Fewer partners, using condoms every time, testing together, and PrEP if you are at high risk all lower your risk. This is about staying safe, not about judging anyone.
  B HIV-0053 [hiv] Is being faithful enough to prevent HIV: Being faithful to one partner lowers HIV risk only if both of you tested negative and both of you stay faithful. If one partner had earlier partners and has not tested, or has sex with others, the risk remains. You cannot be sure of another person's choices. The safest plan is to test together, keep testing, and use condoms until you both know your status.

0.74
  A HIV-0016 [hiv] HIV risk from vaginal sex: Vaginal sex without a condom is one of the most common ways HIV is passed, both from man to woman and from woman to man. The virus in semen, pre-cum or vaginal fluids can enter through the thin lining of the vagina or the penis. Risk is higher if there are sores, other STIs, bleeding, or if the partner with HIV is not on treatment. Using a condom correctly every time greatly lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0017 [hiv] HIV risk from anal sex: Anal sex without a condom carries the highest risk of passing HIV during sex. The lining of the anus is thin and can tear easily, letting the virus get into the blood. The receptive partner, the one being penetrated, has the highest risk, but the other partner can also get HIV. This is true for any couple, whether man and woman or two men. Using a condom with plenty of water-based lubricant every time lowers the risk.

0.74
  A SRV-0070 [services_rights] Age to test for HIV without a parent: According to Rwanda's HIV Guidelines, young people aged 12 and above can agree to an HIV test themselves, without needing a parent's permission. You will get counselling before and after the test, and your result is kept confidential. HIV testing is offered at health centres and some youth centres. Knowing your status is a smart, brave step for your health.
  B SRV-0071 [services_rights] HIV testing for children under 12: For children under 12, a parent or guardian usually needs to give consent for an HIV test. If you are younger than 12 and worried about HIV, talk to a trusted adult, such as a parent, relative, teacher or community health worker, who can go with you to the health centre. You can also call 114 or 116 for advice. Health workers will support you kindly.

0.74
  A CSB-0086 [contraception] What a vasectomy is: A vasectomy is a simple, safe operation for men. A trained provider closes the small tubes that carry sperm from the testicles, so the semen no longer contains sperm. It is done with local numbing and takes a short time. It is meant to be permanent and is for men who are sure they do not want any more children.
  B CSB-0087 [contraception] Rumour: vasectomy weakens men: A vasectomy does not remove the testicles and is not castration. It does not reduce strength, manhood, erections or desire for sex. A man still ejaculates semen; it just has no sperm. Hormones keep being made normally. Many men and their partners find sex is the same or better because they no longer worry about pregnancy.

0.74
  A CSB-0041 [contraception] Irregular spotting on the injection: In the first months of using the injection, many people have spotting or bleeding at unexpected times. This is common and not harmful. Over time, bleeding often becomes less, and many users stop bleeding completely. It does not mean the injection is not working. If the spotting bothers you, talk to a health worker; they may be able to help, and you can decide together whether to continue.
  B CSB-0043 [contraception] Heavy bleeding on the injection: Heavy or long bleeding is uncommon with the injection, but it can happen, usually in the first months. If you bleed much more than your normal period, for more than 8 days, or feel weak or dizzy, go to your health center. A health worker can check for other causes and may offer treatment. If you soak pads very quickly, faint or feel very unwell, go to a health facility now or call 112.

0.74
  A CSA-0189 [contraception] What LAM is: LAM stands for Lactational Amenorrhea Method. It is a temporary family planning method based on breastfeeding. When a mother breastfeeds her baby fully or nearly fully, day and night, her body may not release eggs. LAM can protect against pregnancy for up to 6 months after birth, but only if all its conditions are met.
  B CSA-0190 [contraception] Conditions for LAM to work: LAM only works when all three conditions are true: your monthly bleeding has not returned since birth; you are fully or nearly fully breastfeeding, often, day and night, without giving other foods or drinks regularly; and your baby is less than 6 months old. If any one of these changes, you need another method straight away.

0.74
  A SRV-0001 [services_rights] What youth-friendly health services are: Youth-friendly health services are health services set up so young people feel welcome, safe and respected. Staff are trained to listen without judging, keep what you say private and explain things clearly. You can get information, counselling, condoms and other contraception, HIV and STI testing, pregnancy tests and support. Many health centres in Rwanda have a youth-friendly space. You do not need a special reason to visit. Asking about your body and health is normal and smart.
  B SRV-0005 [services_rights] Finding a youth-friendly service near you: To find a youth-friendly service near you, ask at your nearest health centre whether they have a youth corner or a nurse who works with young people. You can also ask your community health worker, a school nurse or teacher, a youth centre, or call 114 for free health information. If the nearest place has no special youth space, you can still get SRH services there, because health centres serve young people too.

0.74
  A CSA-0159 [contraception] What spermicides are: Spermicides are products put deep into the vagina before sex to kill or stop sperm. They come as foam, gel, cream, tablets or film, and the most common one contains nonoxynol-9. Used alone, they are one of the least effective methods. They do not protect against HIV or other STIs.
  B CSA-0161 [contraception] How effective spermicide is: Spermicide used alone is one of the least effective methods. With typical use, about 21 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year, and about 16 out of 100 even with correct use every time. It works better combined with a diaphragm, but other methods like condoms, pills, injections, implants or IUDs protect much better.

0.74
  A CSA-0032 [contraception] STIs that condoms protect against less well: Condoms protect very well against infections spread through fluids, like HIV, gonorrhoea and chlamydia. They give less protection against infections spread by skin-to-skin contact, such as herpes, genital warts (HPV) and syphilis sores, if the sore or wart is in an area the condom does not cover. Condoms still lower the risk a lot. If you see sores or warts, avoid sex and visit a health center.
  B STI-0047 [stis] Condoms and HPV: Condoms lower the chance of getting HPV and genital warts, but they do not give full protection, because HPV can live on skin that a condom does not cover, like the scrotum, vulva or thighs. Using condoms every time is still very worth it, because they also protect against many other STIs, HIV and pregnancy. Combining condoms with the HPV vaccine and fewer partners gives the best protection.

0.74
  A PUB-0109 [puberty] One testicle hangs lower: It is very common and normal for one testicle to hang lower than the other, often the left one. This helps them not press against each other when you walk or sit. It is not a sign of a problem. However, if a testicle suddenly moves higher, becomes painful, swollen or hard, go to a health facility straight away.
  B PUB-0110 [puberty] Testicles of different sizes: Having one testicle slightly bigger than the other is common and usually normal, especially during puberty when they are growing. A testicle that is clearly much smaller, suddenly gets bigger, feels hard or lumpy, or is painful should be checked by a health worker. Checking your testicles from time to time helps you know what is normal for you.

0.74
  A PUB-0142 [puberty] Never having a wet dream: Not every boy has wet dreams. Some have many, some have only a few, and some never have them at all. All of these are normal and do not mean anything is wrong with your body or your fertility. Wet dreams may also happen less often if you ejaculate in other ways. If you have no other signs of puberty by around 14, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0145 [puberty] Do girls have wet dreams: Yes, girls can also have sexual dreams that cause excitement, wetness in the vagina, or even an orgasm while sleeping. This is normal and harmless, just like wet dreams in boys. Dreams about sex do not mean you have done something wrong or that you want to act on them. They are a normal part of growing up.

0.74
  A HIV-0061 [hiv] What a reactive self-test result means: A reactive result on an HIV self-test means you might have HIV, but it is not a final diagnosis. You must go to a health facility for further testing to confirm the result. Try to go soon, and consider bringing someone you trust. If the result is confirmed, you can start free treatment right away and live a long, healthy life. You can call 114 for support and information.
  B HIV-0082 [hiv] Where to go after a reactive self-test: After a reactive self-test, go to a health center or hospital as soon as you can and tell the nurse you had a reactive self-test. They will do confirmatory tests and, if HIV is confirmed, help you start free treatment, often the same day. You do not need to bring the kit, but you may. If you feel overwhelmed, talk to someone you trust or call 114.

0.74
  A CSA-0111 [contraception] What progestin-only pills are: Progestin-only pills, sometimes called mini-pills, contain only one hormone, a progestin, and no estrogen. They work mainly by thickening the mucus at the cervix so sperm cannot pass, and in some users by stopping eggs being released. They are taken every day with no break. They are often chosen by breastfeeding women and people who cannot use estrogen.
  B CSA-0112 [contraception] Difference between combined and progestin-only pills: Combined pills contain two hormones, estrogen and progestin; progestin-only pills contain only progestin. Progestin-only pills are taken every day with no break, and timing is stricter. They are suitable for breastfeeding women and for some people who cannot use estrogen. Combined pills often make bleeding more regular, while progestin-only pills more often cause irregular bleeding.

0.74
  A CSB-0042 [contraception] No periods on the injection: Many people who use the injection stop having periods, especially after a year of use. This is expected and safe. The blood is not collecting inside your body or making you sick; the lining of the womb just stays thin because of the hormone. Periods usually return some months after stopping the injection. If you have signs of pregnancy or are worried, visit a health center for a check.
  B CSB-0045 [contraception] Waiting for periods to return after the injection: After your last DMPA injection, it can take several months for your periods to come back. At first they may be irregular. This is normal and not harmful; your body is adjusting as the hormone wears off. If you have not had a period by about a year after your last injection, or you think you might be pregnant, visit a health center for a check.

0.74
  A CSA-0039 [contraception] Condoms for anal sex: Anal sex carries a higher risk of HIV and other STIs than vaginal sex, because the skin inside the anus is thin and can tear easily. Always use a condom with plenty of water-based or silicone-based lubricant, because the anus does not make its own wetness. Use a new condom every time. Pregnancy is unlikely from anal sex, but semen can leak near the vagina.
  B HIV-0017 [hiv] HIV risk from anal sex: Anal sex without a condom carries the highest risk of passing HIV during sex. The lining of the anus is thin and can tear easily, letting the virus get into the blood. The receptive partner, the one being penetrated, has the highest risk, but the other partner can also get HIV. This is true for any couple, whether man and woman or two men. Using a condom with plenty of water-based lubricant every time lowers the risk.

0.74
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0009 [puberty] First sign of puberty in girls: For most girls the first sign of puberty is breast budding: a small, firm bump under one or both nipples, which can feel a bit sore. Soon after, hair may start to grow around the genitals and under the arms. A growth spurt follows, and the first period usually comes about two years after breasts start growing. Some girls notice pubic hair first, which is also normal.

0.74
  A CSB-0009 [contraception] Removing an implant before it expires: Yes. You can ask to have your implant removed at any time and for any reason, even if it was put in recently. You do not need to give a long explanation, and a provider should not refuse or delay removal. If you do not want to get pregnant, ask about starting another method on the same day so you stay protected. Your fertility comes back quickly after removal.
  B CSB-0021 [contraception] When the implant is due to expire: Check the removal date on your card. Plan to visit the health center before or on that date. If you want to keep using an implant, a new one can be inserted at the same visit when the old one is removed, so you stay protected. If you want a different method, start it at that visit. After the date passes, the implant may not protect you well, so use condoms until you get help.

0.74
  A MEN-0110 [menstruation] Pain when peeing during a period: Burning or pain when you pee, needing to pee very often, or cloudy or smelly urine can be signs of a urine infection. These can happen at any time, including during your period. Drinking plenty of water and changing pads often help, but an infection needs a check and treatment. Visit a health center. Go urgently if you have fever, back pain on one side, or vomiting.
  B PRB-0077 [pregnancy] Pain when urinating in pregnancy: Burning or pain when you pass urine, needing to go urgently, cloudy or smelly urine, or blood in urine can be signs of a urine infection. These infections are more common in pregnancy and, if untreated, can spread to the kidneys and lead to early labour. Do not ignore them. Go to your health center to be checked and treated with medicine that is safe in pregnancy. Drink plenty of water.

0.74
  A PUB-0116 [puberty] Does penis size matter: Penis size does not affect a man's health, fertility or ability to have children. It does not decide whether someone is a good partner. Good relationships depend much more on respect, communication and care. Messages that say bigger is better come from jokes, media and pornography, not from health facts. Try to accept your body as it is.
  B WEL-0105 [wellbeing] Penis size worries: Many boys and men worry their penis is too small, often because of jokes or pornography, which shows unusual sizes. Penises vary a lot in size when soft, but sizes are much more similar when erect. The penis keeps growing during puberty, often until the late teens. Size does not affect fertility, manhood or the ability to have a satisfying relationship. If you have real concerns, a health worker can check privately.

0.74
  A CSA-0013 [contraception] When to put on a condom during sex: Put the condom on as soon as the penis is hard and before it touches the vagina, anus or mouth of your partner. Pre-ejaculate (pre-cum) that comes out before ejaculation can carry sperm and infections like HIV. Putting the condom on only just before ejaculating is not safe. Keep it on from start to finish for every act of sex.
  B CSA-0052 [contraception] Putting in a female condom before sex: A female condom can be put in some hours before sex, up to about 8 hours before. This means you do not have to stop in the moment, and you can be ready and protected. Make sure it stays in place until sex is finished. Remove it after sex and use a new one the next time.

0.74
  A HIV-0121 [hiv] Using PEP more than once: PEP can be given again if you have another possible HIV exposure, and a health worker will not refuse you because you used it before. But PEP is an emergency medicine, not a regular prevention method. If you keep needing PEP, it means you are at ongoing risk, and PrEP together with condoms may protect you better. Talk to a health worker about your options without fear of being judged.
  B HIV-0125 [hiv] Does PEP always work: PEP greatly lowers the chance of getting HIV when it is started early and taken every day for all 28 days, but it is not 100 percent effective. It works best when started within hours, not days. That is why follow-up HIV tests are needed after finishing. Keep using condoms during and after PEP until your follow-up tests are done.

0.74
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0007 [puberty] What causes puberty to start: Puberty starts when a part of your brain sends chemical messages, called hormones, to your body. In girls these messages tell the ovaries to make more estrogen. In boys they tell the testicles to make more testosterone. These hormones cause the changes you notice, like body hair, growth, breasts or a deeper voice. Nothing you do or eat switches it on. It happens naturally when your body is ready.

0.74
  A STI-0086 [stis] What is scabies: Scabies is a skin condition caused by tiny mites that burrow under the skin, causing very itchy spots. It is not only an STI: it spreads through any long, close skin contact, such as sleeping in the same bed, within families, schools and boarding schools, and also during sex. Anyone can get it, and it has nothing to do with being dirty. Scabies can be treated.
  B STI-0088 [stis] Signs of scabies: The main sign of scabies is strong itching, often worse at night. You may see small red bumps, blisters or thin wavy lines on the skin, often between the fingers, on the wrists, elbows, armpits, waist, buttocks and genitals. On the penis, it can cause itchy lumps. Scratching can cause sores that get infected. Many skin problems look similar, so see a health worker to check.

0.74
  A CON-0051 [consent_safety] Abuse by a family member: Sexual abuse can happen inside families, by a parent, step-parent, uncle, cousin, sibling or grandparent. It is just as serious as abuse by a stranger and is a crime. The family member is responsible, not the child. Families sometimes pressure victims to stay quiet to protect the family name, but the child's safety comes first. Another trusted adult, the child helpline 116, the GBV line 3512 or Isange (3029) can help keep the child safe.
  B CON-0137 [consent_safety] Reporting abuse by a family member: Reporting a family member is very hard. People may fear breaking the family, losing support, or being blamed. But the child's or survivor's safety matters most, and staying silent usually lets abuse continue, sometimes to other children too. The abuser, not the person who reports, is responsible for any trouble. Services like 116, 3512 and Isange (3029) are used to these situations and can help plan for safety and support.

0.74
  A CSA-0160 [contraception] How to use spermicide: Spermicide must be put deep in the vagina, close to the cervix, shortly before sex. Some types work immediately and others need a few minutes to melt, so follow the package instructions on timing. Use more spermicide before each act of sex. Do not wash inside the vagina for several hours after sex.
  B CSA-0161 [contraception] How effective spermicide is: Spermicide used alone is one of the least effective methods. With typical use, about 21 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year, and about 16 out of 100 even with correct use every time. It works better combined with a diaphragm, but other methods like condoms, pills, injections, implants or IUDs protect much better.

0.74
  A HIV-0080 [hiv] Can someone test me for HIV without my consent: HIV testing should be voluntary. Health workers should explain the test and get your agreement before testing you, and young people 12 and older in Rwanda can give their own consent. No one, including a partner, school or employer, should force you to test or test you secretly. If you feel your rights were not respected, you can talk to the health facility management or call 114.
  B SRV-0070 [services_rights] Age to test for HIV without a parent: According to Rwanda's HIV Guidelines, young people aged 12 and above can agree to an HIV test themselves, without needing a parent's permission. You will get counselling before and after the test, and your result is kept confidential. HIV testing is offered at health centres and some youth centres. Knowing your status is a smart, brave step for your health.

0.74
  A STI-0051 [stis] Why the HPV vaccine is given young: The HPV vaccine works best when given before a person is ever exposed to HPV, which means before they start any sexual activity. Young people's bodies also make a very strong immune response to the vaccine. Getting it at age 9 to 14 does not mean anyone expects the girl to have sex; it simply protects her for the future, like other childhood vaccines.
  B STI-0052 [stis] HPV vaccine and boys: Some countries vaccinate boys against HPV too, but in many countries, including Rwanda, programmes focus on girls because cervical cancer causes the most deaths. When most girls are vaccinated, boys are also indirectly protected because less HPV spreads in the community. Boys can protect themselves and partners by using condoms and limiting partners. Ask a health worker if you want to know about vaccine options for boys.

0.74
  A CSA-0170 [contraception] How effective withdrawal is: With withdrawal, the man pulls his penis out of the vagina before ejaculating. With typical use about 20 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year, and about 4 out of 100 when it is done correctly every time. It is one of the less effective methods. It costs nothing and is better than no method, but condoms or other methods protect much better.
  B CSA-0171 [contraception] Why withdrawal often fails: Withdrawal fails because it needs perfect timing and self-control at the most exciting moment. Many men cannot always feel when they are about to ejaculate, or pull out too late. Semen near the vulva can also enter the vagina. Young or less experienced men may find it especially hard. Using condoms or another method is safer.

0.74
  A STI-0029 [stis] What is genital herpes: Genital herpes is a common infection caused by the herpes simplex virus. It can cause painful blisters or sores on or around the genitals or anus, but many people have very mild signs or none at all. The virus stays in the body for life and can cause sores to come back from time to time. There is no cure yet, but medicine from a health worker can make outbreaks shorter and less frequent.
  B STI-0031 [stis] Signs of genital herpes: Signs of genital herpes can include tingling, itching or burning on the skin, followed by small painful blisters that break into sores, then scab and heal. The sores may be on the penis, vulva, vagina, anus, buttocks or thighs. Some people also feel flu-like, with fever or swollen glands, especially the first time. Many people have mild or no signs. Only a health worker can tell what is causing a sore.

0.74
  A MEN-0068 [menstruation] Period underwear: Period underwear looks like normal underwear but has special absorbent layers in the crotch that soak up blood and keep you feeling dry. Some can be worn alone on light days, while others are used as backup with a pad or cup. They can be washed and reused many times. They cost more at first and can be hard to find, and you need enough pairs and time to wash and dry them between uses.
  B MEN-0069 [menstruation] Washing period underwear: After wearing period underwear, rinse it in cold water until the water runs mostly clear. Then wash it with mild soap by hand or in a gentle wash, without bleach or fabric softener, which can damage the absorbent layer. Hang it to dry fully in the sun or open air; do not wear it damp. Follow any instructions on the label. With good care, a pair can last for a long time.

0.74
  A STI-0069 [stis] What is hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is an infection of the liver caused by a virus. It can cause a short illness, or it can stay in the body for life (chronic hepatitis B), slowly damaging the liver and sometimes leading to liver cancer. Many people have no signs for years. Hepatitis B can be prevented with a safe and effective vaccine, and chronic infection can be controlled with treatment from a health worker.
  B STI-0077 [stis] Living with hepatitis B: People with hepatitis B can live full lives. Go to regular check-ups, take any medicine exactly as advised, and avoid alcohol, which harms the liver. Do not take herbal or other remedies without asking a health worker, as some damage the liver. Protect others by using condoms, not sharing razors or toothbrushes, and covering cuts. Encourage partners and family to get tested and vaccinated. You can still eat, study and play with others.

0.73
  A CSA-0189 [contraception] What LAM is: LAM stands for Lactational Amenorrhea Method. It is a temporary family planning method based on breastfeeding. When a mother breastfeeds her baby fully or nearly fully, day and night, her body may not release eggs. LAM can protect against pregnancy for up to 6 months after birth, but only if all its conditions are met.
  B CSA-0192 [contraception] How effective LAM is: When all three LAM conditions are met, it works well: about 2 out of 100 women get pregnant in the first 6 months after birth with typical use, and fewer than 1 out of 100 with correct use. It only works for a short time and does not protect against HIV or STIs, so use condoms if there is any risk.

0.73
  A MEN-0087 [menstruation] Shaving or trimming pubic hair during a period: Pubic hair is normal and you do not need to remove it, during your period or at any time. It does not make periods dirty. If you choose to trim or shave, you can do it during your period, but use your own clean razor or scissors, never share, and be gentle because skin can be more sensitive. Trimming with scissors is less likely to cause cuts and ingrown hairs than shaving.
  B PUB-0051 [puberty] Should I remove pubic hair: You do not need to remove pubic hair. It is normal and helps protect the skin of the genitals from rubbing and dirt. Removing it is not cleaner or healthier. If you choose to remove or trim it, that is your choice, but shaving can cause cuts, itching, bumps and infections. Keeping the area clean with water and mild soap on the outside is what matters most.

0.73
  A STI-0078 [stis] What is hepatitis C: Hepatitis C is a liver infection caused by a virus. Many people have no signs for years, while the virus slowly damages the liver, sometimes leading to scarring or liver cancer. Unlike hepatitis B, there is no vaccine for hepatitis C. The good news is that today's medicines can cure most people in a few months. A blood test at a health center can show whether you have it.
  B STI-0080 [stis] Testing for hepatitis C: Hepatitis C is tested with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick test. If it is positive, a second test checks whether the virus is still active, because some people clear it naturally. Testing is a good idea if you ever shared needles, razors or had unsterile tattoos or piercings, or a family member has it. Rwanda has expanded hepatitis testing; ask at a health center or call 114.

0.73
  A CSA-0028 [contraception] Non-latex condom options: Condoms that are not made of latex are made of materials like polyurethane or nitrile. They are good for people with a latex allergy and can be used with any type of lubricant, including oil-based ones. The female (internal) condom is usually made of nitrile. Non-latex condoms also protect against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs when used correctly. Ask at a pharmacy or health center if they are available.
  B CSA-0059 [contraception] Female condoms and latex allergy: Most female condoms are made of nitrile or polyurethane, not latex. This makes them a good choice if you or your partner are allergic to latex. They protect against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs. If you still get itching, swelling or rash, see a health worker to find the cause.

0.73
  A CSA-0125 [contraception] Taking EC when already pregnant: If you are already pregnant, emergency contraceptive pills will not work, but they will not harm the pregnancy or the baby either. So if you took EC and later find out you were pregnant, there is no need to worry about damage from the pills. If your period is late, take a pregnancy test and talk to a health worker about your options and care.
  B CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.

0.73
  A PUB-0051 [puberty] Should I remove pubic hair: You do not need to remove pubic hair. It is normal and helps protect the skin of the genitals from rubbing and dirt. Removing it is not cleaner or healthier. If you choose to remove or trim it, that is your choice, but shaving can cause cuts, itching, bumps and infections. Keeping the area clean with water and mild soap on the outside is what matters most.
  B PUB-0052 [puberty] Trimming pubic hair safely: If you want to make pubic hair shorter, trimming with clean small scissors or clippers is gentler than shaving. Use your own tools and do not share them. If you shave, wet the skin, use soap, shave slowly in the direction the hair grows and use a new or clean razor. Never use sharp blades you cannot control, and stop if the skin is cut, sore or infected.

0.73
  A CSB-0058 [contraception] Copper IUD vs hormonal IUD: Both are placed in the womb and are very effective. The copper IUD has no hormones and lasts up to 12 years, but may make periods heavier or more painful, especially at first. The hormonal IUD releases a small amount of progestin, lasts several years, and usually makes periods lighter, sometimes stopping them. Neither protects against STIs. A health worker can help you compare them for your needs.
  B CSB-0066 [contraception] Heavier periods with the copper IUD: With the copper IUD, periods often become heavier, longer or more crampy, especially in the first 3 to 6 months. This usually improves with time and is not harmful. A health worker can suggest pain relief that helps. If bleeding is very heavy, lasts more than 8 days, or you feel weak or dizzy, go to your health center. You can also switch methods if you prefer.

0.73
  A CSB-0019 [contraception] Cannot feel the implant under the skin: Usually you can feel the implant gently under the skin of your arm. If you suddenly cannot feel it, or it feels broken or different, go to your health center so a provider can check. Until they confirm it is in place, use condoms or avoid sex to prevent pregnancy. Do not press hard or try to find it with anything sharp. Most of the time it is still there, just deeper.
  B CSB-0028 [contraception] Implant bent or broken under the skin: Implants are flexible and rarely break. If your implant feels bent, broken, or changes shape after a knock to your arm, go to your health center to have it checked. A bent implant may still work, but a provider should look at it. Until they check, use condoms to be safe. Do not try to straighten or remove it yourself.

0.73
  A CSA-0015 [contraception] What to do if a condom breaks: Stay calm. Stop and pull out, and put on a new condom if you continue. To lower the chance of pregnancy, the girl can take emergency contraceptive pills as soon as possible, within 5 days, from a pharmacy or health center. Because of the risk of HIV and other infections, go to a health center as soon as you can, ideally within 72 hours, to ask about HIV prevention medicine and testing. You can call 114 for free advice.
  B CSA-0016 [contraception] Condom slipped off during sex: If a condom slips off, stop and gently remove it. Semen may have entered the vagina or anus, so think of it like having sex without a condom. The girl can use emergency contraceptive pills as soon as possible, within 5 days. Visit a health center soon, ideally within 72 hours, to talk about HIV prevention medicine and STI testing. Next time, hold the rim when pulling out and check the condom fits well.

0.73
  A PRB-0028 [pregnancy] Healthy eating in pregnancy: A healthy pregnancy diet includes a variety of foods every day: staples like sweet potatoes, cassava, maize or rice; beans, peas, groundnuts, eggs, fish or meat for protein; dark green vegetables like dodo (amaranth) and cassava leaves; fruits; and milk if you can. Eat an extra small meal or snack each day. Use iodised salt, limit sugary drinks and very salty food, and drink clean water. Good food helps your baby grow and keeps you strong.
  B PRB-0039 [pregnancy] Vegetarian diet in pregnancy: If you do not eat meat or fish, you can still have a healthy pregnancy. Eat plenty of beans, peas, lentils, soybeans, groundnuts and other nuts for protein and iron. If you eat eggs and milk, they are excellent. Add dark green vegetables and fruits rich in vitamin C to help absorb iron. Take your iron and folic acid supplements as advised. Tell your health worker about your diet so they can check you have enough nutrients.

0.73
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PU-001 [puberty] What is puberty: Puberty is the time when your body changes from a child's body to an adult's body. Your body, your feelings and your social life all change. It is normal and healthy. It does not start at the same age for everyone, and girls usually start earlier than boys.

0.73
  A CON-0134 [consent_safety] Calling helplines without airtime: The main help lines in Rwanda, 116 (child helpline), 3512 (GBV line), 3029 (Isange) and 112 (emergency), are toll-free, so you can call even without airtime. If you do not have a phone, you can borrow one from a trusted person, ask a teacher, local leader or health worker to call, or go directly to a police station, health centre or Isange. Help should never depend on having money.
  B SRV-0091 [services_rights] Are helpline calls free: The main health and protection lines in Rwanda, such as 114, 116, 3512, 3029, 112 and 912, are meant to be free to call, so you do not need airtime. If a call does not go through, try again, use another phone, or go in person to a health centre, police station or trusted adult. In an emergency, keep trying or ask someone nearby to help.

0.73
  A SRV-0059 [services_rights] When a health worker shares your information: Health workers must keep your health information private. If you find out that a nurse, doctor or other staff member told people about your visit, test results or problem without your permission, that is wrong.
