You can talk to the person in charge of the facility, with a trusted adult if you like. It is not your fault, and you still have the right to go on getting care.
  B SRV-0063 [services_rights] When a health worker may need to share information: Health workers keep most things private, but there are a few situations where they may need to involve others to protect you or someone else. For example, if you are being abused, if your life is in danger, or if you might seriously harm yourself. Even then, a good health worker will explain what they need to do, who they will tell and why, and will try to include you in the decision.

0.73
  A STI-0082 [stis] What are pubic lice: Pubic lice, sometimes called crabs, are tiny insects that live in pubic hair and suck small amounts of blood. They can also live in other coarse body hair, like armpits, chest or eyebrows. They are different from head lice. Pubic lice are annoying and itchy but not dangerous, and they can be treated. Having them is nothing to be ashamed of; see a health worker for help.
  B STI-0084 [stis] Signs of pubic lice: The main sign of pubic lice is itching in the pubic area, often worse at night. You may see tiny greyish or brownish insects moving in the hair, or small white eggs (nits) stuck to the hair near the skin. Some people notice small blue spots or tiny drops of blood on their underwear from bites. Scratching can make the skin sore. A health worker can confirm it quickly.

0.73
  A CSB-0003 [contraception] What happens when an implant is inserted: Getting an implant takes only a few minutes. A trained nurse or health worker first talks with you and checks that the implant suits you. They clean the inside of your upper arm and give a small injection to numb it. Then they slide the rod or rods under the skin with a special applicator. No stitches are needed. They cover it with a bandage, show you how to feel it, and give you a card with the removal date.
  B CSB-0008 [contraception] How an implant is removed: Only a trained health worker should remove an implant. They clean your arm, give a numbing injection, make a very small cut near the end of the rod, and gently pull it out. It usually takes a few minutes. You may have a small bandage and some soreness or bruising for a few days afterwards. Never try to remove an implant yourself or ask an untrained person to do it, because this can cause infection or injury.

0.73
  A PUB-0007 [puberty] What causes puberty to start: Puberty starts when a part of your brain sends chemical messages, called hormones, to your body. In girls these messages tell the ovaries to make more estrogen. In boys they tell the testicles to make more testosterone. These hormones cause the changes you notice, like body hair, growth, breasts or a deeper voice. Nothing you do or eat switches it on. It happens naturally when your body is ready.
  B PU-001 [puberty] What is puberty: Puberty is the time when your body changes from a child's body to an adult's body. Your body, your feelings and your social life all change. It is normal and healthy. It does not start at the same age for everyone, and girls usually start earlier than boys.

0.73
  A PRB-0072 [pregnancy] Mood swings in pregnancy: During pregnancy, changing hormones, tiredness and worries about the future can make your moods change quickly. You may feel happy one moment and teary or irritable the next. This is common. Rest, eat well, stay active, and talk about your feelings with someone you trust. Sharing worries with your health worker also helps. If sadness or worry lasts for weeks or stops you from coping, ask for help.
  B PRB-0073 [pregnancy] Depression and anxiety during pregnancy: Some women feel very sad, hopeless, worried or anxious for weeks during pregnancy. This is more than normal mood swings and can be depression or anxiety, which are common and treatable. It is not a weakness. If you lose interest in things, cannot sleep or eat, feel worthless or cannot cope, talk to a health worker at antenatal care or call 114. If you have thoughts of harming yourself, get help right away by calling 112 or going to the nearest health facility.

0.73
  A STI-0072 [stis] Testing for hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is found with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick test. If it is positive, more blood tests show whether the infection is new or long-lasting and how the liver is doing. Testing is a good idea if you were never vaccinated, if a family member or partner has hepatitis B, or if you are pregnant. Ask at your nearest health center or call 114 to find out where testing is offered.
  B STI-0080 [stis] Testing for hepatitis C: Hepatitis C is tested with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick test. If it is positive, a second test checks whether the virus is still active, because some people clear it naturally. Testing is a good idea if you ever shared needles, razors or had unsterile tattoos or piercings, or a family member has it. Rwanda has expanded hepatitis testing; ask at a health center or call 114.

0.73
  A STI-0098 [stis] Signs of bacterial vaginosis: Signs of bacterial vaginosis can include a thin white or grey vaginal discharge and a fishy smell, which may be stronger after sex or during your period. It does not usually cause much itching or soreness. Many women have no signs at all. Trichomoniasis and other infections can cause similar signs, so a health worker needs to check to know what is causing them.
  B STI-0099 [stis] What causes bacterial vaginosis: The exact cause of bacterial vaginosis is not fully known. It happens when the healthy bacteria in the vagina decrease and other bacteria increase. Things that make it more likely include washing or douching inside the vagina, using scented soaps, having a new sex partner or several partners, and not using condoms. Girls who have never had sex can also get it.

0.73
  A MEN-0120 [menstruation] Spotting between periods: Spotting means light bleeding or brown marks between periods, needing only a liner or none. It can happen in the first years of periods, around ovulation, with some contraceptive methods, or from stress. But it can also be a sign of infection or another problem, especially if it happens often, after sex, or with pain, bad-smelling discharge or fever. If spotting keeps happening, see a health worker at your health center.
  B MEN-0122 [menstruation] Spotting a few days before the period: Many people see light brown or pink spotting a day or two before their period properly starts. This is often the first sign that the womb lining is beginning to shed and is usually normal. It often leads into the full flow. You can use a liner or a thin pad. If spotting before your period lasts many days, is getting longer each month, or comes with pain, see a health worker.

0.73
  A STI-0045 [stis] HPV in boys and men: Boys and men get HPV too, and can pass it to partners. Most have no signs and clear the virus naturally. Some get genital warts on the penis, scrotum, anus or groin. Rarely, long-lasting HPV can cause cancers of the penis, anus or throat. There is no routine HPV test for men. Using condoms and having fewer partners lowers the risk. If you notice any growths, see a health worker.
  B STI-0052 [stis] HPV vaccine and boys: Some countries vaccinate boys against HPV too, but in many countries, including Rwanda, programmes focus on girls because cervical cancer causes the most deaths. When most girls are vaccinated, boys are also indirectly protected because less HPV spreads in the community. Boys can protect themselves and partners by using condoms and limiting partners. Ask a health worker if you want to know about vaccine options for boys.

0.73
  A PRA-0018 [pregnancy] Pregnancy before the first period: Yes, it is possible. A girl releases her first egg about two weeks before her first period. If she has vaginal sex, or sperm gets into the vagina, around that time, she could get pregnant even though she has never bled. Because you cannot know when your first egg will come, not having had a period yet does not protect you.
  B PRA-0024 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex just before your period: Pregnancy from sex in the few days just before a period is less likely, because ovulation has usually already happened. But it is not impossible. Many young people cannot know exactly when their next period will come, and ovulation can happen later than expected. What you think is the week before your period might actually be your fertile time. So these days are not guaranteed safe.

0.73
  A MEN-0102 [menstruation] Lower back pain during periods: Many girls feel aching in the lower back during their period. The same muscle squeezing that causes cramps in the womb can spread pain to the back. Warmth on your lower back, gentle stretching, a back rub, rest, and pain relief from a pharmacy can help. If the back pain is severe, on one side, or comes with fever, pain when peeing, or vomiting, see a health worker because it may be something else.
  B M-006 [menstruation] Period pain and cramps: Pain in the lower back or stomach during a period is common. To feel better you can move and stretch, do some exercise, put a hot compress on the painful area or have a warm bath. Ibuprofen may help if you have it. If the pain is very strong or stops you from doing your normal activities, see a health worker.

0.73
  A PUB-0071 [puberty] Why stretch marks appear: Stretch marks are lines on the skin that appear when the body grows quickly, as it often does in puberty. They are common on the hips, thighs, buttocks, breasts, back and arms, in both girls and boys. They may first look pink, purple or dark, and later fade. Stretch marks are harmless and very common. They do not mean you are unhealthy.
  B PUB-0072 [puberty] Can stretch marks go away: Stretch marks usually fade over time, becoming lighter and less noticeable, but they often do not disappear completely. Creams and oils sold to remove them have little proven effect, though moisturising can keep skin soft. Most people have some stretch marks, and they are nothing to be ashamed of. If the marks are very wide, many, and appear with other changes, a health worker can check.

0.73
  A CSA-0071 [contraception] Missed several combined pills: Missing several pills, or starting a pack late, raises the chance of pregnancy. Take a pill as soon as you remember and continue the pack, then use condoms or avoid sex for the next 7 days. Read your pack leaflet carefully. If you had unprotected sex recently, talk to a pharmacist or nurse quickly about emergency contraception. You can call 114 for free advice.
  B CSA-0145 [contraception] EC after missed regular pills: If you missed several combined pills, or were late with a progestin-only pill, and had unprotected sex, emergency contraception may be needed. Talk to a pharmacist or health worker quickly, as it depends on how many pills you missed and when. Keep taking your regular pills, and use condoms until you are protected again.

0.73
  A MEN-0102 [menstruation] Lower back pain during periods: Many girls feel aching in the lower back during their period. The same muscle squeezing that causes cramps in the womb can spread pain to the back. Warmth on your lower back, gentle stretching, a back rub, rest, and pain relief from a pharmacy can help. If the back pain is severe, on one side, or comes with fever, pain when peeing, or vomiting, see a health worker because it may be something else.
  B MEN-0103 [menstruation] Leg pain during periods: Some girls feel aching or heaviness in their thighs or legs during their period. This happens because cramping pain from the womb can spread along nerves into the legs. Warmth, gentle stretching, walking, raising your legs while resting, and pain relief from a pharmacy can help. See a health worker if one leg is swollen, red, hot or very painful, or if the pain is severe and does not go away after your period.

0.73
  A CSA-0122 [contraception] How long after sex EC pills work: Emergency contraceptive pills can be taken up to 5 days (120 hours) after unprotected sex. The sooner you take them, the better they work, so do not wait until the next morning if you can get them today. Even on day 4 or 5 they can still help. A copper IUD can also be fitted within 5 days.
  B CSA-0148 [contraception] Sex again after taking EC: Emergency pills only protect against sex that happened before you took them. They do not protect you during the rest of the month. If you have sex again after taking EC, use a condom or start a regular method. Because EC can delay ovulation, you might release an egg later than usual and get pregnant.

0.73
  A PUB-0003 [puberty] Puberty starting very early: If a girl shows breast growth or pubic hair before about age 8, or a boy shows genital growth, pubic hair or a deep voice before about age 9, this is called early puberty. Sometimes it is just the child's natural timing, but sometimes there is a medical cause that can be treated. Please take the child to a health center so a nurse or doctor can check. It is not the child's fault.
  B PUB-0004 [puberty] Puberty starting late: Some young people start puberty later than others, and this is often normal, especially if parents were also late. But if a girl has no breast development by about 13, or a boy has no growth of the testicles by about 14, it is a good idea to see a health worker. They can check your growth and health and explain what is happening. Late puberty can often be helped.

0.73
  A CSA-0046 [contraception] Condoms with spermicide: Some condoms are coated with a spermicide called nonoxynol-9. They do not protect better against pregnancy than ordinary lubricated condoms, and the spermicide can irritate the vagina or anus. Frequent use of nonoxynol-9 may raise the risk of HIV infection. Ordinary condoms without spermicide are the better choice, especially for anal sex or for people at risk of HIV.
  B CSA-0162 [contraception] Spermicide and HIV risk: Spermicides do not protect against HIV or other STIs. Frequent use of nonoxynol-9 can irritate the vagina or anus and cause small sores, which may actually raise the risk of getting HIV. For this reason, spermicides are not recommended for people at higher risk of HIV, and should never be used for anal sex. Condoms are the safer choice.

0.73
  A CSA-0065 [contraception] When to start the combined pill: You can start the combined pill any day of the month if it is reasonably sure you are not pregnant. If you start within 5 days after your period began, you are protected straight away. If you start at another time, use condoms or avoid sex for the first 7 days. A health worker will check you can use it safely and explain exactly how to begin.
  B CSA-0066 [contraception] How soon the pill starts working: If you start combined pills within the first 5 days of your period, you are protected from pregnancy right away. If you start on any other day, it takes 7 days of taking pills every day to be protected, so use condoms or avoid sex during that time. Remember that pills never protect against HIV or other STIs.

0.73
  A PRB-0031 [pregnancy] Food safety in pregnancy: During pregnancy your body is more sensitive to some food infections, which can harm the baby. Wash hands before cooking and eating, wash fruit and vegetables with clean water, cook meat, fish and eggs well until no pink or runny parts remain, and drink boiled or pasteurised milk. Avoid food left uncovered for a long time and drink safe water. These simple steps help prevent food poisoning and infections.
  B PRB-0032 [pregnancy] Food taboos in pregnancy: In some families, pregnant women are told not to eat certain foods, like eggs, meat or some fruits, because of beliefs that they harm the baby or make birth difficult. Most of these beliefs are not true, and avoiding healthy foods can leave you and your baby without important nutrients. Eggs, meat, fish, milk and fruits are good in pregnancy if prepared safely. If you are unsure about a food, ask your health worker.

0.73
  A CSA-0023 [contraception] Wearing two condoms at once: Wearing two male condoms at the same time does not give double protection. The two condoms rub against each other, which makes them more likely to tear or slip. One condom, used correctly every time, gives the best protection. For extra protection against pregnancy, combine one condom with another method like pills or an implant.
  B CSA-0024 [contraception] Using a male and female condom together: Do not use a male condom and a female (internal) condom at the same time. They can rub against each other, causing one or both to tear, slip or be pushed out of place. Choose one type of condom and use it correctly. To add more pregnancy protection, use the condom with another method like the pill, an injection or an implant.

0.73
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B H-004 [hiv] HIV testing: Testing is voluntary and private. In Rwanda, people aged 12 and above can agree to a test themselves, and for children aged 10 to 11 a parent or guardian agrees. A self-test kit is for people aged 16 and above and is sold at health facilities and pharmacies. A positive self-test must be confirmed by a health worker.

0.73
  A CSB-0012 [contraception] Irregular bleeding with an implant: Changes in bleeding are the most common effect of the implant. You may have spotting, bleeding at unexpected times, or periods that come more or less often. This is not harmful and does not mean the implant is failing. Often it becomes more regular after the first year. If the bleeding bothers you, a health worker may be able to offer treatment that helps. Go back if bleeding is very heavy or long.
  B CSB-0014 [contraception] Heavy or long bleeding with an implant: A few people have heavier or longer bleeding with the implant. If you bleed much more than usual, for more than 8 days, or feel weak, dizzy or very tired, go to your health center. A health worker can check you, look for other causes, and may offer treatment that helps. They can also check for anaemia, which can happen with heavy bleeding. If you soak through pads very fast or faint, go to a health facility now.

0.73
  A CSA-0101 [contraception] Combined pill while breastfeeding: Combined pills are usually not recommended for women who are breastfeeding a baby less than 6 months old, because estrogen may reduce milk. Breastfeeding mothers can use progestin-only pills, condoms, LAM or other methods instead. After 6 months, combined pills can usually be used. A health worker can help a new mother choose.
  B CSA-0118 [contraception] Progestin-only pills while breastfeeding: Progestin-only pills are a good choice for breastfeeding mothers. They do not reduce milk and do not harm the baby. A breastfeeding mother can start them at any time after birth. When breastfeeding stops or reduces, she can continue them or switch to another method; protection is strongest while breastfeeding.

0.73
  A PUB-0126 [puberty] Lump or swelling in a testicle: A new lump, hardness, or swelling in a testicle or the scrotum should be checked by a health worker soon, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as fluid collections or cysts, but some need treatment, and a few can be serious. Finding problems early makes treatment easier. If the swelling comes with sudden strong pain, go to a health facility immediately.
  B STI-0111 [stis] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle is an emergency. It can be testicular torsion, where the testicle twists and loses its blood supply, which can destroy the testicle within hours. Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.

0.73
  A CSA-0138 [contraception] Types of EC pills: There are three kinds of emergency contraceptive pills: progestin-only pills containing levonorgestrel (the most common), pills containing ulipristal acetate, and some ordinary combined pills used in a special way under a provider's guidance. Each has its own instructions. A pharmacist or health worker will give you the right one and explain how to take it.
  B CSA-0155 [contraception] Ordinary pills as EC: Some ordinary combined pills can be used as emergency contraception in a special way, but the right number depends on the brand, and they cause more nausea than dedicated EC pills. Do not try this on your own. Ask a pharmacist or health worker; dedicated emergency pills or a copper IUD are better choices if available.

0.73
  A CSA-0031 [contraception] Condoms and protection from HIV: Yes. Male and female condoms, used correctly every time, are very good at preventing HIV because they stop semen, vaginal fluids and blood from passing between partners. They protect against HIV during vaginal and anal sex. To be protected, use a condom for the whole act of sex, every time. You can also ask a health center about HIV testing and other ways to prevent HIV.
  B CSA-0032 [contraception] STIs that condoms protect against less well: Condoms protect very well against infections spread through fluids, like HIV, gonorrhoea and chlamydia. They give less protection against infections spread by skin-to-skin contact, such as herpes, genital warts (HPV) and syphilis sores, if the sore or wart is in an area the condom does not cover. Condoms still lower the risk a lot. If you see sores or warts, avoid sex and visit a health center.

0.73
  A SRV-0030 [services_rights] Questions to ask a pharmacist: Pharmacists are trained health professionals, and you can ask them questions. Useful questions include: what is this medicine for, how and when should I take it, what side effects can happen, can I take it with my other medicines, what should I do if I miss a dose, and when should I see a nurse or doctor. You can ask to speak quietly at the side if you want privacy.
  B SRV-0047 [services_rights] Questions to ask about medicine you are given: When a health worker gives you medicine, it is good to ask: what is it for, how and when do I take it, for how many days, what should I avoid, what side effects can happen, and when should I come back. Ask what to do if you forget to take it. Finish the full course if told to, even if you feel better. If something worries you later, go back or call 114.

0.73
  A HIV-0018 [hiv] HIV risk from oral sex: The risk of getting HIV from oral sex (mouth on penis, vagina or anus) is very low, much lower than vaginal or anal sex, but it is not zero. The risk goes up if there are cuts, sores or bleeding gums in the mouth, sores on the genitals, or if semen goes into the mouth. Oral sex can also pass other STIs. Using a condom or a barrier for oral sex lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.

0.72
  A HIV-0017 [hiv] HIV risk from anal sex: Anal sex without a condom carries the highest risk of passing HIV during sex. The lining of the anus is thin and can tear easily, letting the virus get into the blood. The receptive partner, the one being penetrated, has the highest risk, but the other partner can also get HIV. This is true for any couple, whether man and woman or two men. Using a condom with plenty of water-based lubricant every time lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0018 [hiv] HIV risk from oral sex: The risk of getting HIV from oral sex (mouth on penis, vagina or anus) is very low, much lower than vaginal or anal sex, but it is not zero. The risk goes up if there are cuts, sores or bleeding gums in the mouth, sores on the genitals, or if semen goes into the mouth. Oral sex can also pass other STIs. Using a condom or a barrier for oral sex lowers the risk.

0.72
  A STI-0069 [stis] What is hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is an infection of the liver caused by a virus. It can cause a short illness, or it can stay in the body for life (chronic hepatitis B), slowly damaging the liver and sometimes leading to liver cancer. Many people have no signs for years. Hepatitis B can be prevented with a safe and effective vaccine, and chronic infection can be controlled with treatment from a health worker.
  B STI-0072 [stis] Testing for hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is found with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick test. If it is positive, more blood tests show whether the infection is new or long-lasting and how the liver is doing. Testing is a good idea if you were never vaccinated, if a family member or partner has hepatitis B, or if you are pregnant. Ask at your nearest health center or call 114 to find out where testing is offered.

0.72
  A STI-0063 [stis] What is trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis, often called trich, is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by a tiny parasite. It mostly infects the vagina in women and the urethra (the tube inside the penis) in men. Many people have no signs, especially men. It can be easily cured with the right medicine from a health worker. Both partners need treatment at the same time to avoid passing it back and forth.
  B STI-0066 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in boys and men: Most boys and men with trichomoniasis have no signs at all, which is why they can pass it on without knowing. Some notice itching or irritation inside the penis, a thin discharge, or burning after urinating or ejaculating. Signs may come and go. Because other infections cause similar signs, only a health worker can tell the cause. If your partner is diagnosed with trich, you need treatment too.

0.72
  A CON-0086 [consent_safety] Main types of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence has four main types. Physical violence: hitting, slapping, kicking, burning or using weapons. Sexual violence: rape, unwanted touching, forced sex, sexual harassment and exploitation. Emotional or psychological violence: insults, threats, humiliation, control and isolation. Economic violence: taking someone's money, stopping them from working or studying, or refusing basic needs. A person may experience more than one type. All are harmful. Help: 3512 or Isange (3029).
  B CON-0095 [consent_safety] Health effects of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence can harm health in many ways: injuries, chronic pain, unwanted pregnancy, HIV and other infections, problems during pregnancy, depression, anxiety, trouble sleeping, alcohol or drug use, and thoughts of self-harm. Children who experience or see violence may struggle at school and with feelings. Getting care early protects health. Isange One Stop Centres (3029) and health centres offer medical and psychosocial care, and 3512 can guide survivors.

0.72
  A HIV-0079 [hiv] Does a normal blood test check for HIV: No. A normal blood test for things like malaria, blood count or sugar does not automatically test for HIV. HIV testing is done only with your knowledge and agreement, and with counselling. If you want to know your HIV status, you need to ask specifically for an HIV test. Never assume you are negative because you had other blood tests.
  B HIV-0092 [hiv] What an HIV test cannot tell you: A standard HIV test tells you whether you have HIV. It does not tell you when you got it, who gave it to you, how strong your immune system is, or whether you have AIDS. Other tests, such as viral load and CD4 count, help with that after a diagnosis. Try not to blame yourself or others based on a test; focus on getting care and support.

0.72
  A PRA-0009 [pregnancy] When a boy becomes able to cause pregnancy: A boy can make a girl pregnant once his body starts making sperm during puberty. This often begins somewhere between about 11 and 15 years old, but it differs for each boy. A sign is having wet dreams or ejaculating, but sperm may be present even before he notices this. So a young boy who has started puberty can cause a pregnancy, even the first time he has sex.
  B PRA-0063 [pregnancy] Can a boy get pregnant: No. A boy's body does not have a womb or ovaries, so a boy cannot get pregnant. Pregnancy needs an egg and a womb where a baby can grow. Boys produce sperm, which can cause a pregnancy in a girl or woman. So boys share responsibility for preventing unplanned pregnancy.

0.72
  A STI-0086 [stis] What is scabies: Scabies is a skin condition caused by tiny mites that burrow under the skin, causing very itchy spots. It is not only an STI: it spreads through any long, close skin contact, such as sleeping in the same bed, within families, schools and boarding schools, and also during sex. Anyone can get it, and it has nothing to do with being dirty. Scabies can be treated.
  B STI-0087 [stis] How scabies spreads: Scabies spreads through prolonged skin-to-skin contact with a person who has it, such as sharing a bed, caring for family members, or sexual contact. Brief contact like a handshake rarely spreads it. Less often, it spreads through shared bedding, clothes or towels. Because it spreads easily in homes and dormitories, everyone who has close contact should be treated at the same time.

0.72
  A HIV-0103 [hiv] Using PrEP and condoms together: Using PrEP and condoms together gives you the strongest protection. PrEP protects against HIV, while condoms protect against HIV, other STIs and pregnancy. Condoms also add protection on days when you may have missed PrEP pills. You and your partner can choose what works best, and a health worker can help you plan.
  B HIV-0107 [hiv] PrEP when your partner has HIV: If your partner has HIV, PrEP can help protect you, especially while your partner is starting treatment or if their viral load is not yet undetectable. When your partner takes treatment well and is undetectable, HIV is not passed through sex. Talk with a health worker together about the best plan, which may include PrEP, condoms, and regular testing.

0.72
  A PUB-0126 [puberty] Lump or swelling in a testicle: A new lump, hardness, or swelling in a testicle or the scrotum should be checked by a health worker soon, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as fluid collections or cysts, but some need treatment, and a few can be serious. Finding problems early makes treatment easier. If the swelling comes with sudden strong pain, go to a health facility immediately.
  B WEL-0134 [wellbeing] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, severe pain in a testicle, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, can mean the testicle has twisted (testicular torsion). This is an emergency because the blood supply is cut off and the testicle can be lost within hours. Go to the nearest hospital or health facility immediately or call 112. Do not wait, and do not feel embarrassed; health workers see this often.

0.72
  A PUB-0024 [puberty] How breasts develop: Breasts develop slowly over several years, often from around age 8 to 13 until the late teens. First a small bud forms under the nipple. Then the breast and the darker area around the nipple, called the areola, get bigger. Breasts keep changing shape and size for a few years. Every girl's breasts grow at their own speed, and the final size and shape are different for everyone.
  B PUB-0040 [puberty] Do breasts keep growing after puberty: Breasts usually finish most of their growth by the late teens, but they can keep changing a little after that. Breast size can change with weight gain or loss, during your monthly cycle, during pregnancy and breastfeeding, and with age. So it is normal if your breasts look a little different from year to year. Sudden changes in one breast only should be checked by a health worker.

0.72
  A STI-0083 [stis] How pubic lice spread: Pubic lice spread mainly through close body contact, usually during sex or intimate touching, even without penetration. Less often, they can spread through sharing bedding, towels or clothing that someone with lice has just used. Condoms do not prevent pubic lice because the lice live in hair outside the area a condom covers. They do not come from toilet seats or animals.
  B STI-0085 [stis] Getting rid of pubic lice: Pubic lice are treated with a special lotion or shampoo recommended by a health worker or pharmacist, used exactly as instructed and often repeated after a week. Wash bedding, towels and clothes used in the past few days in hot water and dry them well. Sexual partners need treatment at the same time. Shaving alone does not reliably get rid of them. Never use petrol, kerosene or insecticides on your skin.

0.72
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B SRV-0071 [services_rights] HIV testing for children under 12: For children under 12, a parent or guardian usually needs to give consent for an HIV test. If you are younger than 12 and worried about HIV, talk to a trusted adult, such as a parent, relative, teacher or community health worker, who can go with you to the health centre. You can also call 114 or 116 for advice. Health workers will support you kindly.

0.72
  A HIV-0016 [hiv] HIV risk from vaginal sex: Vaginal sex without a condom is one of the most common ways HIV is passed, both from man to woman and from woman to man. The virus in semen, pre-cum or vaginal fluids can enter through the thin lining of the vagina or the penis. Risk is higher if there are sores, other STIs, bleeding, or if the partner with HIV is not on treatment. Using a condom correctly every time greatly lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0018 [hiv] HIV risk from oral sex: The risk of getting HIV from oral sex (mouth on penis, vagina or anus) is very low, much lower than vaginal or anal sex, but it is not zero. The risk goes up if there are cuts, sores or bleeding gums in the mouth, sores on the genitals, or if semen goes into the mouth. Oral sex can also pass other STIs. Using a condom or a barrier for oral sex lowers the risk.

0.72
  A MEN-0055 [menstruation] How to insert a menstrual cup: Wash your hands well. Fold the clean cup, for example into a C shape, so it is narrow. Relax and sit, squat or stand with one leg up. Gently push the folded cup into your vagina, aiming towards your lower back, not straight up. Let it open; you can turn it slightly or run a finger around it to check it has opened. It should sit lower than a tampon and you should not feel it. Practise at home first.
  B MEN-0056 [menstruation] How to remove a menstrual cup: Wash your hands. Sit or squat and relax your muscles. Gently pull the stem until you can reach the base of the cup. Pinch the base to break the seal, because pulling only the stem can hurt. Keep it upright as you slide it out slowly, then empty the blood into the toilet or latrine. Rinse it with clean water and reinsert, or wash and store it. If you cannot remove it after trying calmly, a nurse can help.

0.72
  A STI-0022 [stis] Second stage of syphilis: rash: If syphilis is not treated, a second stage can start weeks after the sore. The most common sign is a rash that does not usually itch, often on the palms of the hands and soles of the feet, but it can be anywhere. Some people also get fever, tiredness, swollen glands, sore throat, patchy hair loss or sores in the mouth. These signs go away by themselves, but the infection does not. Get tested at a health center.
  B STI-0023 [stis] Hidden and late stages of syphilis: After the early signs fade, syphilis can stay hidden in the body for many years with no symptoms. This is called latent syphilis. In some people who are never treated, it later causes late (tertiary) syphilis, which can seriously damage the brain, nerves, eyes, heart, blood vessels and bones. This damage can be life-threatening. Testing and treatment at any stage stops the infection from going further, so getting checked is always worth it.

0.72
  A MEN-0082 [menstruation] Should you wash inside the vagina: No, you do not need to wash inside your vagina, even during your period. The vagina cleans itself with natural discharge and has healthy bacteria that protect it. Washing or squirting water, soap, herbs or other products inside (douching) can upset this balance and cause infections. Only wash the outside area, the vulva, with clean water. If you notice a bad smell or itching, see a nurse instead of washing inside.
  B PUB-0100 [puberty] Putting things inside the vagina to clean it: The vagina cleans itself with its natural discharge. Washing inside it (douching), or putting soap, herbs, powders, salt, toothpaste, leaves or other objects inside to clean, tighten or dry it, can upset its natural balance, irritate or damage it, and cause infections. Only wash the outside. If you have a smell, itching or unusual discharge, see a health worker instead.

0.72
  A MEN-0069 [menstruation] Washing period underwear: After wearing period underwear, rinse it in cold water until the water runs mostly clear. Then wash it with mild soap by hand or in a gentle wash, without bleach or fabric softener, which can damage the absorbent layer. Hang it to dry fully in the sun or open air; do not wear it damp. Follow any instructions on the label. With good care, a pair can last for a long time.
  B MEN-0088 [menstruation] How often to change underwear during a period: Change into clean underwear at least once a day during your period, and change it sooner if blood leaks onto it or it feels damp. Cotton underwear is best because it lets air through and is easy to wash. Wash used underwear in cold water first to remove blood, then with soap. Dry it in the sun. Having a spare pair in your bag is helpful in case of leaks.

0.72
  A STI-0088 [stis] Signs of scabies: The main sign of scabies is strong itching, often worse at night. You may see small red bumps, blisters or thin wavy lines on the skin, often between the fingers, on the wrists, elbows, armpits, waist, buttocks and genitals. On the penis, it can cause itchy lumps. Scratching can cause sores that get infected. Many skin problems look similar, so see a health worker to check.
  B STI-0089 [stis] Treating scabies: Scabies is treated with a cream, lotion or sometimes tablets recommended by a health worker. Everyone living closely with you or sexual partners should be treated at the same time, even if they do not itch yet. Wash clothes, bedding and towels in hot water and dry them in the sun. Itching may last a few weeks after treatment; if it continues longer, go back to the health center.

0.72
  A PUB-0148 [puberty] Health benefits of male circumcision: Besides lowering a man's risk of getting HIV from vaginal sex, medical circumcision makes it easier to keep the penis clean and lowers the risk of some other infections, such as some STIs and infections under the foreskin. It does not give full protection against HIV or other STIs, so condoms and testing are still needed. The choice to be circumcised should be voluntary.
  B PUB-0155 [puberty] Myths about circumcision: Medical circumcision does not make the penis smaller, does not reduce fertility, and does not stop a man from enjoying sex. It does not make a man fully safe from HIV, so it is not a reason to stop using condoms. It also does not make someone more of a man. Ask a health worker if you hear other rumours.

0.72
  A CSA-0022 [contraception] Lubricated and non-lubricated condoms: Many male condoms come already lubricated, which makes them feel smoother and helps prevent tearing. Some are non-lubricated, which some people choose for oral sex. Both protect well when used correctly. If sex feels dry or rough with any condom, add a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Never add oil-based products to latex condoms.
  B CSA-0060 [contraception] Lubricant with female condoms: Female condoms usually come with lubricant already on them. Because they are made of nitrile or polyurethane, you can add any type of lubricant, including water-based, silicone-based or oil-based ones, without damaging them. Extra lubricant can make sex more comfortable and reduce noise. Do not use a male condom at the same time.

0.72
  A PUB-0036 [puberty] Breast lump in a teenage girl: Breasts can feel lumpy during puberty because they are made of glands and tissue that are still growing. Most lumps in teenage girls are harmless, and breast cancer is very rare at this age. Still, if you find a new lump that does not go away after your next period, grows, is hard, or comes with skin or nipple changes, see a health worker so they can examine it.
  B PUB-0039 [puberty] Painful lump under a boy's nipple: A tender, firm lump under one or both nipples is common in boys during puberty. It is usually normal breast tissue growing because of hormones and is not cancer. It often gets softer and disappears with time. Avoid squeezing or pressing it. See a health worker if the lump is hard, not under the nipple, growing fast, very painful, or has fluid or blood coming out.

0.72
  A HIV-0006 [hiv] Why symptoms cannot tell you if you have HIV: You cannot know if you have HIV by checking how you feel. Early signs like fever, rash or sore throat are the same as many common illnesses such as flu or malaria, and many people with HIV have no signs at all for years. Feeling healthy does not mean you are HIV negative, and feeling sick does not mean you are positive. The only way to know is to take an HIV test at a health center or youth corner.
  B HIV-0092 [hiv] What an HIV test cannot tell you: A standard HIV test tells you whether you have HIV. It does not tell you when you got it, who gave it to you, how strong your immune system is, or whether you have AIDS. Other tests, such as viral load and CD4 count, help with that after a diagnosis. Try not to blame yourself or others based on a test; focus on getting care and support.

0.72
  A CSA-0026 [contraception] Washing a condom to use again: Washing a condom does not make it safe to use again. Soap, water and rubbing can weaken it and cause tiny holes you cannot see, and it may still carry sperm or germs. Condoms are made for one use only. Throw it away after sex and use a new one next time.
  B CSA-0058 [contraception] Reusing a female condom: Female condoms are made to be used only once. Washing or reusing them is not recommended, because they may tear or carry germs and sperm. Use a new female condom each time you have sex, and throw the old one in a bin or pit latrine.

0.72
  A HIV-0041 [hiv] How well condoms protect against HIV: When used correctly every time, from start to finish, male and female condoms are very effective at preventing HIV and many other STIs, and they also prevent pregnancy. Most failures happen because condoms are not used every time, are put on late, are torn by nails or teeth, or are used with oil-based lubricants. Check the expiry date, open the packet carefully, and use a new condom for each act of sex. Free condoms are available at many health centers.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.72
  A CSA-0129 [contraception] How effective EC pills are: Emergency contraceptive pills greatly reduce the chance of pregnancy but do not remove it. For example, if 100 women had unprotected sex once in the middle of their cycle, about 8 might become pregnant without EC, but only about 1 or 2 with EC pills. They work best when taken as soon as possible. The copper IUD is even more effective as EC.
  B CSA-0155 [contraception] Ordinary pills as EC: Some ordinary combined pills can be used as emergency contraception in a special way, but the right number depends on the brand, and they cause more nausea than dedicated EC pills. Do not try this on your own. Ask a pharmacist or health worker; dedicated emergency pills or a copper IUD are better choices if available.

0.72
  A STI-0095 [stis] Preventing yeast infections: To lower the chance of yeast infections, wear clean cotton underwear, change out of wet or sweaty clothes soon, and avoid very tight clothes. Wash the outside of the genitals with clean water only and avoid scented soaps, sprays or douching inside the vagina. Wipe from front to back after using the toilet. Only take antibiotics when a health worker prescribes them. If infections keep coming back, get checked.
  B STI-0096 [stis] Treating a yeast infection: Yeast infections are treated with antifungal medicine, such as a cream, vaginal tablet or pill, recommended by a health worker or pharmacist. See a health worker if it is your first time, if you are pregnant, if signs keep coming back, or if treatment does not work, because another infection might be the cause. Do not put yogurt, garlic, herbs or other home remedies inside the vagina.

0.72
  A HIV-0010 [hiv] Which body fluids can carry HIV: HIV is found in enough amount to infect someone only in certain body fluids: blood, semen (cum), pre-cum, vaginal fluids, rectal (anal) fluids and breast milk. HIV is passed when one of these fluids from a person with HIV gets into another person's body, for example through sex without a condom, sharing needles, or from mother to baby. These fluids need to reach the bloodstream or a mucous membrane, like the inside of the vagina, anus, penis tip or mouth.
  B HIV-0011 [hiv] Body fluids that do not pass HIV: Saliva (spit), tears, sweat, urine (pee) and faeces (poo) do not pass HIV, unless they have visible blood in them. These fluids do not carry enough virus to infect anyone. So you cannot get HIV from someone's sweat during sports, from their tears, from being spat on, or from sharing a toilet. HIV is passed only through blood, semen, pre-cum, vaginal fluids, anal fluids and breast milk.

0.72
  A STI-0063 [stis] What is trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis, often called trich, is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by a tiny parasite. It mostly infects the vagina in women and the urethra (the tube inside the penis) in men. Many people have no signs, especially men. It can be easily cured with the right medicine from a health worker. Both partners need treatment at the same time to avoid passing it back and forth.
  B STI-0065 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in girls and women: Many girls and women with trichomoniasis have no signs. When signs appear, they may include a frothy yellow-green or grey vaginal discharge, often with a strong or fishy smell, itching or soreness around the vagina, burning when urinating, and discomfort during sex. These signs can also come from other infections such as bacterial vaginosis or yeast, so only a test at a health center can tell the cause.

0.72
  A CSA-0022 [contraception] Lubricated and non-lubricated condoms: Many male condoms come already lubricated, which makes them feel smoother and helps prevent tearing. Some are non-lubricated, which some people choose for oral sex. Both protect well when used correctly. If sex feels dry or rough with any condom, add a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Never add oil-based products to latex condoms.
  B WEL-0102 [wellbeing] Choosing a lubricant with condoms: If you use a lubricant with latex condoms, choose a water-based or silicone-based lubricant. Oil-based products like petroleum jelly, body lotion, cooking oil, shea butter or baby oil can weaken latex and make condoms break. Saliva is not a good lubricant and can carry infections. Some condoms already come lubricated. You can ask at a health center or pharmacy for a lubricant that is safe with condoms.

0.72
  A PRA-0046 [pregnancy] Wet dreams and pregnancy: A wet dream is when a boy ejaculates while sleeping. It is a normal part of puberty. A boy's wet dream cannot make anyone pregnant unless his fresh semen gets into or right at a girl's vagina, which does not happen when he is simply asleep in his own bed. A wet dream does mean his body is making sperm and could cause pregnancy during sex.
  B PUB-0145 [puberty] Do girls have wet dreams: Yes, girls can also have sexual dreams that cause excitement, wetness in the vagina, or even an orgasm while sleeping. This is normal and harmless, just like wet dreams in boys. Dreams about sex do not mean you have done something wrong or that you want to act on them. They are a normal part of growing up.

0.72
  A CSA-0074 [contraception] Spotting between periods on the pill: Light bleeding or spotting between periods is common in the first few months of taking the pill. It is not harmful and does not mean the pill is failing. Missing pills or taking them late can make it worse, so take one every day. If bleeding is heavy, lasts a long time, comes with pain, or starts after months of normal cycles, see a health worker.
  B MEN-0120 [menstruation] Spotting between periods: Spotting means light bleeding or brown marks between periods, needing only a liner or none. It can happen in the first years of periods, around ovulation, with some contraceptive methods, or from stress. But it can also be a sign of infection or another problem, especially if it happens often, after sex, or with pain, bad-smelling discharge or fever. If spotting keeps happening, see a health worker at your health center.

0.72
  A PRA-0046 [pregnancy] Wet dreams and pregnancy: A wet dream is when a boy ejaculates while sleeping. It is a normal part of puberty. A boy's wet dream cannot make anyone pregnant unless his fresh semen gets into or right at a girl's vagina, which does not happen when he is simply asleep in his own bed. A wet dream does mean his body is making sperm and could cause pregnancy during sex.
  B PUB-0142 [puberty] Never having a wet dream: Not every boy has wet dreams. Some have many, some have only a few, and some never have them at all. All of these are normal and do not mean anything is wrong with your body or your fertility. Wet dreams may also happen less often if you ejaculate in other ways. If you have no other signs of puberty by around 14, see a health worker.

0.72
  A CSA-0190 [contraception] Conditions for LAM to work: LAM only works when all three conditions are true: your monthly bleeding has not returned since birth; you are fully or nearly fully breastfeeding, often, day and night, without giving other foods or drinks regularly; and your baby is less than 6 months old. If any one of these changes, you need another method straight away.
  B CSA-0191 [contraception] When LAM stops protecting you: LAM stops protecting you when your period comes back, when you start giving your baby other food, water or formula regularly, when you breastfeed less often or stop night feeds, or when your baby reaches 6 months. Pregnancy can happen even before the first period returns, so start another method as soon as any of these happens.

0.72
  A CON-0045 [consent_safety] What is sexual abuse: Sexual abuse is any sexual act or contact that a person does not agree to, cannot agree to, or is forced, tricked or pressured into. It includes unwanted touching, rape, forcing someone to touch another person, showing sexual images, sexual comments or messages, and taking sexual photos. Any sexual activity with a child is abuse. Abuse can be done by strangers, but most often by someone the person knows. It is never the victim's fault. Help: 116, 3512 or Isange on 3029.
  B CON-0047 [consent_safety] Sexual abuse without touching: Sexual abuse does not always involve touching. Non-contact abuse includes showing a child pornography or sexual pictures, exposing private parts to them, watching them undress or bathe for sexual reasons, making them watch sexual acts, sending sexual messages, or asking them to pose or undress for photos or videos. These acts harm children and are serious. If a child experiences this, they should tell a trusted adult. It can be reported to 116 or 3512.

0.72
  A CSA-0182 [contraception] How effective fertility awareness is: Fertility awareness methods can work well for couples who use them carefully, but mistakes are common. For example, with the Standard Days Method about 12 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year with typical use, and about 5 with correct use. Other methods have similar or wider ranges. They are less effective than pills, injections, implants or IUDs.
  B CSA-0186 [contraception] Partner support for fertility awareness: Fertility awareness methods only work if both partners agree to avoid sex or use condoms on fertile days. If a partner pressures you on those days, the method will fail. Talk openly before choosing it. If your partner will not cooperate, choose a method you can control yourself, like pills, an injection, an implant or an IUD.

0.72
  A STI-0047 [stis] Condoms and HPV: Condoms lower the chance of getting HPV and genital warts, but they do not give full protection, because HPV can live on skin that a condom does not cover, like the scrotum, vulva or thighs. Using condoms every time is still very worth it, because they also protect against many other STIs, HIV and pregnancy. Combining condoms with the HPV vaccine and fewer partners gives the best protection.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.72
  A MEN-0052 [menstruation] When to replace reusable pads: Good reusable pads can last many months, often around a year or more, depending on how often you use and wash them. Replace a pad when the cloth becomes thin, torn, hard, or no longer absorbs well, or if it keeps a bad smell even after careful washing and sun drying. Having several pads lets each one rest and dry fully, which makes them last longer.
  B MEN-0053 [menstruation] Reusable versus disposable pads: Disposable pads are used once and thrown away. They are easy to use, good when you are away from water, but they cost money every month and create waste. Reusable pads cost more at first but can last a long time, saving money and making less rubbish. They need water, soap, a private place to wash, and sun to dry. Both are safe when used properly. Some girls use reusables at home and disposables at school.

0.72
  A HIV-0050 [hiv] Washing or peeing after sex does not prevent HIV: Washing your genitals, peeing, douching or using water, soap, salt or other products after sex does not prevent HIV or pregnancy. The virus can enter the body very quickly during sex. Washing inside the vagina can even irritate it and raise infection risk. If you had sex without a condom, go to a health center within 72 hours to ask about PEP and emergency contraception.
  B WEL-0127 [wellbeing] Peeing and washing after sex: Urinating after sex can help flush out germs and may lower the chance of a urine infection, especially for women. Washing the outside of the genitals with clean water is fine. However, peeing or washing after sex does not prevent pregnancy, HIV or other STIs. Do not wash inside the vagina. Condoms remain the best protection against infections, and contraception protects against pregnancy.

0.72
  A STI-0039 [stis] What is HPV: HPV (human papillomavirus) is a very common virus passed through skin-to-skin sexual contact. Most sexually active people get it at some point, and most never know because it causes no signs and the body clears it by itself. Some types of HPV cause genital warts. Other types, if they stay for many years, can cause cancer, especially cancer of the cervix. Vaccination and screening protect against these problems.
  B STI-0045 [stis] HPV in boys and men: Boys and men get HPV too, and can pass it to partners. Most have no signs and clear the virus naturally. Some get genital warts on the penis, scrotum, anus or groin. Rarely, long-lasting HPV can cause cancers of the penis, anus or throat. There is no routine HPV test for men. Using condoms and having fewer partners lowers the risk. If you notice any growths, see a health worker.

0.72
  A PRA-0032 [pregnancy] Sperm on fingers touching the vagina: Pregnancy from fingers is very unlikely. It could only happen if fresh, wet semen were on the fingers and was pushed right into the vagina immediately. Once semen has dried or been wiped off, the sperm are no longer able to cause pregnancy. If there was no semen on the fingers, there is no chance at all. A health worker can answer worries in private.
  B PRA-0070 [pregnancy] Touching a boy's penis with your hand: Touching a boy's penis with your hand cannot make you pregnant, even if he ejaculates on your hand. Pregnancy can only happen if fresh semen then gets into or right at your vagina. Wiping or washing your hands afterwards removes any risk. A hand job alone, with no semen near the vagina, carries no chance of pregnancy.

0.72
  A CSA-0171 [contraception] Why withdrawal often fails: Withdrawal fails because it needs perfect timing and self-control at the most exciting moment. Many men cannot always feel when they are about to ejaculate, or pull out too late. Semen near the vulva can also enter the vagina. Young or less experienced men may find it especially hard. Using condoms or another method is safer.
  B CSA-0172 [contraception] Who withdrawal may not suit: Withdrawal may not suit men who ejaculate quickly or cannot tell when they are about to ejaculate, young men who are new to sex, or couples where pregnancy would be a serious problem. It also does not suit anyone at risk of HIV or STIs. In these cases, condoms or a more effective method are a better choice.

0.72
  A PUB-0017 [puberty] Being short for your age: Being shorter than friends is very common during puberty, especially if you start later. Many late starters catch up and keep growing after others have stopped. Height also runs in families. Being short does not make you less healthy or less valuable. If you are much shorter than others your age, or seem to have stopped growing, a health worker can check your growth and nutrition.
  B PUB-0018 [puberty] Being very tall for your age: Some young people grow tall early and stand above everyone else. This is usually normal and often runs in families. Girls in particular may be taller than boys for a while because they start their growth spurt earlier. Being tall is nothing to be ashamed of. Stand up straight and be proud. If growth seems extremely fast or unusual, a health worker can check.

0.72
  A HIV-0093 [hiv] Who PrEP is for: PrEP is medicine taken by HIV-negative people to prevent HIV. It is for people at higher risk, for example someone whose partner has HIV and is not virally suppressed, someone with several partners or who often has sex without condoms, someone who recently had an STI, or someone who sells sex or injects drugs. A health worker will talk with you to see if PrEP is right for you.
  B HIV-0106 [hiv] Stigma about taking PrEP: Taking PrEP is a responsible choice to protect your health. It does not mean you have HIV or that you are a bad person. Some people may misunderstand PrEP because the pills look like HIV treatment. You decide whom to tell. If you face judgement, a counsellor or peer educator can support you. Your health is what matters.

0.72
  A CSA-0065 [contraception] When to start the combined pill: You can start the combined pill any day of the month if it is reasonably sure you are not pregnant. If you start within 5 days after your period began, you are protected straight away. If you start at another time, use condoms or avoid sex for the first 7 days. A health worker will check you can use it safely and explain exactly how to begin.
  B CSA-0119 [contraception] Starting the progestin-only pill: You can start progestin-only pills on any day if it is reasonably sure you are not pregnant. If you start within 5 days after your period begins, you are protected at once. At other times, use condoms or avoid sex for the first 2 days. Breastfeeding mothers will get specific advice from their health worker on when to start.

0.72
  A CON-0085 [consent_safety] What is gender-based violence: Gender-based violence (GBV) is harm done to someone because of their gender, or because of unequal power between women and men. It includes physical, sexual, emotional and economic violence. It can happen at home, in relationships, at school, at work or online. Women and girls are affected most, but men and boys can also be victims. GBV is never acceptable and never the victim's fault. In Rwanda, help is available on 3512 (GBV line), 3029 (Isange), 116 and 112.
  B CON-0086 [consent_safety] Main types of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence has four main types. Physical violence: hitting, slapping, kicking, burning or using weapons. Sexual violence: rape, unwanted touching, forced sex, sexual harassment and exploitation. Emotional or psychological violence: insults, threats, humiliation, control and isolation. Economic violence: taking someone's money, stopping them from working or studying, or refusing basic needs. A person may experience more than one type. All are harmful. Help: 3512 or Isange (3029).

0.72
  A CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.
  B CSA-0086 [contraception] The pill and STI protection: No. Combined and progestin-only pills only protect against pregnancy. They give no protection against HIV, chlamydia, gonorrhoea, syphilis or other STIs. If there is any chance of infection, also use a condom every time you have sex. Using the pill together with condoms is called dual protection.

0.71
  A MEN-0004 [menstruation] What ovulation is: Ovulation is when one of your ovaries releases a mature egg. The egg travels down a tube towards the womb. This usually happens once in each cycle, often about two weeks before your next period starts, but the exact day differs between people and from month to month. The egg lives for about one day. Ovulation is a normal sign that your reproductive system is working, and it is the time when pregnancy is most possible if there is sex.
  B PRA-0010 [pregnancy] When ovulation happens in the cycle: Ovulation usually happens about 14 days before the next period starts, not 14 days after the last one. In a 28-day cycle, that is around day 14, counting from the first day of bleeding. But cycles differ from person to person and month to month, especially in teenagers. Stress, illness and travel can move ovulation earlier or later, so you cannot know the exact day for sure.

0.71
  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.

0.71
  A STI-0019 [stis] What is syphilis: Syphilis is a sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It develops in stages. It often starts with a painless sore, then may cause a rash, and then can hide in the body for years without signs. If it is never treated, it can later damage the brain, nerves, eyes and heart. Syphilis can be found with a simple blood test and cured with the right medicine from a health worker.
  B STI-0020 [stis] How syphilis spreads: Syphilis spreads mainly through vaginal, anal or oral sex, when skin or mucous membranes touch a syphilis sore or rash. The sores can be hidden inside the vagina, anus or mouth, so a person may not know they have one. A pregnant woman can also pass syphilis to her baby. It does not spread through toilet seats, sharing clothes or eating together. Condoms lower the risk when they cover the sore area.

0.71
  A CSA-0031 [contraception] Condoms and protection from HIV: Yes. Male and female condoms, used correctly every time, are very good at preventing HIV because they stop semen, vaginal fluids and blood from passing between partners. They protect against HIV during vaginal and anal sex. To be protected, use a condom for the whole act of sex, every time. You can also ask a health center about HIV testing and other ways to prevent HIV.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.71
  A CSA-0114 [contraception] Taking progestin-only pills on time: Progestin-only pills must be taken at the same time every day, with no breaks between packs. For women who are not breastfeeding, taking a pill more than 3 hours late can reduce protection. Set a daily alarm and keep the pack where you see it. Breastfeeding women have a little more protection, but should still be on time.
  B CSA-0119 [contraception] Starting the progestin-only pill: You can start progestin-only pills on any day if it is reasonably sure you are not pregnant. If you start within 5 days after your period begins, you are protected at once. At other times, use condoms or avoid sex for the first 2 days. Breastfeeding mothers will get specific advice from their health worker on when to start.

0.71
  A CSA-0132 [contraception] Where to get EC in Rwanda: In Rwanda, emergency contraceptive pills are available at health centers, youth corners and many pharmacies. Go as soon as possible after unprotected sex. A health center can also tell you about the copper IUD as emergency contraception. If you were forced to have sex, Isange One Stop Centre (3029) gives EC and other care for free. You can call 114 for advice.
  B HIV-0115 [hiv] Where to get PEP in Rwanda: You can get PEP at health centers and hospitals in Rwanda. If it is night or a weekend, go to a hospital emergency service or the nearest facility that is open. After sexual assault, go to an Isange One Stop Centre or call 3029. Tell the staff you need PEP and what happened. You can also call 114 for information.

0.71
  A CSA-0071 [contraception] Missed several combined pills: Missing several pills, or starting a pack late, raises the chance of pregnancy. Take a pill as soon as you remember and continue the pack, then use condoms or avoid sex for the next 7 days. Read your pack leaflet carefully. If you had unprotected sex recently, talk to a pharmacist or nurse quickly about emergency contraception. You can call 114 for free advice.
  B CSA-0115 [contraception] Late or missed progestin-only pill: If you are more than 3 hours late or miss a progestin-only pill, take one as soon as you remember and keep taking them at the usual time. Use condoms or avoid sex for the next 2 days. If you had sex in the last few days, ask a pharmacist or nurse about emergency contraception. Your pack leaflet also explains what to do.

0.71
  A CON-0128 [consent_safety] The 116 child helpline: 116 is Rwanda's free child helpline. Children and young people, or adults worried about a child, can call to report abuse, neglect, exploitation or child marriage, or to get advice and support. You do not need airtime to call. You can call if you are scared, if something happened to a friend or sibling, or if you just need to talk to someone about a worry. In immediate danger, call 112.
  B SRV-0086 [services_rights] What the 116 line is for: 116 is Rwanda's free child helpline. Children and young people, or adults worried about a child, can call it to report abuse, neglect, violence or any situation where a child is not safe, and to get advice and support. You can call 116 for yourself or for another child. If someone is in danger right now, call 112.

0.71
  A PUB-0041 [puberty] When a boy's voice changes: A boy's voice usually starts to get deeper sometime between about 12 and 15, but it can be earlier or later. This happens because testosterone makes the voice box and vocal cords grow longer and thicker. The change can take months to finish. Every boy's timing is different, so if your voice has not changed yet while your friends' have, give it time.
  B PUB-0043 [puberty] Do girls' voices change: Yes, girls' voices also change during puberty, but much less than boys'. A girl's voice box grows a little, so her voice may become slightly lower and fuller. Most people do not notice a big difference. Girls do not usually get the sudden cracking that many boys have. Every voice is different, and a deeper or higher voice in a girl is normal.

0.71
  A CSA-0032 [contraception] STIs that condoms protect against less well: Condoms protect very well against infections spread through fluids, like HIV, gonorrhoea and chlamydia. They give less protection against infections spread by skin-to-skin contact, such as herpes, genital warts (HPV) and syphilis sores, if the sore or wart is in an area the condom does not cover. Condoms still lower the risk a lot. If you see sores or warts, avoid sex and visit a health center.
  B HIV-0041 [hiv] How well condoms protect against HIV: When used correctly every time, from start to finish, male and female condoms are very effective at preventing HIV and many other STIs, and they also prevent pregnancy. Most failures happen because condoms are not used every time, are put on late, are torn by nails or teeth, or are used with oil-based lubricants. Check the expiry date, open the packet carefully, and use a new condom for each act of sex. Free condoms are available at many health centers.

0.71
  A SRV-0086 [services_rights] What the 116 line is for: 116 is Rwanda's free child helpline. Children and young people, or adults worried about a child, can call it to report abuse, neglect, violence or any situation where a child is not safe, and to get advice and support. You can call 116 for yourself or for another child. If someone is in danger right now, call 112.
  B SRV-0089 [services_rights] What the 112 line is for: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police help. Call it if you or someone else is in danger right now, for example if someone is being attacked, threatened or hurt, or if there is a serious emergency. Tell them clearly where you are and what is happening. For medical emergencies you can also call 912 for an ambulance.

0.71
  A CSA-0031 [contraception] Condoms and protection from HIV: Yes. Male and female condoms, used correctly every time, are very good at preventing HIV because they stop semen, vaginal fluids and blood from passing between partners. They protect against HIV during vaginal and anal sex. To be protected, use a condom for the whole act of sex, every time. You can also ask a health center about HIV testing and other ways to prevent HIV.
  B STI-0047 [stis] Condoms and HPV: Condoms lower the chance of getting HPV and genital warts, but they do not give full protection, because HPV can live on skin that a condom does not cover, like the scrotum, vulva or thighs. Using condoms every time is still very worth it, because they also protect against many other STIs, HIV and pregnancy. Combining condoms with the HPV vaccine and fewer partners gives the best protection.

0.71
  A STI-0070 [stis] How hepatitis B spreads: Hepatitis B spreads through blood, semen and vaginal fluids. This can happen during sex without a condom, sharing razors, toothbrushes or needles, unsterile tattoos or piercing, and from a mother to her baby at birth. It does not spread through hugging, kissing on the cheek, sharing food or cups, coughing or breastfeeding. The vaccine, condoms and not sharing sharp objects protect you.
  B STI-0079 [stis] How hepatitis C spreads: Hepatitis C spreads mostly through blood, for example by sharing needles or syringes, unsterile medical, tattoo or piercing tools, sharing razors or toothbrushes, and from mother to baby. It can spread through sex, but this is less common, with higher risk during sex that may cause bleeding, such as anal sex. It does not spread by hugging, kissing, sharing food or breastfeeding.

0.71
  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PU-001 [puberty] What is puberty: Puberty is the time when your body changes from a child's body to an adult's body. Your body, your feelings and your social life all change. It is normal and healthy. It does not start at the same age for everyone, and girls usually start earlier than boys.

0.71
  A MEN-0099 [menstruation] Pain medicine for period cramps: Common pain medicines sold in pharmacies, such as ibuprofen or paracetamol, can ease period cramps for many people. Ask a pharmacist or health worker which one is right for you and how much to take for your age and weight, and always follow the instructions on the packet. Some work best taken when pain first starts, with food. Do not take medicines prescribed for someone else. If pain medicine does not help, see a health worker.
  B MEN-0101 [menstruation] Herbal and traditional remedies for cramps: Many families have traditional remedies for period pain. Simple things like warm ginger or lemon tea are generally safe. Be careful with unknown herbal mixtures, roots, or medicines from traditional healers, because the strength and safety are not known and some can harm your liver, kidneys or womb. Never put herbs inside your vagina. If you want to try a remedy, ask a health worker first, especially if your pain is severe.

0.71
  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B HIV-0055 [hiv] Is HIV testing free in Rwanda: HIV testing is offered free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay to know your status. If anyone asks you for money for an HIV test at a public facility, you can ask the health center management or call 114 for information. Private clinics may have their own fees, so ask before testing there.

0.71
  A STI-0039 [stis] What is HPV: HPV (human papillomavirus) is a very common virus passed through skin-to-skin sexual contact. Most sexually active people get it at some point, and most never know because it causes no signs and the body clears it by itself. Some types of HPV cause genital warts. Other types, if they stay for many years, can cause cancer, especially cancer of the cervix. Vaccination and screening protect against these problems.
  B S-007 [stis] HPV vaccine: The HPV vaccine protects against the virus that can cause cervical cancer. Ask your school nurse or health center about the vaccine and who can get it.

0.71
  A MEN-0134 [menstruation] Drinking enough water during a period: Drinking enough clean water during your period helps with bloating, headaches, tiredness and constipation. Some girls drink less so they do not have to visit the toilet at school, but this can make you feel worse. Carry a bottle of clean water, and include fruits and soups. Your urine should be pale yellow; dark urine means you need to drink more.
  B MEN-0190 [menstruation] Drinking cold water during a period: Drinking cold water or cold drinks during your period is safe. It does not cause cramps, block your period, or cause infertility, despite what some people say. What matters is drinking enough fluids. Some people find warm drinks more soothing when they have cramps, so choose whatever feels best for you.

0.71
  A CSA-0133 [contraception] Period after taking EC: After emergency pills, your next period may come on time, a few days early or a few days late. It may also be a bit lighter or heavier than usual. This is normal. If your period is more than a week late, take a pregnancy test at a pharmacy or health center.
  B CSA-0135 [contraception] Spotting or bleeding after EC: Some people have light spotting or bleeding a few days after taking emergency pills. This is a common side effect and is not harmful, but it is not your period and does not prove the pill worked. Wait for your normal period. If it is more than a week late, take a pregnancy test. If bleeding is heavy or painful, see a health worker.

0.71
  A CSA-0017 [contraception] Getting a condom out of the vagina: A condom cannot get lost inside the body, because the vagina ends at the cervix. Wash your hands, relax, squat or put one leg up, and gently reach in with your fingers to pull it out. Do not use sharp objects. If you cannot reach it, go to a health center where a nurse can remove it. Because semen may have leaked, think about emergency contraception and ask about HIV prevention.
  B CSA-0051 [contraception] How to remove a female condom: After sex, and before standing up, hold the outer ring and twist it gently to close the condom and keep the semen inside. Then pull it out slowly. Wrap it in paper or a bag and put it in a bin or pit latrine, not a flush toilet. Use a new female condom every time you have sex.

0.71
  A HIV-0058 [hiv] What an HIV self-test is: An HIV self-test lets you test yourself privately, using a kit with either a mouth swab (oral fluid) or a finger-prick blood drop. You do the test yourself and read the result in a short time, following the instructions.
