It is a screening test: a reactive result must be confirmed at a health facility, and a non-reactive result may need repeating if you had a recent risk. It is for people 16 and older in Rwanda.
  B HIV-0059 [hiv] How to get an HIV self-test kit in Rwanda: If you are 16 or older, you can ask for an HIV self-test kit at a health facility, and some places such as youth-friendly services may also offer them. Availability can differ from place to place. To find out where kits are available near you, ask a nurse at your health center or a community health worker, or call the free line 114.

0.71
  A STI-0010 [stis] What is gonorrhoea: Gonorrhoea (sometimes spelled gonorrhea) is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It can infect the penis, vagina, cervix, anus, throat and sometimes the eyes. Some people notice discharge or pain when urinating, but many, especially girls, have no signs. Gonorrhoea can be cured with the right medicine from a health worker, but it is becoming harder to treat because some bacteria resist medicines. Get tested at a health center if you are worried.
  B STI-0016 [stis] Gonorrhoea complications: Untreated gonorrhoea can cause serious problems. In girls and women, it can spread to the womb and tubes (PID), causing long-term pain, trouble getting pregnant, or a pregnancy outside the womb. In boys and men, it can cause painful swelling of the testicles and, rarely, affect fertility. Rarely, it spreads to the blood and joints. It also makes HIV easier to catch. Early testing and treatment prevent these problems.

0.71
  A STI-0099 [stis] What causes bacterial vaginosis: The exact cause of bacterial vaginosis is not fully known. It happens when the healthy bacteria in the vagina decrease and other bacteria increase. Things that make it more likely include washing or douching inside the vagina, using scented soaps, having a new sex partner or several partners, and not using condoms. Girls who have never had sex can also get it.
  B STI-0100 [stis] Is bacterial vaginosis an STI: Bacterial vaginosis is not counted as a typical STI, and it can happen to girls who have never had sex. But it is linked to sex: it is more common with new or multiple partners, and condoms can lower the risk. Having BV can also make it easier to catch STIs, including HIV. Male partners usually do not need treatment, but a health worker will advise you.

0.71
  A WEL-0099 [wellbeing] Pain during sex for women: Sex should not be painful. Pain can happen because of nervousness, not enough arousal or wetness, infection, dryness, injury, or other conditions that need checking. Pain deep in the belly with fever or unusual discharge may be an infection. Do not ignore it or keep going through pain. Stop, talk with your partner, and see a nurse or health worker at the health center so they can find the cause and help.
  B WEL-0100 [wellbeing] Genital pain during or after sex for men: Pain in the penis, testicles or lower belly during or after sex or ejaculation is not normal if it keeps happening. Causes can include infections, including STIs, problems with the foreskin, injury or prostate inflammation. Burning when urinating or discharge from the penis are signs to get checked soon. See a health worker at the health center; most causes can be treated. Use condoms until you are checked.

0.71
  A CON-0067 [consent_safety] Warning signs of grooming: Signs that someone may be grooming a young person include: giving special gifts, money or phone credit; wanting time alone with them; asking them to keep secrets; telling them they are 'mature' or 'special'; talking about sex or bodies; testing boundaries with small touches; and trying to separate them from parents or friends. Any one sign may be innocent, but several together are a red flag. A young person noticing this can tell a trusted adult or call 116.
  B CON-0071 [consent_safety] What to do if you notice grooming: If you notice an adult behaving in a grooming way toward a child or friend, such as secret gifts, private messages or trying to be alone with them, take it seriously. Do not confront the adult yourself if it could be unsafe. Gently talk to the young person, and tell a trusted adult such as a parent, teacher or school focal person. You can also call 116 (child helpline) or 3512 for advice. Speaking up early can prevent abuse.

0.71
  A WEL-0114 [wellbeing] No bleeding after first sex: Many girls and women do not bleed the first time they have sex. The hymen is a thin, stretchy tissue that differs from person to person, and it can stretch through sport, daily activity or simply be naturally open. No bleeding does not mean someone has had sex before. Bleeding is not a test of virginity, and nobody can tell your sexual history by looking at your body.
  B WEL-0116 [wellbeing] Bleeding after sex: Bleeding after sex that is not your first time, or that happens again and again, should be checked. It can be caused by dryness, small tears, infections including STIs, changes on the cervix, or bleeding linked to contraception. Most causes can be treated, but some need early care. Please see a nurse or health worker at the health center, especially if you also have pain, unusual discharge or bleeding between periods.

0.71
  A CSB-0113 [contraception] Contraception at a young age and future fertility: Using contraception as a teenager or young adult does not harm your ability to have children later. WHO says young people can safely use almost all methods, including implants, injections and IUDs. In fact, preventing early pregnancy and STIs protects your health and future fertility. When you are ready, your fertility will return after stopping.
  B C-003 [contraception] Are contraceptives safe for young people: Yes. Young people can safely use any contraceptive method, and age alone is not a medical reason to refuse a method. Contraceptives are not only for married people. A health worker can explain what to expect, including common side effects.

0.71
  A STI-0039 [stis] What is HPV: HPV (human papillomavirus) is a very common virus passed through skin-to-skin sexual contact. Most sexually active people get it at some point, and most never know because it causes no signs and the body clears it by itself. Some types of HPV cause genital warts. Other types, if they stay for many years, can cause cancer, especially cancer of the cervix. Vaccination and screening protect against these problems.
  B STI-0040 [stis] How HPV spreads: HPV spreads through skin-to-skin contact of the genitals, anus or mouth, usually during vaginal, anal or oral sex, and sometimes through close genital touching without penetration. It can pass even when the person has no warts or signs. HPV is so common that most people who have sex will meet it. Condoms lower the risk but do not cover all skin. The HPV vaccine gives strong protection against the most dangerous types.

0.71
  A CON-0046 [consent_safety] What is child sexual abuse: Child sexual abuse is when an adult, or an older or more powerful young person, involves a child (anyone under 18) in sexual activity. A child cannot consent to sex, so it is always abuse, even if the child seemed to agree or did not resist. It includes touching, rape, showing sexual images, making sexual photos or videos, and sexual talk. It is a serious crime in Rwanda. The child is never to blame. Report it to 116, 3512, Isange (3029) or police on 112.
  B CON-0059 [consent_safety] Sexual abuse between children: Sometimes an older or stronger child forces, tricks or pressures a younger child into sexual acts. Curious play between children of similar age is different from this. Warning signs of harmful behaviour include a big age or size difference, force, threats, secrecy or acts copying adult sex. The child who was harmed needs protection and support, and the child who caused harm also needs help from adults. Parents, teachers or 116 can guide what to do.

0.71
  A CSA-0004 [contraception] Checking a condom's expiry date: Every condom packet has an expiry date, and sometimes a manufacturing date, printed on the wrapper or the box. Look for words like EXP or 'use by'. If the date has passed, the rubber may be weak and break more easily, so do not use it. When you get condoms from a shop, pharmacy or health center, check the date before you leave, and check it again before you use one.
  B CSA-0005 [contraception] Using an expired condom: It is not a good idea to use an expired condom. Old rubber can become dry and weak, so it is more likely to tear and will not protect you well from pregnancy, HIV or other infections. If the only condom you have is expired, using it is still better than using nothing, but get new ones as soon as you can. Free condoms are available at health centers and youth corners.

0.71
  A SRV-0092 [services_rights] What happens when you call a helpline: When you call a helpline, a trained person will answer, listen and ask a few questions to understand what is happening and how they can help. They may give advice, tell you where to get services, or send help if you are in danger. You can take your time, cry or pause. Share what you feel comfortable sharing, but give your location if you need urgent help.
  B SRV-0096 [services_rights] Feeling nervous to call a helpline: It is normal to feel nervous before calling a helpline. The people who answer are trained to listen kindly and they talk to many young people. You can start with something simple, like: I need some advice, or I am worried about something. You can write down what you want to say first, or ask a trusted friend to sit with you. Making the call is a brave step.

0.71
  A CSB-0068 [contraception] IUD and infection: facts: The IUD itself does not cause infection. There is a small risk of pelvic infection in the first few weeks after insertion, mainly if a person already has an STI like gonorrhoea or chlamydia. That is why a provider asks questions and may check for STIs. After that, risk is very low. Using condoms protects against STIs. Fever, belly pain or bad-smelling discharge need a health worker quickly.
  B CSB-0074 [contraception] Sex with an IUD: You can have sex normally with an IUD. It does not get in the way and usually cannot be felt by either partner. Sex will not move it out of place. You can have sex as soon as you feel comfortable after insertion. Remember, the IUD does not protect against STIs or HIV, so use condoms too if there is any risk.

0.71
  A MEN-0039 [menstruation] Does the period come on the same date every month: Your period does not usually come on the same calendar date each month. It follows your cycle length, not the calendar. For example, if your cycle is 28 days and your period started on 3 March, the next one may start around 31 March. Many cycles also change by a few days from month to month, which is normal. Marking each start day helps you learn your own pattern.
  B MEN-0040 [menstruation] Why the period comes earlier each calendar month: If your cycle is shorter than the number of days in a month, your period will start a little earlier in the month each time. For example, with a 28-day cycle, a period that started on 10 June may start around 8 July and then about 5 August. This is normal and does not mean your periods are coming too often. It is simply how cycle days fit into calendar months.

0.71
  A CSA-0030 [contraception] How effective male condoms are: When male condoms are used correctly every time, about 2 out of 100 women become pregnant in a year. In real life, when people sometimes forget or make mistakes, about 13 out of 100 become pregnant. So condoms work very well, but only if you use one correctly from start to finish every time. They are also the only method that protects against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs together.
  B C-004 [contraception] Male condoms: Condoms are worn on the penis and block sperm and infections. They are the only method that protects against both pregnancy and STIs, including HIV. Used correctly every time, about 2 in 100 women get pregnant in a year; as commonly used, about 13 in 100. Use a new condom each time and talk about it with your partner before sex. Using two condoms at once does not protect more and can make them break.

0.71
  A STI-0024 [stis] Testing for syphilis: Syphilis is usually found with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick rapid test that gives results the same day. Some rapid tests check for HIV and syphilis together. A health worker may also look at any sores. Because some syphilis tests can stay positive even after past treatment, the health worker will explain what your result means. Ask for a syphilis test at your nearest health center or youth corner.
  B STI-0028 [stis] Follow-up after syphilis treatment: After treatment for syphilis, a health worker may ask you to come back for blood tests over the following months to make sure the treatment worked. Some syphilis tests stay positive for a long time, even for life, after the infection is cured, so a positive rapid test does not always mean a new infection. Keep your follow-up visits, avoid sex until told it is safe, and ask the nurse to explain your results.

0.71
  A SRV-0023 [services_rights] What a community health worker is: A community health worker, often called an abajyanama b'ubuzima, is a person chosen by the community and trained to help with health in the village. They give health advice, treat some common illnesses in children, follow up pregnant women, share family planning information and help people get to the health centre when needed. They live near you, so they are easy to reach. They work closely with the health centre.
  B WEL-0068 [wellbeing] Community health workers and emotional support: Community health workers (abajyanama b'ubuzima) live in your village or cell and are trained to help with many health issues. You can talk to them if you feel stressed or worried or need information about sexual and reproductive health. They can listen, give basic advice and guide you to the health center when needed. They are expected to treat what you share with respect and privacy.

0.71
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0003 [puberty] Puberty starting very early: If a girl shows breast growth or pubic hair before about age 8, or a boy shows genital growth, pubic hair or a deep voice before about age 9, this is called early puberty. Sometimes it is just the child's natural timing, but sometimes there is a medical cause that can be treated. Please take the child to a health center so a nurse or doctor can check. It is not the child's fault.

0.71
  A STI-0074 [stis] Is hepatitis B curable: When adults get hepatitis B, most clear the virus on their own within about six months. If it stays longer, it is called chronic hepatitis B. There is no complete cure for chronic hepatitis B yet, but medicine can control the virus, protect the liver and help people live long, healthy lives. Not everyone needs medicine; a health worker decides based on tests. Regular check-ups are important.
  B STI-0077 [stis] Living with hepatitis B: People with hepatitis B can live full lives. Go to regular check-ups, take any medicine exactly as advised, and avoid alcohol, which harms the liver. Do not take herbal or other remedies without asking a health worker, as some damage the liver. Protect others by using condoms, not sharing razors or toothbrushes, and covering cuts. Encourage partners and family to get tested and vaccinated. You can still eat, study and play with others.

0.71
  A HIV-0043 [hiv] Male circumcision and HIV: Voluntary medical male circumcision, done by a trained health worker, lowers a man's chance of getting HIV from vaginal sex with a woman by about half or more. But it does not give full protection, and it does not protect his partner. Circumcised men still need to use condoms and test for HIV. In Rwanda, safe circumcision is offered at health facilities. Traditional or unsafe circumcision with shared tools can spread infections.
  B PUB-0148 [puberty] Health benefits of male circumcision: Besides lowering a man's risk of getting HIV from vaginal sex, medical circumcision makes it easier to keep the penis clean and lowers the risk of some other infections, such as some STIs and infections under the foreskin. It does not give full protection against HIV or other STIs, so condoms and testing are still needed. The choice to be circumcised should be voluntary.

0.71
  A PRA-0023 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex right after your period: Yes, you can get pregnant from sex right after your period ends. Some people, especially those with short or irregular cycles, ovulate only a few days after bleeding stops. Sperm can live up to about 5 days and wait for the egg. So the days just after a period are not safe days. If you had unprotected sex, see a health worker if you are worried.
  B PRA-0024 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex just before your period: Pregnancy from sex in the few days just before a period is less likely, because ovulation has usually already happened. But it is not impossible. Many young people cannot know exactly when their next period will come, and ovulation can happen later than expected. What you think is the week before your period might actually be your fertile time. So these days are not guaranteed safe.

0.71
  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PUB-0003 [puberty] Puberty starting very early: If a girl shows breast growth or pubic hair before about age 8, or a boy shows genital growth, pubic hair or a deep voice before about age 9, this is called early puberty. Sometimes it is just the child's natural timing, but sometimes there is a medical cause that can be treated. Please take the child to a health center so a nurse or doctor can check. It is not the child's fault.

0.71
  A PRA-0022 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex during your period: Pregnancy from sex during a period is less likely, but it is possible. Sperm can live up to about 5 days inside the body. If your period is short or your cycle is short or irregular, you could ovulate soon after your period ends, and sperm from sex during your period could still be alive. So sex during a period is not a reliable way to avoid pregnancy.
  B PRA-0024 [pregnancy] Pregnancy from sex just before your period: Pregnancy from sex in the few days just before a period is less likely, because ovulation has usually already happened. But it is not impossible. Many young people cannot know exactly when their next period will come, and ovulation can happen later than expected. What you think is the week before your period might actually be your fertile time. So these days are not guaranteed safe.

0.71
  A WEL-0010 [wellbeing] Coping with exam stress: Some exam stress can help you focus, but too much makes it hard to think. Make a simple study plan, break work into small parts and take short breaks. Sleep well, because the brain stores what you learned during sleep. Eat regular meals, drink water and move your body. Avoid comparing yourself with others. Talk to a teacher, school counsellor or family member if you feel overwhelmed. Your worth is more than one exam result.
  B WEL-0018 [wellbeing] Self-care during exams: Looking after yourself during exams helps you perform better. Sleep at least 8 hours, especially the night before a paper. Eat breakfast and regular meals, drink water, and avoid too much coffee or energy drinks. Take a short walk or stretch between study sessions. Spend a little time with friends or doing something you enjoy. Do not use pills or drugs to stay awake; they can harm you and make thinking worse.

0.71
  A CSA-0095 [contraception] Where to get contraceptive pills in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get combined and progestin-only pills at public health centers and health posts, youth corners, and pharmacies. In many areas, community health workers can also give pills to continue a method. A health worker will ask some questions and may check your blood pressure. Call 114, the free RBC line, to find services near you.
  B CSB-0053 [contraception] Where to get the injection in Rwanda: In Rwanda, contraceptive injections are available at public health centers and many health posts. Some areas have trained community health workers who provide family planning services. Many health centers have youth corners where young people can get services in a friendly way. If you are not sure where to go, ask a community health worker or call 114, the free RBC line.

0.71
  A CSB-0083 [contraception] What female sterilisation is: Female sterilisation, also called tubal ligation, is a surgical procedure where a trained doctor blocks or cuts the tubes that carry eggs to the womb, so sperm and egg cannot meet. It is meant to be permanent. It does not remove the womb or ovaries, and periods continue. It is usually chosen by adults who are sure they do not want any more children.
  B CSB-0089 [contraception] Rumour: sterilisation changes periods or womanhood: Female sterilisation does not remove the womb or ovaries. A woman continues to have her normal periods and her body keeps making its natural hormones. It does not make her less of a woman, change her desire for sex, or make her weak or sick. It only stops eggs from meeting sperm.

0.71
  A CSA-0190 [contraception] Conditions for LAM to work: LAM only works when all three conditions are true: your monthly bleeding has not returned since birth; you are fully or nearly fully breastfeeding, often, day and night, without giving other foods or drinks regularly; and your baby is less than 6 months old. If any one of these changes, you need another method straight away.
  B CSA-0192 [contraception] How effective LAM is: When all three LAM conditions are met, it works well: about 2 out of 100 women get pregnant in the first 6 months after birth with typical use, and fewer than 1 out of 100 with correct use. It only works for a short time and does not protect against HIV or STIs, so use condoms if there is any risk.

0.71
  A CSA-0070 [contraception] Forgot one combined pill: If you forget one combined pill, take it as soon as you remember, then carry on with the rest of the pack as usual. Missing just one pill is usually not a big risk. Read the leaflet in your pack for exact advice, and if you are unsure, ask a pharmacist, nurse or call 114.
  B CSA-0071 [contraception] Missed several combined pills: Missing several pills, or starting a pack late, raises the chance of pregnancy. Take a pill as soon as you remember and continue the pack, then use condoms or avoid sex for the next 7 days. Read your pack leaflet carefully. If you had unprotected sex recently, talk to a pharmacist or nurse quickly about emergency contraception. You can call 114 for free advice.

0.71
  A PUB-0125 [puberty] Sudden pain in a testicle: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle or the scrotum, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, is an emergency. It can mean the testicle has twisted and lost its blood supply. Go to a health facility now, or call 912 or 112. Treatment within a few hours can save the testicle. Do not wait to see if it goes away, and do not feel shy; health workers see this often.
  B WEL-0133 [wellbeing] A lump on the testicle: A new lump, hard area or swelling on a testicle should always be checked by a health worker, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as cysts or fluid, but testicular cancer, though rare, is most common in young men and is very treatable when found early. Do not be shy or wait to see if it goes away. The check is quick and private.

0.71
  A STI-0064 [stis] How trichomoniasis spreads: Trichomoniasis spreads mainly during vaginal sex without a condom, and through genital-to-genital contact, with someone who has the infection. It can pass from a man to a woman, a woman to a man, or between women. Because many people have no signs, they can pass it without knowing. It does not spread by hugging or sharing food. Using condoms every time lowers the risk.
  B STI-0067 [stis] Testing and cure for trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis can be found by testing a swab from the vagina or a urine sample, depending on what the health facility has. Sometimes the nurse treats based on signs. Trich is curable with medicine from a health worker. Take all the medicine, avoid alcohol if the nurse tells you to, avoid sex until you and your partner have both finished treatment, and go back if signs return.

0.71
  A CSA-0170 [contraception] How effective withdrawal is: With withdrawal, the man pulls his penis out of the vagina before ejaculating. With typical use about 20 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year, and about 4 out of 100 when it is done correctly every time. It is one of the less effective methods. It costs nothing and is better than no method, but condoms or other methods protect much better.
  B CSA-0172 [contraception] Who withdrawal may not suit: Withdrawal may not suit men who ejaculate quickly or cannot tell when they are about to ejaculate, young men who are new to sex, or couples where pregnancy would be a serious problem. It also does not suit anyone at risk of HIV or STIs. In these cases, condoms or a more effective method are a better choice.

0.71
  A CON-0125 [consent_safety] Calling Isange on 3029: 3029 is the toll-free number for Isange One Stop Centres in Rwanda. Anyone can call to get advice after sexual or gender-based violence or child abuse, to find the nearest Isange centre, or to ask what to do next. You can call for yourself or for someone else. If a person is in immediate danger, calling 112 is fastest. For GBV support you can also call 3512, and children can call 116.
  B SRV-0088 [services_rights] What the 3029 line is for: 3029 is the free number for Isange One Stop Centres, which support people who have experienced sexual or physical violence, including children. By calling, you can get advice and find out where the nearest centre is. At the centre you can receive medical care, counselling and help with legal steps. If the violence happened recently, go as soon as possible, because some treatments work best early.

0.71
  A HIV-0017 [hiv] HIV risk from anal sex: Anal sex without a condom carries the highest risk of passing HIV during sex. The lining of the anus is thin and can tear easily, letting the virus get into the blood. The receptive partner, the one being penetrated, has the highest risk, but the other partner can also get HIV. This is true for any couple, whether man and woman or two men. Using a condom with plenty of water-based lubricant every time lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.

0.71
  A PRA-0063 [pregnancy] Can a boy get pregnant: No. A boy's body does not have a womb or ovaries, so a boy cannot get pregnant. Pregnancy needs an egg and a womb where a baby can grow. Boys produce sperm, which can cause a pregnancy in a girl or woman. So boys share responsibility for preventing unplanned pregnancy.
  B PUB-0138 [puberty] When a boy can make a girl pregnant: Once a boy's body starts making sperm in puberty, he can make a girl pregnant, even if he is young, small, has no beard yet, or has only just had his first ejaculation. Sperm can be present even before he notices semen. So any sex where the penis enters or touches the vagina can lead to pregnancy. Talk to a health worker or call 114 for advice on protection.

0.71
  A STI-0052 [stis] HPV vaccine and boys: Some countries vaccinate boys against HPV too, but in many countries, including Rwanda, programmes focus on girls because cervical cancer causes the most deaths. When most girls are vaccinated, boys are also indirectly protected because less HPV spreads in the community. Boys can protect themselves and partners by using condoms and limiting partners. Ask a health worker if you want to know about vaccine options for boys.
  B S-007 [stis] HPV vaccine: The HPV vaccine protects against the virus that can cause cervical cancer. Ask your school nurse or health center about the vaccine and who can get it.

0.71
  A WEL-0090 [wellbeing] Not getting an erection sometimes: It is normal for young men to sometimes not get an erection or lose it, especially when tired, stressed, nervous, after drinking alcohol, or when not really in the mood. This does not mean something is wrong with your body. Erections come and go naturally. Worrying a lot can make it happen more. If it happens almost every time for several months, it is a good idea to see a health worker.
  B WEL-0093 [wellbeing] When erection problems need a health worker: If you can rarely or never get or keep an erection for a few months, or it happens suddenly with pain or after an injury, see a health worker. Ongoing erection problems can be linked to stress, depression, alcohol, smoking, drugs, some medicines, or health issues like diabetes or heart problems. A health worker can check you privately. Do not buy sex pills or herbs from markets or online; they can be dangerous.

0.71
  A HIV-0021 [hiv] Can a woman pass HIV to a man: Yes. A woman living with HIV who is not on effective treatment can pass HIV to a man during sex without a condom. The virus in vaginal fluids or menstrual blood can enter through the tip of the penis, the foreskin, or small cuts or sores. Some boys wrongly believe only men pass HIV. Condoms protect both partners, and a partner on treatment with an undetectable viral load does not pass HIV through sex.
  B HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.

0.71
  A PUB-0027 [puberty] Uneven breasts: It is very common for one breast to grow faster or end up a little bigger than the other. Many adult women have breasts of slightly different sizes. While you are growing, the difference may be bigger, and it often becomes smaller over time. This is normal and healthy. A soft or padded bra can help if it bothers you. See a health worker if one breast suddenly changes shape or has a lump.
  B WEL-0035 [wellbeing] Worries about breast size: Breasts come in many sizes and shapes, and it is normal for one breast to be slightly bigger than the other. Breasts may keep growing until your late teens. Size does not affect your health, femininity or ability to breastfeed. Wearing a comfortable, well-fitting bra can help you feel better. If you notice a lump, pain that does not go away or very fast changes, see a health worker.

0.71
  A PRB-0043 [pregnancy] Rest and sleep in pregnancy: Your body works hard to grow a baby, so you need more rest than usual. Try to sleep enough at night and rest during the day if you can, even for a short time with your feet up. Feeling tired is common, especially in the first and last months. Ask family or friends to help with chores. If you feel extremely tired, weak or breathless all the time, tell a health worker, as it can be a sign of anaemia.
  B PRB-0062 [pregnancy] Tiredness in pregnancy: Feeling very tired is common in pregnancy, especially in the first three months and the last weeks. Your body is working hard to grow the baby. Rest when you can, sleep enough, eat regular healthy meals, drink water and take your iron and folic acid as advised. Gentle activity can also boost energy. If you are exhausted all the time, breathless or pale, tell a health worker, as you may need a check for anaemia.

0.71
  A STI-0069 [stis] What is hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is an infection of the liver caused by a virus. It can cause a short illness, or it can stay in the body for life (chronic hepatitis B), slowly damaging the liver and sometimes leading to liver cancer. Many people have no signs for years. Hepatitis B can be prevented with a safe and effective vaccine, and chronic infection can be controlled with treatment from a health worker.
  B STI-0074 [stis] Is hepatitis B curable: When adults get hepatitis B, most clear the virus on their own within about six months. If it stays longer, it is called chronic hepatitis B. There is no complete cure for chronic hepatitis B yet, but medicine can control the virus, protect the liver and help people live long, healthy lives. Not everyone needs medicine; a health worker decides based on tests. Regular check-ups are important.

0.71
  A STI-0019 [stis] What is syphilis: Syphilis is a sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It develops in stages. It often starts with a painless sore, then may cause a rash, and then can hide in the body for years without signs. If it is never treated, it can later damage the brain, nerves, eyes and heart. Syphilis can be found with a simple blood test and cured with the right medicine from a health worker.
  B STI-0021 [stis] First stage of syphilis: painless sore: The first sign of syphilis is often a single sore, called a chancre. It usually appears around 3 weeks after infection where the bacteria entered, such as the penis, vagina, anus or mouth. It is usually firm, round and painless, so many people do not notice it. The sore heals on its own in a few weeks, but the infection stays in the body. Any genital sore should be checked at a health center.

0.71
  A CSA-0112 [contraception] Difference between combined and progestin-only pills: Combined pills contain two hormones, estrogen and progestin; progestin-only pills contain only progestin. Progestin-only pills are taken every day with no break, and timing is stricter. They are suitable for breastfeeding women and for some people who cannot use estrogen. Combined pills often make bleeding more regular, while progestin-only pills more often cause irregular bleeding.
  B CSA-0117 [contraception] How effective progestin-only pills are: For women who are not breastfeeding, progestin-only pills work about as well as combined pills: with typical use, about 7 in 100 women get pregnant in a year, and fewer than 1 in 100 with perfect use. For breastfeeding women they are even more effective. Taking every pill on time is the key.

  A CON-0131 [consent_safety] School focal persons and counsellors: Many schools have a teacher or staff member who is a focal person for student welfare, guidance and counselling, or protection from abuse. Students can talk to them about harassment, abuse, pressure for sex, bullying or problems at home. They can listen, help keep the student safe, and connect them to services like Isange, 116 or the police. If the school focal person is part of the problem, a student can go to the head teacher, a parent or call 116.
  B WEL-0063 [wellbeing] What a school counsellor does: A school counsellor, or a teacher or patron/matron trained to support students, is there to listen and help with problems like stress, exam worries, family difficulties, friendships, bullying and feelings about relationships or your body. They can help you find solutions and connect you with health services if needed. Talking to them is not a punishment. If your school has no counsellor, ask a teacher you trust.

0.71
  A HIV-0137 [hiv] What a viral load test is: A viral load test measures how much HIV is in your blood. It shows whether your ART is working. The goal is a very low or undetectable viral load. In Rwanda, people on ART have viral load tests at regular times set by the guidelines. Your health worker will explain your results and what to do if the viral load is high.
  B HIV-0140 [hiv] What a high viral load result means: A high viral load result while on ART means the virus is not fully controlled. The most common reason is missed doses, but other causes include vomiting, other medicines, or resistance. Your health worker will talk with you, offer adherence support, and repeat the test. If it stays high, they may change your medicine. Be honest about any problems so they can help.

0.71
  A CON-0106 [consent_safety] Why people stay in abusive relationships: People stay in abusive relationships for many reasons: fear of more violence, love and hope the partner will change, depending on them for money or a home, children, shame, family or community pressure, threats, or not knowing where to go. Leaving can be dangerous and hard. This is why we should never blame someone for staying. Instead, offer support and information. Help and safety planning are available on 3512 and at Isange (3029).
  B CON-0110 [consent_safety] Leaving an abusive relationship safely: Leaving an abusive partner can be the most dangerous time, so planning helps. A person can tell a trusted friend or relative, keep important documents, some money and a phone ready, know where to go (a relative's home or a safe place), and memorise helpline numbers. They can get support from 3512, Isange (3029) or the police. If in immediate danger, call 112. Leaving is a process, and support makes it safer.

  A CSA-0132 [contraception] Where to get EC in Rwanda: In Rwanda, emergency contraceptive pills are available at health centers, youth corners and many pharmacies. Go as soon as possible after unprotected sex. A health center can also tell you about the copper IUD as emergency contraception. If you were forced to have sex, Isange One Stop Centre (3029) gives EC and other care for free. You can call 114 for advice.
  B CSB-0053 [contraception] Where to get the injection in Rwanda: In Rwanda, contraceptive injections are available at public health centers and many health posts. Some areas have trained community health workers who provide family planning services. Many health centers have youth corners where young people can get services in a friendly way. If you are not sure where to go, ask a community health worker or call 114, the free RBC line.

  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B CSA-0095 [contraception] Where to get contraceptive pills in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get combined and progestin-only pills at public health centers and health posts, youth corners, and pharmacies. In many areas, community health workers can also give pills to continue a method. A health worker will ask some questions and may check your blood pressure. Call 114, the free RBC line, to find services near you.

0.70
  A HIV-0017 [hiv] HIV risk from anal sex: Anal sex without a condom carries the highest risk of passing HIV during sex. The lining of the anus is thin and can tear easily, letting the virus get into the blood. The receptive partner, the one being penetrated, has the highest risk, but the other partner can also get HIV. This is true for any couple, whether man and woman or two men. Using a condom with plenty of water-based lubricant every time lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0022 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between women: The risk of HIV passing between two women during sex is very low, but it is possible if blood, such as menstrual blood or blood from cuts, or vaginal fluids pass between partners, or if sex toys are shared without cleaning or a new condom. Women who have sex with women can also have male partners or other risks. Using barriers, not sharing toys, and regular testing help keep you healthy.

0.70
  A STI-0078 [stis] What is hepatitis C: Hepatitis C is a liver infection caused by a virus. Many people have no signs for years, while the virus slowly damages the liver, sometimes leading to scarring or liver cancer. Unlike hepatitis B, there is no vaccine for hepatitis C. The good news is that today's medicines can cure most people in a few months. A blood test at a health center can show whether you have it.
  B STI-0081 [stis] Is hepatitis C curable: Yes. Modern medicines taken by mouth for about two to three months can cure more than 9 out of 10 people with hepatitis C. Treatment must be prescribed and followed up by a health worker. Rwanda has worked to make hepatitis C testing and treatment widely available. Being cured does not protect you from getting it again, so keep avoiding shared needles, razors and unsterile tools.

0.70
  A CON-0085 [consent_safety] What is gender-based violence: Gender-based violence (GBV) is harm done to someone because of their gender, or because of unequal power between women and men. It includes physical, sexual, emotional and economic violence. It can happen at home, in relationships, at school, at work or online. Women and girls are affected most, but men and boys can also be victims. GBV is never acceptable and never the victim's fault. In Rwanda, help is available on 3512 (GBV line), 3029 (Isange), 116 and 112.
  B CON-0095 [consent_safety] Health effects of gender-based violence: Gender-based violence can harm health in many ways: injuries, chronic pain, unwanted pregnancy, HIV and other infections, problems during pregnancy, depression, anxiety, trouble sleeping, alcohol or drug use, and thoughts of self-harm. Children who experience or see violence may struggle at school and with feelings. Getting care early protects health. Isange One Stop Centres (3029) and health centres offer medical and psychosocial care, and 3512 can guide survivors.

  A SRV-0051 [services_rights] When the health worker knows your family: In small communities, the nurse may know your parents or neighbours. Health workers must still keep your information private, whoever they know. If you are worried, you can tell them: I am worried because you know my family, can I trust that this stays private? If you still feel uncomfortable, you can ask to see a different provider, call 114, or visit another facility.
  B SRV-0059 [services_rights] When a health worker shares your information: Health workers must keep your health information private. If you find out that a nurse, doctor or other staff member told people about your visit, test results or problem without your permission, that is wrong. You can talk to the person in charge of the facility, with a trusted adult if you like. It is not your fault, and you still have the right to go on getting care.

0.70
  A CSA-0115 [contraception] Late or missed progestin-only pill: If you are more than 3 hours late or miss a progestin-only pill, take one as soon as you remember and keep taking them at the usual time. Use condoms or avoid sex for the next 2 days. If you had sex in the last few days, ask a pharmacist or nurse about emergency contraception. Your pack leaflet also explains what to do.
  B CSA-0145 [contraception] EC after missed regular pills: If you missed several combined pills, or were late with a progestin-only pill, and had unprotected sex, emergency contraception may be needed. Talk to a pharmacist or health worker quickly, as it depends on how many pills you missed and when. Keep taking your regular pills, and use condoms until you are protected again.

0.70
  A HIV-0068 [hiv] What a negative HIV test result means: A negative HIV test result means no HIV was found in your test. If you had a possible exposure recently, the test may be too early to show it, so the health worker may ask you to test again later. A negative result is a chance to keep protecting yourself: use condoms, limit partners, test regularly, and ask about PrEP if you are at high risk.
  B HIV-0069 [hiv] What a positive HIV test result means: A positive HIV result, after confirmation, means you have HIV. It does not mean you have AIDS or that you will die soon. Today, free treatment in Rwanda lets people with HIV live long, healthy lives, study, work, love and have children. You can start treatment right away. The counsellor will support you, and you are not alone. Take time to breathe and ask every question you have.

0.70
  A CSA-0096 [contraception] The pill and alcohol: Alcohol does not stop the pill from working. But drinking a lot can make you forget to take your pill, and if you vomit soon after taking it, it may not be absorbed, which is like missing a pill. Drinking also makes it more likely you will forget condoms. Keep your pill routine and check the leaflet if you vomit.
  B CSA-0158 [contraception] EC and alcohol: Drinking alcohol does not stop emergency contraceptive pills from working. But if you vomit within about 2 hours of taking EC, the pill may not be absorbed, so contact a pharmacist or health worker. Alcohol can also lead to unplanned sex without condoms, so plan protection before you drink.

0.70
  A STI-0064 [stis] How trichomoniasis spreads: Trichomoniasis spreads mainly during vaginal sex without a condom, and through genital-to-genital contact, with someone who has the infection. It can pass from a man to a woman, a woman to a man, or between women. Because many people have no signs, they can pass it without knowing. It does not spread by hugging or sharing food. Using condoms every time lowers the risk.
  B STI-0066 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in boys and men: Most boys and men with trichomoniasis have no signs at all, which is why they can pass it on without knowing. Some notice itching or irritation inside the penis, a thin discharge, or burning after urinating or ejaculating. Signs may come and go. Because other infections cause similar signs, only a health worker can tell the cause. If your partner is diagnosed with trich, you need treatment too.

0.70
  A MEN-0131 [menstruation] Anaemia from heavy periods: Anaemia means your blood has too few red blood cells or too little iron to carry oxygen well. Heavy or long periods are a common cause in girls and young women. Signs include feeling very tired or weak, pale skin, inner eyelids or nails, dizziness, headaches, fast heartbeat and shortness of breath. A simple blood test at a health center can check for it. If you have these signs, see a health worker; they can treat it and look at the cause.
  B PRB-0014 [pregnancy] Anaemia in pregnancy: Anaemia means your blood does not have enough healthy red cells to carry oxygen. It is common in pregnancy. Signs can include feeling very tired, weak, dizzy, short of breath, or having pale eyelids, palms or lips. Anaemia can make bleeding at birth more dangerous and affect the baby's growth. Health workers test for it at antenatal care. Eating iron-rich foods, taking supplements as advised and preventing malaria all help. If you notice these signs, see a health worker.

0.70
  A PRB-0056 [pregnancy] Nausea and vomiting in early pregnancy: Feeling sick or vomiting, often called morning sickness, is very common in the first three months and can happen at any time of day. It usually improves after about 12 to 16 weeks. Eating small amounts often, starting the day with something dry like bread, avoiding strong smells and greasy food, and sipping water between meals can help. Ginger may also help some women. Rest when you can. Ask a health worker before taking any medicine.
  B PRB-0057 [pregnancy] Severe vomiting in pregnancy: Some vomiting is normal in early pregnancy, but go to a health facility quickly if you cannot keep any food or drink down for a day, you pass very little or dark urine, you feel very weak or dizzy, you are losing weight, or you vomit blood. Severe vomiting can cause dehydration and needs treatment from a health worker. It is not your fault and it can be treated.

0.70
  A CSA-0121 [contraception] What emergency contraception is: Emergency contraception (EC) is a way to lower the chance of pregnancy after sex without protection, or when a method failed, for example a condom broke or pills were missed. There are two kinds: emergency contraceptive pills, sometimes called the morning-after pill, and a copper IUD put in by a trained provider. EC works best when used as soon as possible after sex.
  B C-009 [contraception] Emergency contraceptive pills: Emergency pills can help prevent pregnancy after sex without protection, a broken condom or forced sex. Take them as soon as possible, ideally within 3 days. They can still help up to 5 days after. They do not end an existing pregnancy and do not protect against STIs. They are safe to use, even more than once, but a regular method works better. A copper IUD can also be used for emergency contraception. If your period is more than 7 days late afterwards, take a pregnancy test.

0.70
  A MEN-0132 [menstruation] Iron-rich foods in Rwanda: Your body uses iron to make blood, and you lose some iron each period. Good local sources include beans, peas and lentils, dark green leaves like isombe (cassava leaves) and dodo (amaranth), small fish like sambaza, eggs, meat, and liver. Groundnuts and sorghum also help. Try to include some of these foods in your meals every day, especially during and after your period.
  B PRB-0030 [pregnancy] Iron-rich foods in pregnancy: Iron helps your body make blood for you and your baby. Good sources of iron include beans, peas, lentils, dark green leafy vegetables like amaranth (dodo) and cassava leaves, eggs, liver, meat, fish and small dried fish (indagara). Eating fruits with vitamin C, like oranges, passion fruit or tomatoes, at the same meal helps your body absorb iron. Tea or coffee with meals can reduce iron absorption, so have them between meals instead.

0.70
  A STI-0111 [stis] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle is an emergency. It can be testicular torsion, where the testicle twists and loses its blood supply, which can destroy the testicle within hours. Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.
  B WEL-0133 [wellbeing] A lump on the testicle: A new lump, hard area or swelling on a testicle should always be checked by a health worker, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as cysts or fluid, but testicular cancer, though rare, is most common in young men and is very treatable when found early. Do not be shy or wait to see if it goes away. The check is quick and private.

0.70
  A HIV-0107 [hiv] PrEP when your partner has HIV: If your partner has HIV, PrEP can help protect you, especially while your partner is starting treatment or if their viral load is not yet undetectable. When your partner takes treatment well and is undetectable, HIV is not passed through sex. Talk with a health worker together about the best plan, which may include PrEP, condoms, and regular testing.
  B HIV-0111 [hiv] Telling a partner or family about PrEP: Whether you tell anyone about PrEP is your choice. Telling a partner can help you plan protection together and avoid misunderstandings if they see your pills. Telling a trusted family member may give you support to take it daily. Explain that PrEP is a prevention medicine for HIV-negative people. A counsellor can help you plan the conversation.

0.70
  A PRB-0028 [pregnancy] Healthy eating in pregnancy: A healthy pregnancy diet includes a variety of foods every day: staples like sweet potatoes, cassava, maize or rice; beans, peas, groundnuts, eggs, fish or meat for protein; dark green vegetables like dodo (amaranth) and cassava leaves; fruits; and milk if you can. Eat an extra small meal or snack each day. Use iodised salt, limit sugary drinks and very salty food, and drink clean water. Good food helps your baby grow and keeps you strong.
  B PRB-0031 [pregnancy] Food safety in pregnancy: During pregnancy your body is more sensitive to some food infections, which can harm the baby. Wash hands before cooking and eating, wash fruit and vegetables with clean water, cook meat, fish and eggs well until no pink or runny parts remain, and drink boiled or pasteurised milk. Avoid food left uncovered for a long time and drink safe water. These simple steps help prevent food poisoning and infections.

0.70
  A MEN-0036 [menstruation] Feeling angry or irritable before a period: Some girls feel short-tempered, impatient or easily annoyed before their period. This is a common part of PMS and is linked to hormone changes. You are still in charge of how you act, and these tips can help: take a short walk, breathe slowly, get enough sleep, and step away before an argument grows. It can help to explain to people close to you how you feel. If anger feels out of control, talk to a health worker or counsellor.
  B MEN-0037 [menstruation] Very low mood before periods (PMDD): For a small number of people, mood changes before a period are so strong that they make school, home or friendships very hard, with deep sadness, hopelessness, anxiety or anger that lifts once the period starts. This can be a condition sometimes called PMDD, and it can be treated. Please talk to a health worker at your health center or youth corner. If you ever have thoughts of hurting yourself, tell a trusted adult now, call 114, or go to the nearest health facility.

0.70
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B H-004 [hiv] HIV testing: Testing is voluntary and private. In Rwanda, people aged 12 and above can agree to a test themselves, and for children aged 10 to 11 a parent or guardian agrees. A self-test kit is for people aged 16 and above and is sold at health facilities and pharmacies. A positive self-test must be confirmed by a health worker.

0.70
  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B H-004 [hiv] HIV testing: Testing is voluntary and private. In Rwanda, people aged 12 and above can agree to a test themselves, and for children aged 10 to 11 a parent or guardian agrees. A self-test kit is for people aged 16 and above and is sold at health facilities and pharmacies. A positive self-test must be confirmed by a health worker.

0.70
  A STI-0066 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in boys and men: Most boys and men with trichomoniasis have no signs at all, which is why they can pass it on without knowing. Some notice itching or irritation inside the penis, a thin discharge, or burning after urinating or ejaculating. Signs may come and go. Because other infections cause similar signs, only a health worker can tell the cause. If your partner is diagnosed with trich, you need treatment too.
  B STI-0067 [stis] Testing and cure for trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis can be found by testing a swab from the vagina or a urine sample, depending on what the health facility has. Sometimes the nurse treats based on signs. Trich is curable with medicine from a health worker. Take all the medicine, avoid alcohol if the nurse tells you to, avoid sex until you and your partner have both finished treatment, and go back if signs return.

0.70
  A STI-0055 [stis] What is cervical cancer: Cervical cancer is a cancer that grows in the cervix, the lower part of the womb that opens into the vagina. It is one of the most common cancers in women in Africa, and almost all cases are caused by long-lasting HPV infection. It usually develops slowly over many years, which gives time to find and treat early changes. It can be prevented through HPV vaccination and regular screening, and treated if found early.
  B WEL-0137 [wellbeing] Mother with cervical cancer: am I at risk: Cervical cancer is mostly caused by long-lasting infection with HPV, a virus passed through sexual contact, not by genes passed from mother to child. So having a mother with cervical cancer does not mean you will get it. You can protect yourself by getting the HPV vaccine, delaying sex, using condoms, not smoking, and going for cervical screening when you reach the recommended age. Your mother also needs love and support.

0.70
  A CSB-0009 [contraception] Removing an implant before it expires: Yes. You can ask to have your implant removed at any time and for any reason, even if it was put in recently. You do not need to give a long explanation, and a provider should not refuse or delay removal. If you do not want to get pregnant, ask about starting another method on the same day so you stay protected. Your fertility comes back quickly after removal.
  B CSB-0024 [contraception] Replacing an implant with a new one: When your implant is due, a trained provider can remove the old one and insert a new one at the same visit, often through the same small cut. This keeps you protected without a gap. You do not need a break between implants. If the date has already passed, still go; they may check that you are not pregnant before inserting the new one, and advise condoms for a few days.

0.70
  A STI-0102 [stis] What is a urinary tract infection: A urinary tract infection (UTI) is an infection of the parts of the body that make and carry urine, most often the bladder. It is usually caused by bacteria from the bowel getting into the urethra, the tube urine comes out of. UTIs are very common in girls and women because their urethra is short. A UTI is not a sexually transmitted infection, and it can be treated.
  B STI-0104 [stis] Difference between a UTI and an STI: A UTI is an infection of the urinary system, usually caused by bowel bacteria, and it is not passed between partners. An STI is passed through sexual contact and can affect the genitals, throat or anus. Both can cause burning when urinating, which is why they are easily confused. STIs more often cause discharge, sores or itching. A health worker can test to tell them apart, and treatments are different.

0.70
  A MEN-0052 [menstruation] When to replace reusable pads: Good reusable pads can last many months, often around a year or more, depending on how often you use and wash them. Replace a pad when the cloth becomes thin, torn, hard, or no longer absorbs well, or if it keeps a bad smell even after careful washing and sun drying. Having several pads lets each one rest and dry fully, which makes them last longer.
  B MEN-0078 [menstruation] Reusing a disposable pad: Disposable pads are made to be used only once. Washing and reusing them is not safe because they do not get clean inside, they break apart, and they can grow germs that cause infections. If money is tight, reusable cloth pads or a menstrual cup are safer ways to save. You can also ask a teacher, school matron or health worker if they know of support for pads in your area.

0.70
  A CSA-0159 [contraception] What spermicides are: Spermicides are products put deep into the vagina before sex to kill or stop sperm. They come as foam, gel, cream, tablets or film, and the most common one contains nonoxynol-9. Used alone, they are one of the least effective methods. They do not protect against HIV or other STIs.
  B CSA-0160 [contraception] How to use spermicide: Spermicide must be put deep in the vagina, close to the cervix, shortly before sex. Some types work immediately and others need a few minutes to melt, so follow the package instructions on timing. Use more spermicide before each act of sex. Do not wash inside the vagina for several hours after sex.

0.70
  A CSA-0015 [contraception] What to do if a condom breaks: Stay calm. Stop and pull out, and put on a new condom if you continue. To lower the chance of pregnancy, the girl can take emergency contraceptive pills as soon as possible, within 5 days, from a pharmacy or health center. Because of the risk of HIV and other infections, go to a health center as soon as you can, ideally within 72 hours, to ask about HIV prevention medicine and testing. You can call 114 for free advice.
  B HIV-0117 [hiv] PEP after a condom breaks: If a condom breaks or slips off during sex and you do not know your partner's HIV status, or they have HIV and are not virally suppressed, go to a health center as soon as possible, within 72 hours, and ask about PEP. Also ask about emergency contraception and STI testing. Next time, check the expiry date, use water-based lubricant, and put the condom on correctly.

0.70
  A CSA-0123 [contraception] How EC pills work: Emergency contraceptive pills work mainly by stopping or delaying the release of an egg from the ovary. If no egg is released while sperm are still alive in the body, fertilisation cannot happen. They do not work after an egg has been fertilised and they do not end a pregnancy that has already started.
  B CSA-0138 [contraception] Types of EC pills: There are three kinds of emergency contraceptive pills: progestin-only pills containing levonorgestrel (the most common), pills containing ulipristal acetate, and some ordinary combined pills used in a special way under a provider's guidance. Each has its own instructions. A pharmacist or health worker will give you the right one and explain how to take it.

0.70
  A HIV-0058 [hiv] What an HIV self-test is: An HIV self-test lets you test yourself privately, using a kit with either a mouth swab (oral fluid) or a finger-prick blood drop. You do the test yourself and read the result in a short time, following the instructions. It is a screening test: a reactive result must be confirmed at a health facility, and a non-reactive result may need repeating if you had a recent risk. It is for people 16 and older in Rwanda.
  B HIV-0089 [hiv] Giving an HIV self-test kit to a partner: If you are 16 or older and got a self-test kit, you can offer one to your partner, if they are also 16 or older, so they can test privately. This can help partners who are shy about going to a clinic. Never force or trick someone into testing; it must be their choice. If their result is reactive, they should go to a health facility to confirm.

0.70
  A HIV-0055 [hiv] Is HIV testing free in Rwanda: HIV testing is offered free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay to know your status. If anyone asks you for money for an HIV test at a public facility, you can ask the health center management or call 114 for information. Private clinics may have their own fees, so ask before testing there.
  B HIV-0059 [hiv] How to get an HIV self-test kit in Rwanda: If you are 16 or older, you can ask for an HIV self-test kit at a health facility, and some places such as youth-friendly services may also offer them. Availability can differ from place to place. To find out where kits are available near you, ask a nurse at your health center or a community health worker, or call the free line 114.

0.70
  A SRV-0036 [services_rights] Medicines from the street or market: Medicines sold in markets, on the street or by people walking around are not checked and can be fake, expired or the wrong medicine. They may not work, can make you sick and can delay real treatment. This includes pills sold to treat STIs, end a pregnancy or improve sex. Get medicines only from a registered pharmacy or a health facility, and see a health worker first.
  B SRV-0102 [services_rights] Buying SRH medicines online or from social media sellers: Medicines sold through social media, WhatsApp or unknown websites can be fake, expired, the wrong strength or dangerous. You cannot check where they came from, and some sellers take money and send nothing. This is especially risky for medicines claiming to cure STIs, prevent or end pregnancy, or boost sex. Get medicines only from a health facility or registered pharmacy, after talking to a health worker.

0.70
  A CSA-0145 [contraception] EC after missed regular pills: If you missed several combined pills, or were late with a progestin-only pill, and had unprotected sex, emergency contraception may be needed. Talk to a pharmacist or health worker quickly, as it depends on how many pills you missed and when. Keep taking your regular pills, and use condoms until you are protected again.
  B CSA-0151 [contraception] When you need EC: You may need emergency contraception after sex with no method, when a condom broke, slipped or leaked, after missing several pills, being late for an injection, if withdrawal failed, if you were forced to have sex, or if you made a mistake with any method. If you are not sure, ask a pharmacist or health worker quickly, or call 114.

0.70
  A CSA-0112 [contraception] Difference between combined and progestin-only pills: Combined pills contain two hormones, estrogen and progestin; progestin-only pills contain only progestin. Progestin-only pills are taken every day with no break, and timing is stricter. They are suitable for breastfeeding women and for some people who cannot use estrogen. Combined pills often make bleeding more regular, while progestin-only pills more often cause irregular bleeding.
  B CSA-0113 [contraception] Who can use progestin-only pills: Almost all women and girls can use progestin-only pills, including those who are breastfeeding, those who smoke, those with high blood pressure that is controlled, and those who cannot take estrogen. A health worker will ask a few questions to check they are right for you, for example about current breast cancer or certain liver diseases.

0.70
  A MEN-0103 [menstruation] Leg pain during periods: Some girls feel aching or heaviness in their thighs or legs during their period. This happens because cramping pain from the womb can spread along nerves into the legs. Warmth, gentle stretching, walking, raising your legs while resting, and pain relief from a pharmacy can help. See a health worker if one leg is swollen, red, hot or very painful, or if the pain is severe and does not go away after your period.
  B M-006 [menstruation] Period pain and cramps: Pain in the lower back or stomach during a period is common. To feel better you can move and stretch, do some exercise, put a hot compress on the painful area or have a warm bath. Ibuprofen may help if you have it. If the pain is very strong or stops you from doing your normal activities, see a health worker.

0.70
  A WEL-0085 [wellbeing] Smoking and women's reproductive health: Smoking harms women's reproductive health. It can make it harder to get pregnant, cause more painful periods, and increase the risk of cervical cancer. In pregnancy it raises the risk of miscarriage, early birth and small babies. Smoking also damages the heart and lungs and ages the skin. Women who smoke should tell their health provider when choosing contraception. Quitting at any age brings health benefits.
  B WEL-0086 [wellbeing] Smoking and men's sexual health: Smoking damages blood vessels, including those that carry blood to the penis, so over time it can cause erection problems. It also lowers sperm quality and number, which can reduce fertility. Smoking increases the risk of heart disease and cancers, including some that affect the genitals. Many of these effects improve after quitting, so stopping early is one of the best things you can do for your sexual health.

0.70
  A HIV-0080 [hiv] Can someone test me for HIV without my consent: HIV testing should be voluntary. Health workers should explain the test and get your agreement before testing you, and young people 12 and older in Rwanda can give their own consent. No one, including a partner, school or employer, should force you to test or test you secretly. If you feel your rights were not respected, you can talk to the health facility management or call 114.
  B H-004 [hiv] HIV testing: Testing is voluntary and private. In Rwanda, people aged 12 and above can agree to a test themselves, and for children aged 10 to 11 a parent or guardian agrees. A self-test kit is for people aged 16 and above and is sold at health facilities and pharmacies. A positive self-test must be confirmed by a health worker.

0.70
  A MEN-0004 [menstruation] What ovulation is: Ovulation is when one of your ovaries releases a mature egg. The egg travels down a tube towards the womb. This usually happens once in each cycle, often about two weeks before your next period starts, but the exact day differs between people and from month to month. The egg lives for about one day. Ovulation is a normal sign that your reproductive system is working, and it is the time when pregnancy is most possible if there is sex.
  B PRA-0003 [pregnancy] What an egg (ovum) is: An egg, also called an ovum, is a tiny cell stored in the ovaries of a girl or woman. Girls are born with all their eggs already inside. After puberty, usually one egg becomes mature and is released each cycle. The egg carries half of the information needed to make a baby. It is very small, about the size of a dot. If it does not meet a sperm, it breaks down and leaves the body.

0.70
  A STI-0054 [stis] Screening after the HPV vaccine: Yes, women who had the HPV vaccine still need cervical cancer screening when they reach the recommended age. The vaccine protects against the types of HPV that cause most cervical cancer, but not every type. Screening finds early changes in the cervix so they can be treated before they become cancer. Vaccination when young plus screening as an adult gives the strongest protection.
  B S-007 [stis] HPV vaccine: The HPV vaccine protects against the virus that can cause cervical cancer. Ask your school nurse or health center about the vaccine and who can get it.

0.70
  A PRA-0004 [pregnancy] What fertilisation means: Fertilisation is the moment when one sperm enters an egg and they join together. The sperm and egg each carry half of the information needed to make a person, so together they make one new cell. This cell starts dividing into more cells as it travels to the womb. Fertilisation alone does not mean pregnancy has fully started; the growing cells must also attach to the lining of the womb.
  B PRA-0005 [pregnancy] Where in the body fertilisation happens: Fertilisation usually happens in one of the fallopian tubes. These are two thin tubes that connect the ovaries to the womb. After an egg is released from an ovary, it moves into a tube. If sperm are there, one may join with the egg. The fertilised egg then moves slowly down the tube into the womb over several days, where it can attach and grow.

0.70
  A CSA-0170 [contraception] How effective withdrawal is: With withdrawal, the man pulls his penis out of the vagina before ejaculating. With typical use about 20 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year, and about 4 out of 100 when it is done correctly every time. It is one of the less effective methods. It costs nothing and is better than no method, but condoms or other methods protect much better.
  B CSA-0173 [contraception] Making withdrawal safer with another method: If you use withdrawal, adding another method makes pregnancy much less likely. Using a condom gives protection against both pregnancy and STIs. You could also combine it with pills, an injection or an implant. Keep emergency contraception in mind if he does not pull out in time.

0.70
  A HIV-0018 [hiv] HIV risk from oral sex: The risk of getting HIV from oral sex (mouth on penis, vagina or anus) is very low, much lower than vaginal or anal sex, but it is not zero. The risk goes up if there are cuts, sores or bleeding gums in the mouth, sores on the genitals, or if semen goes into the mouth. Oral sex can also pass other STIs. Using a condom or a barrier for oral sex lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0022 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between women: The risk of HIV passing between two women during sex is very low, but it is possible if blood, such as menstrual blood or blood from cuts, or vaginal fluids pass between partners, or if sex toys are shared without cleaning or a new condom. Women who have sex with women can also have male partners or other risks. Using barriers, not sharing toys, and regular testing help keep you healthy.

0.70
  A CON-0137 [consent_safety] Reporting abuse by a family member: Reporting a family member is very hard. People may fear breaking the family, losing support, or being blamed. But the child's or survivor's safety matters most, and staying silent usually lets abuse continue, sometimes to other children too. The abuser, not the person who reports, is responsible for any trouble. Services like 116, 3512 and Isange (3029) are used to these situations and can help plan for safety and support.
  B CON-0138 [consent_safety] Reporting abuse for someone else: Yes, you can report abuse even if it is happening to someone else, such as a friend, sibling, classmate or neighbour's child. You do not need proof; you only need a genuine worry. Share what you saw or heard. It is best to tell the person you are getting help, if it is safe. Call 116 for children, 3512 for GBV, 3029 for Isange, or 112 if someone is in immediate danger.

0.70
  A HIV-0050 [hiv] Washing or peeing after sex does not prevent HIV: Washing your genitals, peeing, douching or using water, soap, salt or other products after sex does not prevent HIV or pregnancy. The virus can enter the body very quickly during sex. Washing inside the vagina can even irritate it and raise infection risk. If you had sex without a condom, go to a health center within 72 hours to ask about PEP and emergency contraception.
  B PRA-0051 [pregnancy] Washing the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy: Washing or rinsing inside the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm can reach the cervix and womb within minutes, before you can wash. Putting water, soap, salt, lemon, soda or other liquids inside the vagina can also hurt the skin and cause infections. Just wash the outside gently with water. If you are worried about pregnancy, see a health worker.

0.70
  A CSB-0057 [contraception] What the hormonal IUD is: The hormonal IUD is a small T-shaped device placed in the womb that slowly releases a progestin hormone. It thickens cervical mucus so sperm cannot pass. It works for several years, often 3 to 8 depending on the type. Periods usually become lighter, and some users stop bleeding. It may not be available at every facility, so ask your health worker if they offer it.
  B C-008 [contraception] IUD (coil): The copper IUD is a small device placed in the womb by a trained provider. It protects against pregnancy for up to 12 years and can be removed at any time. Periods may become heavier with more cramps in the first 3 to 6 months. It does not protect against STIs. IUDs can come out more often in women who have never given birth, so check with a health worker.

0.70
  A CSB-0033 [contraception] What Sayana Press is: Sayana Press is a type of DMPA injection that comes in a small, prefilled pouch with a short needle. It is injected just under the skin, usually in the belly or thigh, every 3 months. Because it is simple to use, a trained health worker can teach some users to inject themselves at home. It works the same way and is as effective as the regular Depo injection.
  B CSB-0034 [contraception] Self-injecting Sayana Press: Yes, many people can learn to give themselves Sayana Press at home. A trained health worker first shows you how, watches you practise, and gives you a calendar for your next dates. You inject into the belly or thigh every 3 months. Self-injection can save trips and protect privacy. Ask at your health center whether self-injection is offered there. If you are unsure, the provider can keep giving you the injection.

0.70
  A PUB-0106 [puberty] What the foreskin is: The foreskin is the fold of skin that covers and protects the head of the penis in boys who are not circumcised. In young boys it is often tight and cannot be pulled back, which is normal. It usually loosens gradually during childhood and puberty. When a boy is circumcised, the foreskin is removed. Never force the foreskin back.
  B PUB-0120 [puberty] Tight foreskin: In many young boys the foreskin cannot be pulled back, and this is normal. It usually loosens with time, often by the teen years. Never force it, as this can cause tears and scarring. If your foreskin is still very tight in your teens, makes urine spray or balloon, causes pain during erections, or keeps getting infected, see a health worker. There are simple treatments.

0.70
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0141 [hiv] Is HIV treatment free in Rwanda: Yes. HIV treatment (ART), viral load tests and routine HIV care are provided free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay for your ARVs. If you face any problem getting your medicine, talk to the health facility or call 114.

0.70
  A MEN-0026 [menstruation] Sore breasts before a period: Many girls notice their breasts feel swollen, heavy or tender in the days before a period. This is caused by normal hormone changes and usually gets better once bleeding starts. Wearing a comfortable, supportive bra, using a warm or cool cloth, and cutting down on salty foods may help. If you find a lump that does not go away after your period, or the pain is in one spot and keeps getting worse, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0025 [puberty] Sore or tender breast buds: When breasts start to grow, it is common for them to feel sore, tender or itchy, and the bud under the nipple can hurt when touched or bumped. This is caused by normal growth and usually settles over time. Wearing a soft, comfortable top or bra and protecting your chest during sport can help. If there is redness, heat, pus or fever, see a health worker.

0.70
  A CSA-0128 [contraception] Why EC is not for regular use: Emergency contraception is safe, but it is meant as a backup, not an everyday method.
