It is less effective than regular methods like pills, injections, implants or IUDs, and using it often can make your bleeding irregular. If you have sex regularly, ask a health worker about a method that protects you all the time. Condoms also protect against STIs.
  B CSA-0151 [contraception] When you need EC: You may need emergency contraception after sex with no method, when a condom broke, slipped or leaked, after missing several pills, being late for an injection, if withdrawal failed, if you were forced to have sex, or if you made a mistake with any method. If you are not sure, ask a pharmacist or health worker quickly, or call 114.

0.70
  A PUB-0011 [puberty] Food and puberty timing: Good food helps your body grow and develop well. Young people who do not get enough food over a long time may start puberty later and grow less. Children who carry a lot of extra body weight may start a little earlier. There is no special food that makes puberty come faster or slower. Eating a mix of beans, vegetables, fruit, grains, and milk, eggs, fish or meat when you can supports healthy growth.
  B PUB-0021 [puberty] Healthy eating during puberty: Your body needs good food to grow during puberty. Try to eat a mix every day: grains or starchy foods for energy, beans, peas, groundnuts, eggs, fish, milk or meat for building the body, and fruit and green vegetables for vitamins. Foods with iron, like beans and dark green leaves, are important, especially for girls. Drink clean water, and limit sugary drinks and very salty or fried snacks.

0.70
  A HIV-0106 [hiv] Stigma about taking PrEP: Taking PrEP is a responsible choice to protect your health. It does not mean you have HIV or that you are a bad person. Some people may misunderstand PrEP because the pills look like HIV treatment. You decide whom to tell. If you face judgement, a counsellor or peer educator can support you. Your health is what matters.
  B HIV-0111 [hiv] Telling a partner or family about PrEP: Whether you tell anyone about PrEP is your choice. Telling a partner can help you plan protection together and avoid misunderstandings if they see your pills. Telling a trusted family member may give you support to take it daily. Explain that PrEP is a prevention medicine for HIV-negative people. A counsellor can help you plan the conversation.

0.70
  A SRV-0024 [services_rights] SRH help from a community health worker: Your community health worker can give you correct information about SRH topics such as family planning, pregnancy, HIV and STIs, and tell you where to get services. Some can provide condoms and certain contraceptive methods. They can follow up pregnant girls and women and help you get to the health centre. If you have a question you feel shy to ask at the clinic, your community health worker can be a good first step.
  B WEL-0068 [wellbeing] Community health workers and emotional support: Community health workers (abajyanama b'ubuzima) live in your village or cell and are trained to help with many health issues. You can talk to them if you feel stressed or worried or need information about sexual and reproductive health. They can listen, give basic advice and guide you to the health center when needed. They are expected to treat what you share with respect and privacy.

0.70
  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PUB-0007 [puberty] What causes puberty to start: Puberty starts when a part of your brain sends chemical messages, called hormones, to your body. In girls these messages tell the ovaries to make more estrogen. In boys they tell the testicles to make more testosterone. These hormones cause the changes you notice, like body hair, growth, breasts or a deeper voice. Nothing you do or eat switches it on. It happens naturally when your body is ready.

0.70
  A CSB-0052 [contraception] Rumour: injection causes permanent infertility: The injection does not make you permanently infertile. It can delay the return of fertility for some months after you stop, which is why some people believe this rumour. But almost all users are able to get pregnant later. If you want a baby soon after stopping, plan ahead or choose another method. If you are not pregnant after a year of trying, see a health worker.
  B CSB-0054 [contraception] Injection for someone who wants a baby soon: If you think you may want to get pregnant within the next year or so, the injection may not be the best choice, because fertility can take several months longer to return after stopping it. Methods like the implant, IUD, pills or condoms allow fertility to return quickly. Talk with a health worker about your plans so you can choose a method that fits your timing.

0.70
  A CON-0057 [consent_safety] Boys can be sexually abused: Boys and young men can be sexually abused, by men or by women. It is just as serious as abuse of girls. Boys often stay silent because of shame, fear of being laughed at, or myths that 'boys can't be victims' or 'he should be happy'. These ideas are false and harmful. Being abused does not change a boy's manhood or sexuality. Boys deserve to be believed and helped. They can call 116, 3512 or go to Isange (3029).
  B CON-0058 [consent_safety] Women can also commit sexual abuse: Most reported abusers are men, but women and girls can also sexually abuse children or young people. For example, an older woman may pressure a teenage boy into sex, or a female carer may touch a child sexually. This is still abuse and is never something a young person should feel lucky about. Victims of female abusers deserve the same belief and support. Report concerns to 116, 3512 or Isange (3029).

0.70
  A CSB-0066 [contraception] Heavier periods with the copper IUD: With the copper IUD, periods often become heavier, longer or more crampy, especially in the first 3 to 6 months. This usually improves with time and is not harmful. A health worker can suggest pain relief that helps. If bleeding is very heavy, lasts more than 8 days, or you feel weak or dizzy, go to your health center. You can also switch methods if you prefer.
  B CSB-0067 [contraception] Lighter periods with the hormonal IUD: The hormonal IUD usually makes periods lighter and less painful. In the first months you may have irregular spotting. Later, many users bleed very little, and some stop having periods. This is safe and not a sign of pregnancy or blood building up inside. If you have signs of pregnancy or severe belly pain, see a health worker.

0.70
  A CSA-0025 [contraception] Reusing a condom: A condom can only be used once. After sex, a used condom has semen on it and may be stretched or have tiny tears, so it will not protect you again. Never wash, turn over or reuse a condom, even with the same partner. Use a new condom for every act of sex. Condoms are free at health centers and youth corners, so you do not need to save them.
  B CSA-0040 [contraception] New condom for every round of sex: Use a new condom each time you have sex, including a second round on the same night with the same partner. Also change to a new condom if you move from anal to vaginal sex, to avoid spreading germs. After ejaculation, take the old condom off carefully, throw it away and put on a fresh one before any genital contact.

0.70
  A MEN-0026 [menstruation] Sore breasts before a period: Many girls notice their breasts feel swollen, heavy or tender in the days before a period. This is caused by normal hormone changes and usually gets better once bleeding starts. Wearing a comfortable, supportive bra, using a warm or cool cloth, and cutting down on salty foods may help. If you find a lump that does not go away after your period, or the pain is in one spot and keeps getting worse, see a health worker.
  B WEL-0129 [wellbeing] Breast pain not linked to periods: Breast pain at times other than before your period is common and is usually not a sign of cancer. It can come from a poorly fitting bra, exercise, muscle strain in the chest, injury, growth, or some hormonal contraception. A supportive bra and rest may help. See a health worker if pain stays in one spot, lasts more than a few weeks, comes with a lump, redness, warmth, fever or nipple changes.

0.70
  A STI-0103 [stis] Signs of a UTI: Signs of a urinary tract infection include burning or pain when urinating, needing to urinate often or urgently but passing only a little, cloudy or bad-smelling urine, blood in the urine, and pain or pressure in the lower belly. STIs can cause some of the same signs, so see a health worker, who may test your urine to find the cause.
  B STI-0104 [stis] Difference between a UTI and an STI: A UTI is an infection of the urinary system, usually caused by bowel bacteria, and it is not passed between partners. An STI is passed through sexual contact and can affect the genitals, throat or anus. Both can cause burning when urinating, which is why they are easily confused. STIs more often cause discharge, sores or itching. A health worker can test to tell them apart, and treatments are different.

0.70
  A HIV-0101 [hiv] HIV testing while on PrEP: You need an HIV test before starting PrEP, because PrEP is only for people who are HIV negative. While on PrEP, you will have regular HIV tests, often every few months, when you collect your refills. This makes sure you are still negative and that PrEP is the right medicine for you. Keep all your clinic appointments.
  B HIV-0120 [hiv] HIV testing after finishing PEP: When you start PEP, you are tested for HIV. After finishing PEP, you need follow-up HIV tests at times your health worker will tell you, to make sure you did not get HIV. Keep using condoms until your follow-up tests are done. If your risk continues, ask about PrEP.

0.70
  A PUB-0109 [puberty] One testicle hangs lower: It is very common and normal for one testicle to hang lower than the other, often the left one. This helps them not press against each other when you walk or sit. It is not a sign of a problem. However, if a testicle suddenly moves higher, becomes painful, swollen or hard, go to a health facility straight away.
  B WEL-0108 [wellbeing] Testicle size worries: Testicles grow during puberty and come in different sizes. It is normal for one testicle to be a little larger than the other. Size does not usually tell you about fertility or manhood. See a health worker if a testicle seems to be shrinking, one is missing from the scrotum, there is a lump or swelling, or there is pain. Steroid use can also shrink testicles.

0.70
  A HIV-0059 [hiv] How to get an HIV self-test kit in Rwanda: If you are 16 or older, you can ask for an HIV self-test kit at a health facility, and some places such as youth-friendly services may also offer them. Availability can differ from place to place. To find out where kits are available near you, ask a nurse at your health center or a community health worker, or call the free line 114.
  B HIV-0089 [hiv] Giving an HIV self-test kit to a partner: If you are 16 or older and got a self-test kit, you can offer one to your partner, if they are also 16 or older, so they can test privately. This can help partners who are shy about going to a clinic. Never force or trick someone into testing; it must be their choice. If their result is reactive, they should go to a health facility to confirm.

0.70
  A STI-0091 [stis] Signs of a yeast infection: Signs of a vaginal yeast infection include strong itching around the vulva and vagina, redness and soreness, a burning feeling when urinating or during sex, and a thick, white discharge that can look like curdled milk, usually without a strong smell. These signs can look like other infections, including STIs. If it is your first time, or the signs keep coming back, see a health worker to check.
  B STI-0092 [stis] What causes yeast infections: Yeast infections happen when the normal balance in the vagina changes and yeast grows too much. Things that can trigger this include taking antibiotics, pregnancy, uncontrolled diabetes, a weak immune system, hormonal changes, staying in wet or tight clothes, and washing inside the vagina or using scented soaps. Sometimes there is no clear reason. Yeast infections are not caused by poor morals or being unclean.

0.70
  A CSB-0037 [contraception] Late for the injection: If you are late for your injection, go to the health center as soon as you can. With DMPA, you can usually still get the injection if you are up to 4 weeks late. If you are later than that, the provider will check whether you might be pregnant before giving it. Until you get your injection, use condoms or avoid sex. Do not stay away because you feel embarrassed; providers want to help.
  B CSB-0048 [contraception] How soon the injection protects: If you get your first injection within 7 days after your period starts, it protects you right away. If you get it at another time, use condoms or avoid sex for the next 7 days. If you are switching from another hormonal method you used correctly, you may be protected right away. Your provider will tell you what applies to you.

0.70
  A CSB-0054 [contraception] Injection for someone who wants a baby soon: If you think you may want to get pregnant within the next year or so, the injection may not be the best choice, because fertility can take several months longer to return after stopping it. Methods like the implant, IUD, pills or condoms allow fertility to return quickly. Talk with a health worker about your plans so you can choose a method that fits your timing.
  B C-007 [contraception] Contraceptive injection: The injection is given every 3 months (every 2 months for NET-EN). As commonly used, about 4 in 100 women get pregnant in a year. Bleeding changes are common and not harmful, and no one else can tell you are using it. It can take about 4 months longer to become pregnant after stopping. It does not protect against STIs.

0.70
  A MEN-0113 [menstruation] Period lasting longer than seven days: Most periods last between two and seven days. If your bleeding goes on for more than seven days, especially more than once, see a health worker. It can happen when hormones are not yet balanced, especially in the first years, but it can also cause low blood (anaemia) or be a sign of another problem. If you bleed heavily for a long time and feel weak, dizzy or short of breath, go to a health facility quickly.
  B M-004 [menstruation] How long a period lasts: Bleeding usually lasts 2 to 7 days, but it can be different for different girls. If bleeding is very heavy, for example soaking a pad every hour, or lasts much longer than 7 days, please see a health worker.

0.70
  A PRA-0029 [pregnancy] Pregnancy without penetration: Pregnancy without the penis entering the vagina is unlikely but possible. If a boy ejaculates, or pre-cum leaks, on or very near the opening of the vagina, sperm can swim inside. Rubbing genitals together without clothes carries this small risk. If clothes are on and no semen touches the vulva, pregnancy cannot happen. If you are worried, a health worker can help you.
  B PRA-0069 [pregnancy] No pregnancy without sperm contact: If no sperm or semen touched your vagina or vulva, you cannot be pregnant. Pregnancy is not caused by thoughts, dreams, being alone with a boy, looking at sexual images or touching yourself. Feeling worried can sometimes cause symptoms like a late period or stomach upset, but these do not mean pregnancy. If worry is hard to manage, you can call 114.

0.70
  A WEL-0133 [wellbeing] A lump on the testicle: A new lump, hard area or swelling on a testicle should always be checked by a health worker, even if it does not hurt. Many lumps are harmless, such as cysts or fluid, but testicular cancer, though rare, is most common in young men and is very treatable when found early. Do not be shy or wait to see if it goes away. The check is quick and private.
  B WEL-0134 [wellbeing] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, severe pain in a testicle, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, can mean the testicle has twisted (testicular torsion). This is an emergency because the blood supply is cut off and the testicle can be lost within hours. Go to the nearest hospital or health facility immediately or call 112. Do not wait, and do not feel embarrassed; health workers see this often.

0.70
  A MEN-0011 [menstruation] What period blood is made of: Period blood is not only blood. It is a mix of blood, pieces of the womb lining, mucus from the cervix and normal vaginal fluid. That is why it can look thicker than blood from a cut and can have small soft lumps. It is not dirty or poisonous. It is a normal body fluid, just like sweat or saliva, and it leaves the body because the lining is not needed that month.
  B MEN-0160 [menstruation] Is period blood dirty: Period blood is not dirty, impure or dangerous. It is made of blood and womb lining and is as clean as blood from any other part of the body. It may smell when it meets air and bacteria outside the body, which is why we change pads and wash. A girl on her period is not unclean, and she can touch people, objects and food normally. Periods are a healthy part of life.

0.70
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.

0.70
  A STI-0078 [stis] What is hepatitis C: Hepatitis C is a liver infection caused by a virus. Many people have no signs for years, while the virus slowly damages the liver, sometimes leading to scarring or liver cancer. Unlike hepatitis B, there is no vaccine for hepatitis C. The good news is that today's medicines can cure most people in a few months. A blood test at a health center can show whether you have it.
  B STI-0079 [stis] How hepatitis C spreads: Hepatitis C spreads mostly through blood, for example by sharing needles or syringes, unsterile medical, tattoo or piercing tools, sharing razors or toothbrushes, and from mother to baby. It can spread through sex, but this is less common, with higher risk during sex that may cause bleeding, such as anal sex. It does not spread by hugging, kissing, sharing food or breastfeeding.

0.70
  A CSA-0017 [contraception] Getting a condom out of the vagina: A condom cannot get lost inside the body, because the vagina ends at the cervix. Wash your hands, relax, squat or put one leg up, and gently reach in with your fingers to pull it out. Do not use sharp objects. If you cannot reach it, go to a health center where a nurse can remove it. Because semen may have leaked, think about emergency contraception and ask about HIV prevention.
  B CSA-0057 [contraception] Penis outside the female condom: Sometimes the penis goes between the female condom and the vaginal wall, or the outer ring is pushed inside. If this happens, stop. Take the penis out, fix the condom, or replace it with a new one. Guide the penis into the condom with your hand. If semen went into the vagina outside the condom, think about emergency contraception and ask a health center about HIV prevention.

0.70
  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B HIV-0059 [hiv] How to get an HIV self-test kit in Rwanda: If you are 16 or older, you can ask for an HIV self-test kit at a health facility, and some places such as youth-friendly services may also offer them. Availability can differ from place to place. To find out where kits are available near you, ask a nurse at your health center or a community health worker, or call the free line 114.

0.70
  A STI-0072 [stis] Testing for hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is found with a blood test, often a quick finger-prick test. If it is positive, more blood tests show whether the infection is new or long-lasting and how the liver is doing. Testing is a good idea if you were never vaccinated, if a family member or partner has hepatitis B, or if you are pregnant. Ask at your nearest health center or call 114 to find out where testing is offered.
  B STI-0077 [stis] Living with hepatitis B: People with hepatitis B can live full lives. Go to regular check-ups, take any medicine exactly as advised, and avoid alcohol, which harms the liver. Do not take herbal or other remedies without asking a health worker, as some damage the liver. Protect others by using condoms, not sharing razors or toothbrushes, and covering cuts. Encourage partners and family to get tested and vaccinated. You can still eat, study and play with others.

0.69
  A CSA-0086 [contraception] The pill and STI protection: No. Combined and progestin-only pills only protect against pregnancy. They give no protection against HIV, chlamydia, gonorrhoea, syphilis or other STIs. If there is any chance of infection, also use a condom every time you have sex. Using the pill together with condoms is called dual protection.
  B S-005 [stis] Condoms and STI protection: Condoms reduce the risk of many STIs when used correctly every time you have sex. They protect best against STIs spread by discharge, such as HIV, gonorrhea and chlamydia, and also help against herpes and HPV. Other methods such as pills, implants, injections and emergency pills do not protect against STIs.

0.69
  A S-001 [stis] What are STIs: STIs are infections that pass from person to person through sexual contact. Anyone who has had sex can get one. Many STIs have no symptoms, especially in girls, so a person can have one without knowing. Testing and treatment protect you and your partner.
  B S-003 [stis] Can STIs be cured: Many STIs can be treated and cured with the right medicine, and early treatment prevents damage to your body. Some, such as HIV and herpes, cannot be cured but can be managed. Go to a health center for testing and treatment.

0.69
  A CSB-0085 [contraception] Why sterilisation is not advised for unsure young people: Sterilisation is permanent, and many young people's plans change as they grow, find new partners or face new life situations. People who are sterilised young are more likely to regret it later. If you are young or not completely sure, a long-acting reversible method like an implant or IUD gives strong protection for years and can be removed whenever you want to have children.
  B CSB-0090 [contraception] Nobody can force sterilisation: Sterilisation must always be a free, informed choice by the person themselves. No partner, family member, health worker or anyone else can force or trick someone into it, and it should never be a condition for other care. The person must receive full information and give clear consent. If someone pressures you, you can refuse and seek help at a health center, Isange One Stop Centre, or call 3512.

0.69
  A STI-0090 [stis] What is a vaginal yeast infection: A vaginal yeast infection, also called thrush or candida, happens when a yeast that normally lives in the body grows too much in the vagina. It is very common, and most women get it at least once. It is not usually passed through sex and is not a sign of being dirty. It causes itching and discharge but is not dangerous, and it can be treated.
  B STI-0093 [stis] Is a yeast infection an STI: No, a yeast infection is not considered an STI. It can happen to girls who have never had sex. However, sex can sometimes irritate the vagina and trigger it, and a partner may occasionally get mild itching too. Because yeast infection signs can look like those of STIs, if you have had sex and have new discharge or itching, it is wise to see a health worker.

0.69
  A HIV-0046 [hiv] Having many sexual partners and HIV risk: Having more than one sexual partner, especially at the same time, increases the chance of HIV, because each partner may have other partners too, creating a network where HIV can spread quickly. You often cannot know your partners' status. Fewer partners, using condoms every time, testing together, and PrEP if you are at high risk all lower your risk. This is about staying safe, not about judging anyone.
  B HIV-0076 [hiv] When one partner has HIV and the other does not: A couple where one partner has HIV and the other does not is called a discordant couple, and it is more common than people think. Such couples can stay together and have a healthy relationship. When the partner with HIV takes treatment and keeps an undetectable viral load, HIV is not passed through sex. The negative partner can also use PrEP and condoms, and test regularly. A counsellor can support you both.

0.69
  A PRA-0100 [pregnancy] Healthy timing and spacing of pregnancies: For the health of mother and baby, it is best to wait until at least age 18 before a first pregnancy, when the body is more ready. After a birth, waiting at least 2 years before trying to get pregnant again is healthier. After a miscarriage or abortion, waiting at least 6 months is advised. A health worker can help you plan and choose contraception until you are ready.
  B PRB-0080 [pregnancy] Healthy birth spacing: After having a baby, WHO advises waiting at least 24 months (two years) before trying to get pregnant again. Spacing births gives your body time to recover and rebuild its strength, and lowers the risk of problems like early birth, low birth weight and anaemia for the next baby. It also gives you time to care for your baby, finish school or work. A health worker can help you choose a family planning method that suits you, including while breastfeeding.

0.69
  A STI-0010 [stis] What is gonorrhoea: Gonorrhoea (sometimes spelled gonorrhea) is a common sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It can infect the penis, vagina, cervix, anus, throat and sometimes the eyes. Some people notice discharge or pain when urinating, but many, especially girls, have no signs. Gonorrhoea can be cured with the right medicine from a health worker, but it is becoming harder to treat because some bacteria resist medicines. Get tested at a health center if you are worried.
  B STI-0011 [stis] How gonorrhoea spreads: Gonorrhoea passes during vaginal, anal or oral sex without a condom with a person who has the infection. It can spread even if there is no ejaculation. A mother can pass it to her baby during birth. It does not spread through hugging, sharing cups, handshakes or toilet seats. Using a condom correctly every time you have sex, and getting tested with your partner, lowers the risk a lot.

0.69
  A C-002 [contraception] What is contraception and what methods exist: Contraception helps you prevent pregnancy and decide if and when to have children. Methods include condoms, pills, implants, injections, IUDs and emergency pills. Condoms are the only method that also protects against STIs, including HIV. A health worker can help you choose the method that fits your life.
  B C-010 [contraception] Contraception myths: Contraceptives are not only for married people. Pills do not make you infertile. Washing the vagina with Coca-Cola or Sprite after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Using two condoms does not protect more. The days between periods are not always safe, especially when periods are irregular.

0.69
  A WEL-0011 [wellbeing] Stress and low interest in sex: It is very common for stress, worry, tiredness or sadness to lower interest in sex for both young men and women. The body focuses on the problem instead. This usually improves when the stress gets better. Getting enough sleep, exercise and talking about what is bothering you can help. Never feel pushed to have sex when you do not want to. If low interest comes with feeling down for weeks, talk to a health worker.
  B WEL-0096 [wellbeing] Low sexual desire: Sexual desire naturally goes up and down. It can be lower because of stress, tiredness, sadness, relationship problems, past bad experiences, illness, alcohol or some medicines, including some hormonal methods for some people. Having lower desire is not a disease, and nobody should pressure you. If a change in desire bothers you or comes with low mood or other symptoms, talk to a health worker.

0.69
  A HIV-0016 [hiv] HIV risk from vaginal sex: Vaginal sex without a condom is one of the most common ways HIV is passed, both from man to woman and from woman to man. The virus in semen, pre-cum or vaginal fluids can enter through the thin lining of the vagina or the penis. Risk is higher if there are sores, other STIs, bleeding, or if the partner with HIV is not on treatment. Using a condom correctly every time greatly lowers the risk.
  B HIV-0021 [hiv] Can a woman pass HIV to a man: Yes. A woman living with HIV who is not on effective treatment can pass HIV to a man during sex without a condom. The virus in vaginal fluids or menstrual blood can enter through the tip of the penis, the foreskin, or small cuts or sores. Some boys wrongly believe only men pass HIV. Condoms protect both partners, and a partner on treatment with an undetectable viral load does not pass HIV through sex.

0.69
  A STI-0087 [stis] How scabies spreads: Scabies spreads through prolonged skin-to-skin contact with a person who has it, such as sharing a bed, caring for family members, or sexual contact. Brief contact like a handshake rarely spreads it. Less often, it spreads through shared bedding, clothes or towels. Because it spreads easily in homes and dormitories, everyone who has close contact should be treated at the same time.
  B STI-0088 [stis] Signs of scabies: The main sign of scabies is strong itching, often worse at night. You may see small red bumps, blisters or thin wavy lines on the skin, often between the fingers, on the wrists, elbows, armpits, waist, buttocks and genitals. On the penis, it can cause itchy lumps. Scratching can cause sores that get infected. Many skin problems look similar, so see a health worker to check.

0.69
  A STI-0082 [stis] What are pubic lice: Pubic lice, sometimes called crabs, are tiny insects that live in pubic hair and suck small amounts of blood. They can also live in other coarse body hair, like armpits, chest or eyebrows. They are different from head lice. Pubic lice are annoying and itchy but not dangerous, and they can be treated. Having them is nothing to be ashamed of; see a health worker for help.
  B STI-0083 [stis] How pubic lice spread: Pubic lice spread mainly through close body contact, usually during sex or intimate touching, even without penetration. Less often, they can spread through sharing bedding, towels or clothing that someone with lice has just used. Condoms do not prevent pubic lice because the lice live in hair outside the area a condom covers. They do not come from toilet seats or animals.

0.69
  A CSA-0174 [contraception] Washing or peeing after sex myth: Peeing, washing the vagina, jumping, or pushing after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm swim into the womb very quickly, within minutes, and urine comes out of a different opening. Washing inside the vagina can also cause infections. If you had unprotected sex, emergency contraception within 5 days is the right step.
  B PRA-0051 [pregnancy] Washing the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy: Washing or rinsing inside the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm can reach the cervix and womb within minutes, before you can wash. Putting water, soap, salt, lemon, soda or other liquids inside the vagina can also hurt the skin and cause infections. Just wash the outside gently with water. If you are worried about pregnancy, see a health worker.

0.69
  A CSA-0019 [contraception] Lubricants that are safe with latex condoms: Lubricant (lube) makes sex more comfortable and helps stop condoms from tearing. With latex condoms, only use water-based or silicone-based lubricants, or clean water. Some condoms already come with lubricant on them. Many pharmacies sell water-based lube. Check the label, and if you are not sure, ask the pharmacist which lubricant is safe with condoms.
  B CSA-0028 [contraception] Non-latex condom options: Condoms that are not made of latex are made of materials like polyurethane or nitrile. They are good for people with a latex allergy and can be used with any type of lubricant, including oil-based ones. The female (internal) condom is usually made of nitrile. Non-latex condoms also protect against pregnancy, HIV and other STIs when used correctly. Ask at a pharmacy or health center if they are available.

0.69
  A HIV-0066 [hiv] Is my HIV test result confidential: Yes. Your HIV test and result are confidential. Health workers must not tell your parents, teachers, partner, friends or anyone else without your permission, except other health workers caring for you. If you are worried about privacy, ask the counsellor how your information will be kept. You decide whom to tell and when.
  B HIV-0087 [hiv] Telling my parents I took an HIV test: You do not have to tell your parents that you tested for HIV; it is your choice and your result is private. Still, many young people find it helpful to have support from a trusted adult, especially if the result is positive. If you want to tell them, a counsellor can help you plan what to say or even be with you when you talk. Choose someone you feel safe with.

0.69
  A PRB-0043 [pregnancy] Rest and sleep in pregnancy: Your body works hard to grow a baby, so you need more rest than usual. Try to sleep enough at night and rest during the day if you can, even for a short time with your feet up. Feeling tired is common, especially in the first and last months. Ask family or friends to help with chores. If you feel extremely tired, weak or breathless all the time, tell a health worker, as it can be a sign of anaemia.
  B PRB-0071 [pregnancy] Trouble sleeping in pregnancy: Many pregnant women find it hard to sleep, especially later on, because of a big belly, needing to urinate, heartburn, leg cramps, the baby moving, or worries. Try lying on your side with a pillow between your knees, eating dinner earlier, keeping a regular bedtime and resting during the day. Gentle activity in the day may help. Do not take sleeping medicines without a health worker's advice. If worries keep you awake, talk to someone you trust.

0.69
  A CSA-0196 [contraception] Condoms with long-acting methods: Implants, injections and IUDs are very good at preventing pregnancy, but they do not protect against HIV or other STIs. If there is any chance you or your partner could have an infection, keep using condoms as well. This is dual protection and keeps you safe from both pregnancy and infections.
  B C-004 [contraception] Male condoms: Condoms are worn on the penis and block sperm and infections. They are the only method that protects against both pregnancy and STIs, including HIV. Used correctly every time, about 2 in 100 women get pregnant in a year; as commonly used, about 13 in 100. Use a new condom each time and talk about it with your partner before sex. Using two condoms at once does not protect more and can make them break.

0.69
  A SRV-0023 [services_rights] What a community health worker is: A community health worker, often called an abajyanama b'ubuzima, is a person chosen by the community and trained to help with health in the village. They give health advice, treat some common illnesses in children, follow up pregnant women, share family planning information and help people get to the health centre when needed. They live near you, so they are easy to reach. They work closely with the health centre.
  B SRV-0027 [services_rights] Finding your community health worker: Every village in Rwanda usually has community health workers. To find yours, ask your parents, a neighbour, your village leader or the staff at your health centre. They can tell you who the community health worker is and where they live. You can visit them or ask for their phone number so you can contact them when you need advice.

0.69
  A CSB-0011 [contraception] Fertility after implant removal: Your ability to get pregnant returns very quickly after an implant is removed, usually within days or weeks. The implant does not cause lasting harm to your fertility, even if you used it for many years. If you do not want to get pregnant right after removal, start another method straight away. If you want a baby, you can start trying soon after removal; a health worker can give you advice about a healthy pregnancy.
  B CSB-0073 [contraception] Fertility after IUD removal: After an IUD is removed, your fertility returns right away. You can become pregnant in your very next cycle. If you do not want to get pregnant yet, start another method immediately. If you do want a baby, you can start trying; visiting a health worker for advice before pregnancy is helpful.

0.69
  A CSB-0086 [contraception] What a vasectomy is: A vasectomy is a simple, safe operation for men. A trained provider closes the small tubes that carry sperm from the testicles, so the semen no longer contains sperm. It is done with local numbing and takes a short time. It is meant to be permanent and is for men who are sure they do not want any more children.
  B CSB-0088 [contraception] How soon a vasectomy works: A vasectomy does not work right away. Sperm can remain in the tubes for about 3 months after the operation. During this time, the couple must use another method, such as condoms. A semen test, where available, can confirm there are no sperm. After that, it is one of the most effective methods.

0.69
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0014 [puberty] Growth spurt in boys: Boys usually have their growth spurt a bit later than girls, often around ages 12 to 16. During the spurt a boy can grow quickly in height, then his shoulders get broader and muscles get stronger. That is why girls are often taller than boys for a while at the start of secondary school. Most boys catch up and keep growing for longer.

0.69
  A CSA-0127 [contraception] EC twice in the same cycle: If you have unprotected sex again after taking EC, even in the same month, you can take emergency contraceptive pills again. It is safe. Your next period may be more irregular. Remember that EC only protects against sex that happened before you took it, so start a regular method or use condoms to stay protected.
  B CSA-0148 [contraception] Sex again after taking EC: Emergency pills only protect against sex that happened before you took them. They do not protect you during the rest of the month. If you have sex again after taking EC, use a condom or start a regular method. Because EC can delay ovulation, you might release an egg later than usual and get pregnant.

0.69
  A CSA-0124 [contraception] EC is not abortion: Emergency contraceptive pills are not abortion pills. They prevent pregnancy from starting, mainly by delaying the release of an egg. If a pregnancy has already begun, they do not end it and do not harm it. Using EC is a safe and responsible way to prevent an unplanned pregnancy after unprotected sex.
  B CSA-0125 [contraception] Taking EC when already pregnant: If you are already pregnant, emergency contraceptive pills will not work, but they will not harm the pregnancy or the baby either. So if you took EC and later find out you were pregnant, there is no need to worry about damage from the pills. If your period is late, take a pregnancy test and talk to a health worker about your options and care.

0.69
  A PRA-0031 [pregnancy] Sperm on clothes or bedsheets: You cannot get pregnant from sperm on clothes, bedsheets, chairs or towels that later touch you. Sperm die quickly once semen dries or is exposed to air, and they cannot swim through fabric. For pregnancy, fresh semen needs to get into or right at the vagina. Sitting on a sheet or wearing clothes with old semen on them will not cause pregnancy.
  B PRA-0047 [pregnancy] Sex through underwear or clothes: If both of you have clothes or underwear on, and the penis does not touch the vulva directly, pregnancy is extremely unlikely. Sperm cannot swim through fabric. A small risk exists only if clothes are moved aside or fresh semen soaks through and touches the vaginal opening. Dry rubbing with clothes still on does not normally lead to pregnancy.

0.69
  A CSB-0069 [contraception] IUD for young women who have never given birth: Yes. Young women who have never been pregnant can usually use an IUD. WHO says age and not having children are not reasons to refuse an IUD. Insertion may be a bit more uncomfortable, and the chance of it coming out may be slightly higher, but it is safe and effective. It does not harm future fertility. A health worker can help you decide.
  B CSB-0081 [contraception] Who may not be able to use an IUD: Most people can use an IUD, including young people. A provider may advise waiting or choosing another method if you might be pregnant, have unexplained vaginal bleeding, have a current pelvic infection or certain STIs, or have some rare conditions of the womb. These are checked at your visit. If an IUD is not right for you now, the provider will help you find another good method.

0.69
  A CSA-0189 [contraception] What LAM is: LAM stands for Lactational Amenorrhea Method. It is a temporary family planning method based on breastfeeding. When a mother breastfeeds her baby fully or nearly fully, day and night, her body may not release eggs. LAM can protect against pregnancy for up to 6 months after birth, but only if all its conditions are met.
  B CSA-0191 [contraception] When LAM stops protecting you: LAM stops protecting you when your period comes back, when you start giving your baby other food, water or formula regularly, when you breastfeed less often or stop night feeds, or when your baby reaches 6 months. Pregnancy can happen even before the first period returns, so start another method as soon as any of these happens.

0.69
  A SRV-0085 [services_rights] What the 114 line is for: 114 is a free health information line run by Rwanda Biomedical Centre (RBC). You can call it to ask health questions, including about HIV, STIs, contraception and other SRH topics, and to find out where to get services. It is a good number to call if you are unsure where to go or want advice before visiting a health centre. In an emergency, call 912 or 112.
  B SRV-0086 [services_rights] What the 116 line is for: 116 is Rwanda's free child helpline. Children and young people, or adults worried about a child, can call it to report abuse, neglect, violence or any situation where a child is not safe, and to get advice and support. You can call 116 for yourself or for another child. If someone is in danger right now, call 112.

0.69
  A HIV-0021 [hiv] Can a woman pass HIV to a man: Yes. A woman living with HIV who is not on effective treatment can pass HIV to a man during sex without a condom. The virus in vaginal fluids or menstrual blood can enter through the tip of the penis, the foreskin, or small cuts or sores. Some boys wrongly believe only men pass HIV. Condoms protect both partners, and a partner on treatment with an undetectable viral load does not pass HIV through sex.
  B HIV-0022 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between women: The risk of HIV passing between two women during sex is very low, but it is possible if blood, such as menstrual blood or blood from cuts, or vaginal fluids pass between partners, or if sex toys are shared without cleaning or a new condom. Women who have sex with women can also have male partners or other risks. Using barriers, not sharing toys, and regular testing help keep you healthy.

0.69
  A CSA-0050 [contraception] How to insert a female condom: Check the expiry date and open the packet carefully. Find a comfortable position, like squatting or lifting one leg. Squeeze the inner ring at the closed end and push it into the vagina as far as it will go. Put a finger inside the condom and push the inner ring up. The outer ring stays outside, covering the opening. During sex, guide the penis into the condom.
  B CSA-0052 [contraception] Putting in a female condom before sex: A female condom can be put in some hours before sex, up to about 8 hours before. This means you do not have to stop in the moment, and you can be ready and protected. Make sure it stays in place until sex is finished. Remove it after sex and use a new one the next time.

0.69
  A MEN-0090 [menstruation] Itching during a period: Itching during your period is often caused by a damp pad, perfumed products, rough cloth, or heat and sweat. Change pads often, use unscented products, wear cotton underwear, and keep the area clean and dry. If the itching is strong, lasts after your period, or comes with thick white or coloured discharge, sores, or a bad smell, it may be an infection, so see a nurse at your health center.
  B PUB-0101 [puberty] Itching around the vulva: Itching around the vulva can come from sweat, tight or synthetic underwear, perfumed soap, shaving, or an infection. Wear clean, loose cotton underwear, wash the outside with plain water, dry well and avoid scented products. Do not scratch. If itching lasts more than a few days, or comes with unusual discharge, sores, swelling or pain, see a health worker for a check.

0.69
  A CON-0133 [consent_safety] Health centres can help after violence: Any health centre or hospital can give first care after violence or sexual assault, such as treating injuries, protecting against HIV and pregnancy, and referring to Isange or counselling. If the nearest Isange One Stop Centre is far, going to the nearest health facility quickly is a good first step, especially within 72 hours after sexual violence. Health workers should treat survivors with respect and privacy.
  B CON-0144 [consent_safety] Police or hospital first after assault: After sexual assault, health comes first. The best place to go is an Isange One Stop Centre, because medical care, counselling and police are all in one place. If Isange is far, go to the nearest health facility first for urgent care, especially within 72 hours, and they can refer you. You can also call 3512 or 112, and police can help you get to care. There is no wrong door.

0.69
  A CSA-0146 [contraception] Starting regular contraception after EC: After using EC, you can start a regular method soon. After levonorgestrel or combined EC pills, you can usually start pills, injections or an implant right away. After ulipristal EC pills, wait 5 days before starting a hormonal method. Use condoms in the meantime. A health worker will explain what to do and may suggest a pregnancy test later.
  B CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.

0.69
  A CSA-0061 [contraception] Female condom during your period: Yes, you can use a female condom during your period. It still protects against pregnancy and STIs, including HIV. Pregnancy during a period is less likely but possible, and HIV and other infections can pass more easily when there is blood. Remove any tampon or menstrual cup before inserting the female condom.
  B HIV-0020 [hiv] HIV and sex during menstruation: Having sex without a condom during a period can increase the risk of passing HIV, because menstrual blood can carry the virus if the woman has HIV, and the cervix may be more open. If a woman does not have HIV, sex during her period does not protect her either. Using a condom every time is the best way to protect both partners at any time of the month.

0.69
  A C-002 [contraception] What is contraception and what methods exist: Contraception helps you prevent pregnancy and decide if and when to have children. Methods include condoms, pills, implants, injections, IUDs and emergency pills. Condoms are the only method that also protects against STIs, including HIV. A health worker can help you choose the method that fits your life.
  B H-010 [hiv] Contraception for people living with HIV or at risk: All family planning methods are safe for people at high risk of HIV or living with HIV, except spermicides containing nonoxynol-9. Condoms are still important to prevent HIV and other STIs. A health worker can help you choose a method that works with your HIV medicine.

0.69
  A CON-0076 [consent_safety] What is sexual exploitation: Sexual exploitation is when someone takes advantage of another person's need, poverty, age or trust to get sex or sexual images, often in exchange for money, food, grades, a job, a place to stay or protection. The person exploited may seem to agree, but they are not truly free to say no. It is abuse of power. Exploiting a child is a serious crime. Help is available from 116, 3512 or Isange (3029).
  B CON-0082 [consent_safety] Commercial sexual exploitation of children: Commercial sexual exploitation of children means using a child (under 18) for sex or sexual images in exchange for money, goods or anything of value, for the child or for someone else. This includes child prostitution, selling sexual images of children, and adults arranging sex with children. It is a serious crime and a form of child abuse. The child is a victim, never a criminal. Anyone who knows of this should call 116, 3512, RIB or police on 112.

0.69
  A PUB-0107 [puberty] What the testicles do: The testicles, also called testes or balls, are two oval organs inside the scrotum. From puberty they make sperm and the hormone testosterone. Testosterone causes many boy changes, like a deeper voice, more body hair and muscles. Sperm leave the body in semen during ejaculation. The testicles are sensitive, so protect them during sport.
  B PUB-0124 [puberty] Checking your testicles: It is good for boys to check their testicles once a month, so they know what is normal. The best time is after a warm bath, when the scrotum is relaxed. Gently roll each testicle between your thumb and fingers. It is normal to feel a soft tube at the back, called the epididymis. If you notice a new lump, swelling, hardness, heaviness or pain, see a health worker.

0.69
  A MEN-0111 [menstruation] What counts as a heavy period: Your period may be heavy if you need to change a pad every one to two hours for several hours in a row, need to use two pads at once, bleed through at night often, pass clots bigger than a large coin, or bleed for more than seven days. Heavy periods can make you tired, weak or short of breath. If this sounds like you, see a health worker. If you soak a pad every hour and feel dizzy or faint, go to a health facility now.
  B MEN-0115 [menstruation] Very light periods: Some people have light periods that need only a few pads, and this can be normal, especially in the first years or if light periods are usual for you. Periods can also become lighter because of stress, weight loss, intense exercise, illness or some contraceptive methods. If your period suddenly becomes much lighter than usual and stays that way, or you also have other changes, a health worker can check you. Pregnancy should also be considered if you have had sex.

0.69
  A CSA-0195 [contraception] Condoms while on the pill: Yes, it is a good idea to use condoms even if you take the pill. The pill protects well against pregnancy, but not against HIV or other STIs. Condoms add that protection and also back up the pill if you miss some. Unless you and your partner have both tested and only have sex with each other, use both.
  B HIV-0103 [hiv] Using PrEP and condoms together: Using PrEP and condoms together gives you the strongest protection. PrEP protects against HIV, while condoms protect against HIV, other STIs and pregnancy. Condoms also add protection on days when you may have missed PrEP pills. You and your partner can choose what works best, and a health worker can help you plan.

0.69
  A PUB-0125 [puberty] Sudden pain in a testicle: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle or the scrotum, especially with swelling, nausea or vomiting, is an emergency. It can mean the testicle has twisted and lost its blood supply. Go to a health facility now, or call 912 or 112. Treatment within a few hours can save the testicle. Do not wait to see if it goes away, and do not feel shy; health workers see this often.
  B WEL-0118 [wellbeing] Testicle ache after arousal: Some boys and men feel a dull ache or heaviness in the testicles after being sexually aroused for a long time without ejaculating. This happens because extra blood flows to the area. It is uncomfortable but harmless and goes away on its own within a short time. It is never a reason to pressure anyone into sex. Severe or sudden pain, swelling or pain that lasts needs a health worker.

0.69
  A MEN-0118 [menstruation] Period a few days late: A period that is a few days late is very common and usually nothing to worry about. Cycle length can change a little each month, especially with stress, illness, travel or changes in sleep or eating. In the first years after periods begin, being late is even more common. If you have had sex, a pregnancy test can give you an answer. If you are late often or by a week or more, talk to a health worker.
  B MEN-0119 [menstruation] Period coming early: Your period may come a few days earlier than expected because of stress, illness, changes in weight, travel, intense exercise, or hormones that are still settling in the first years. Some contraceptive methods can also change bleeding. One early period is usually nothing to worry about. If your periods often come less than three weeks apart, or bleeding is very heavy, see a health worker.

0.69
  A CSA-0095 [contraception] Where to get contraceptive pills in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get combined and progestin-only pills at public health centers and health posts, youth corners, and pharmacies. In many areas, community health workers can also give pills to continue a method. A health worker will ask some questions and may check your blood pressure. Call 114, the free RBC line, to find services near you.
  B CSA-0132 [contraception] Where to get EC in Rwanda: In Rwanda, emergency contraceptive pills are available at health centers, youth corners and many pharmacies. Go as soon as possible after unprotected sex. A health center can also tell you about the copper IUD as emergency contraception. If you were forced to have sex, Isange One Stop Centre (3029) gives EC and other care for free. You can call 114 for advice.

0.69
  A PUB-0001 [puberty] Age puberty starts in girls: Most girls start puberty somewhere between about 8 and 13 years old. The first sign is usually small breast buds, then hair under the arms and around the genitals, a growth spurt and later the first period. Every girl has her own timing, so starting earlier or later than your friends is usually normal. If you have questions about your own timing, a nurse at your health center can check and reassure you.
  B PUB-0010 [puberty] First sign of puberty in boys: For most boys the first sign of puberty is that the testicles get bigger and the skin of the scrotum becomes thinner, a bit darker and hangs lower. Many boys do not notice this at first. Next, pubic hair starts to grow and the penis gets longer and wider. The growth spurt, voice change and facial hair usually come later.

0.69
  A CSB-0088 [contraception] How soon a vasectomy works: A vasectomy does not work right away. Sperm can remain in the tubes for about 3 months after the operation. During this time, the couple must use another method, such as condoms. A semen test, where available, can confirm there are no sperm. After that, it is one of the most effective methods.
  B CSB-0092 [contraception] Vasectomy recovery: After a vasectomy, a man may have mild pain, swelling or bruising in the scrotum for a few days. Resting, wearing supportive underwear and keeping the area clean help. Most men return to light work in a day or two and avoid heavy lifting for a few days. Fever, bleeding, pus or severe pain need a health worker.

0.69
  A HIV-0058 [hiv] What an HIV self-test is: An HIV self-test lets you test yourself privately, using a kit with either a mouth swab (oral fluid) or a finger-prick blood drop. You do the test yourself and read the result in a short time, following the instructions. It is a screening test: a reactive result must be confirmed at a health facility, and a non-reactive result may need repeating if you had a recent risk. It is for people 16 and older in Rwanda.
  B HIV-0063 [hiv] How a rapid HIV test is done: A rapid HIV test at a health facility usually uses a small drop of blood from a finger prick. The health worker puts the blood on a test strip and you get the result in a short time, often during the same visit. Before and after the test, a counsellor talks with you, answers your questions, and explains what the result means. It is quick, safe and private.

0.69
  A HIV-0074 [hiv] Couples HIV testing: Couples HIV testing means you and your partner test together and receive your results together with a counsellor. It helps you both know your status, plan how to protect each other, and support each other whatever the results. It also builds trust. Each person still has the right to decide. Ask at your health center or youth corner about testing together.
  B HIV-0075 [hiv] When a partner refuses to test for HIV: If your partner refuses to test, try to talk calmly about why it matters to both of you, and suggest testing together or offering a self-test kit if they are 16 or older. A counsellor can help you plan the conversation. You cannot force anyone, but you can protect yourself: use condoms every time, test regularly, and ask about PrEP. If you feel unsafe or pressured, call 3512 or 116.

0.69
  A CSA-0137 [contraception] Most effective type of EC: The copper IUD is the most effective emergency contraception. Among pills, those containing ulipristal acetate are somewhat more effective than levonorgestrel pills, especially later in the 5-day window. All types work best when used as early as possible. Use whichever you can get quickly; any EC is better than none.
  B CSA-0138 [contraception] Types of EC pills: There are three kinds of emergency contraceptive pills: progestin-only pills containing levonorgestrel (the most common), pills containing ulipristal acetate, and some ordinary combined pills used in a special way under a provider's guidance. Each has its own instructions. A pharmacist or health worker will give you the right one and explain how to take it.

0.69
  A CON-0091 [consent_safety] Men and boys as victims of GBV: Men and boys can also experience gender-based violence, including sexual abuse, physical violence, emotional abuse and economic control by partners or others. Many do not report because of shame or the belief that men must be strong or that no one will believe them. These ideas stop men from getting help. Every survivor deserves respect and support, whatever their gender. Men and boys can call 3512, 116 (if under 18) or go to Isange (3029).
  B CON-0099 [consent_safety] Role of men and boys in ending GBV: Men and boys play a big part in ending gender-based violence. They can treat girls and women as equals, respect consent, refuse to join jokes about rape or beating, speak up when friends are disrespectful, share housework, and support survivors. Being a strong man means being caring and fair, not controlling. Boys who learn respect grow into partners and fathers who protect families. Anyone who sees violence can report it to 3512.

0.69
  A PUB-0002 [puberty] Age puberty starts in boys: Most boys start puberty somewhere between about 9 and 14 years old, often a little later than girls. The first sign is usually the testicles and scrotum getting bigger. After that come pubic hair, a bigger penis, a growth spurt, a deeper voice and later facial hair. Every boy has his own timing. If you are unsure about yours, a nurse at the health center can check you privately.
  B PUB-0113 [puberty] When the penis grows in puberty: The penis usually starts growing longer and then wider a little after the testicles begin to grow, often from around ages 11 to 15. It keeps growing gradually for several years, often until about 16 to 18. Every boy's timing is different. If you are early in puberty, your penis has likely not finished growing yet.

0.69
  A STI-0091 [stis] Signs of a yeast infection: Signs of a vaginal yeast infection include strong itching around the vulva and vagina, redness and soreness, a burning feeling when urinating or during sex, and a thick, white discharge that can look like curdled milk, usually without a strong smell. These signs can look like other infections, including STIs. If it is your first time, or the signs keep coming back, see a health worker to check.
  B STI-0094 [stis] Yeast infection in boys: Boys and men can also get yeast infections, usually on the head of the penis or under the foreskin. Signs include redness, itching, soreness, a white substance under the foreskin and sometimes a rash. It is more likely if the area stays moist, if the person has diabetes, or after antibiotics. Washing gently with water and drying well helps. Other infections look similar, so see a health worker.

0.69
  A SRV-0085 [services_rights] What the 114 line is for: 114 is a free health information line run by Rwanda Biomedical Centre (RBC). You can call it to ask health questions, including about HIV, STIs, contraception and other SRH topics, and to find out where to get services. It is a good number to call if you are unsure where to go or want advice before visiting a health centre. In an emergency, call 912 or 112.
  B WEL-0062 [wellbeing] Calling 114: 114 is a free phone line run by the Rwanda Biomedical Centre (RBC). You can call it to ask health questions, including about stress, mental health, sexual and reproductive health, HIV and where to find services. You do not need airtime. The people who answer are trained to listen and guide you. If you or someone else is in immediate danger, call 112 instead.

0.69
  A CSB-0067 [contraception] Lighter periods with the hormonal IUD: The hormonal IUD usually makes periods lighter and less painful. In the first months you may have irregular spotting. Later, many users bleed very little, and some stop having periods. This is safe and not a sign of pregnancy or blood building up inside. If you have signs of pregnancy or severe belly pain, see a health worker.
  B CSB-0080 [contraception] Hormonal IUD side effects: With the hormonal IUD, the most common changes are irregular spotting in the first months, then lighter or no periods. Some people have acne, headaches, breast tenderness or mood changes, usually mild. Most effects get better with time. If an effect bothers you, talk to your health worker. Severe belly pain or fever needs care quickly.

0.69
  A CON-0148 [consent_safety] Supporting a friend who was abused: If a friend tells you they were abused, stay calm and listen. Say 'I believe you', 'It's not your fault' and 'Thank you for telling me'. Let them share at their own pace and do not push for details. Ask what they need, and help them reach a trusted adult or services like Isange (3029), 3512 or 116. If they are in danger or very hurt, call 112. Keep checking in, because support over time matters.
  B CON-0153 [consent_safety] Supporting a boy or man who was abused: Male survivors often face extra shame and fear of being mocked. If a boy or man tells you he was abused, take it seriously. Do not joke, question his manhood, or say he should have fought back. Remind him that it is not his fault and that abuse does not change who he is or his sexuality. Encourage him to get support from Isange (3029), 3512 or 116, and offer to go with him.

0.69
  A CSA-0174 [contraception] Washing or peeing after sex myth: Peeing, washing the vagina, jumping, or pushing after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm swim into the womb very quickly, within minutes, and urine comes out of a different opening. Washing inside the vagina can also cause infections. If you had unprotected sex, emergency contraception within 5 days is the right step.
  B HIV-0050 [hiv] Washing or peeing after sex does not prevent HIV: Washing your genitals, peeing, douching or using water, soap, salt or other products after sex does not prevent HIV or pregnancy. The virus can enter the body very quickly during sex. Washing inside the vagina can even irritate it and raise infection risk. If you had sex without a condom, go to a health center within 72 hours to ask about PEP and emergency contraception.

0.69
  A HIV-0128 [hiv] What ART is: ART stands for antiretroviral therapy. It is the medicine people living with HIV take every day to control the virus. ART stops HIV from making copies of itself, which lets the immune system recover and protects the person from getting sick. It does not cure HIV, but when taken well it allows people to live long, healthy lives and stops HIV from passing to sexual partners.
  B HIV-0130 [hiv] Why ART is for life: ART controls HIV but does not remove it from the body. The virus hides in some cells, and if you stop treatment it comes back and starts damaging the immune system again. That is why ART is taken every day for life. Many people find it becomes a normal daily habit, like brushing teeth. Your health worker will support you along the way.

0.69
  A STI-0019 [stis] What is syphilis: Syphilis is a sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It develops in stages. It often starts with a painless sore, then may cause a rash, and then can hide in the body for years without signs. If it is never treated, it can later damage the brain, nerves, eyes and heart. Syphilis can be found with a simple blood test and cured with the right medicine from a health worker.
  B STI-0026 [stis] Congenital syphilis: Congenital syphilis happens when a pregnant woman with syphilis passes it to her baby in the womb. It can cause miscarriage, stillbirth, early birth, low birth weight, death of the newborn, or babies born with infections and lifelong health problems. The good news is that it can be prevented: when pregnant women are tested early and treated, the baby is protected. Partners should also be tested and treated.

0.69
  A STI-0064 [stis] How trichomoniasis spreads: Trichomoniasis spreads mainly during vaginal sex without a condom, and through genital-to-genital contact, with someone who has the infection. It can pass from a man to a woman, a woman to a man, or between women. Because many people have no signs, they can pass it without knowing. It does not spread by hugging or sharing food. Using condoms every time lowers the risk.
  B STI-0065 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in girls and women: Many girls and women with trichomoniasis have no signs. When signs appear, they may include a frothy yellow-green or grey vaginal discharge, often with a strong or fishy smell, itching or soreness around the vagina, burning when urinating, and discomfort during sex. These signs can also come from other infections such as bacterial vaginosis or yeast, so only a test at a health center can tell the cause.

0.69
  A CSB-0070 [contraception] Rumour: IUD causes infertility: The IUD does not cause infertility. When it is removed, your fertility returns quickly, usually right away. This is true for both copper and hormonal IUDs and for young women who have never had a baby. The rumour may come from untreated infections, which can harm fertility; getting checked for STIs and using condoms helps prevent this.
  B CSB-0073 [contraception] Fertility after IUD removal: After an IUD is removed, your fertility returns right away. You can become pregnant in your very next cycle. If you do not want to get pregnant yet, start another method immediately. If you do want a baby, you can start trying; visiting a health worker for advice before pregnancy is helpful.

0.69
  A CSB-0021 [contraception] When the implant is due to expire: Check the removal date on your card. Plan to visit the health center before or on that date. If you want to keep using an implant, a new one can be inserted at the same visit when the old one is removed, so you stay protected. If you want a different method, start it at that visit. After the date passes, the implant may not protect you well, so use condoms until you get help.
  B CSB-0024 [contraception] Replacing an implant with a new one: When your implant is due, a trained provider can remove the old one and insert a new one at the same visit, often through the same small cut. This keeps you protected without a gap. You do not need a break between implants. If the date has already passed, still go; they may check that you are not pregnant before inserting the new one, and advise condoms for a few days.

0.69
  A CSA-0115 [contraception] Late or missed progestin-only pill: If you are more than 3 hours late or miss a progestin-only pill, take one as soon as you remember and keep taking them at the usual time. Use condoms or avoid sex for the next 2 days. If you had sex in the last few days, ask a pharmacist or nurse about emergency contraception. Your pack leaflet also explains what to do.
  B CSA-0119 [contraception] Starting the progestin-only pill: You can start progestin-only pills on any day if it is reasonably sure you are not pregnant. If you start within 5 days after your period begins, you are protected at once. At other times, use condoms or avoid sex for the first 2 days. Breastfeeding mothers will get specific advice from their health worker on when to start.

0.69
  A STI-0055 [stis] What is cervical cancer: Cervical cancer is a cancer that grows in the cervix, the lower part of the womb that opens into the vagina. It is one of the most common cancers in women in Africa, and almost all cases are caused by long-lasting HPV infection. It usually develops slowly over many years, which gives time to find and treat early changes. It can be prevented through HPV vaccination and regular screening, and treated if found early.
  B STI-0056 [stis] Signs of cervical cancer: Early cervical cancer usually has no signs, which is why screening is so important. Later signs can include bleeding between periods, after sex or after menopause, unusual or bad-smelling vaginal discharge, pain during sex, and pain in the lower back or belly. These signs are much more often caused by other problems, but they should always be checked by a health worker at a health center.

0.69
  A CSA-0114 [contraception] Taking progestin-only pills on time: Progestin-only pills must be taken at the same time every day, with no breaks between packs. For women who are not breastfeeding, taking a pill more than 3 hours late can reduce protection. Set a daily alarm and keep the pack where you see it. Breastfeeding women have a little more protection, but should still be on time.
  B CSA-0115 [contraception] Late or missed progestin-only pill: If you are more than 3 hours late or miss a progestin-only pill, take one as soon as you remember and keep taking them at the usual time. Use condoms or avoid sex for the next 2 days. If you had sex in the last few days, ask a pharmacist or nurse about emergency contraception. Your pack leaflet also explains what to do.

0.69
  A CSA-0129 [contraception] How effective EC pills are: Emergency contraceptive pills greatly reduce the chance of pregnancy but do not remove it. For example, if 100 women had unprotected sex once in the middle of their cycle, about 8 might become pregnant without EC, but only about 1 or 2 with EC pills. They work best when taken as soon as possible. The copper IUD is even more effective as EC.
  B CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.

0.69
  A CSA-0002 [contraception] How to take off a condom after sex: Right after ejaculation, while the penis is still hard, hold the rim of the condom against the base of the penis and pull out slowly so no semen leaks. Move away from your partner's genitals before you slide the condom off. Be careful not to spill anything on your partner. If you want to have sex again, always use a brand new condom. Wash your hands after handling a used condom.
  B CSA-0017 [contraception] Getting a condom out of the vagina: A condom cannot get lost inside the body, because the vagina ends at the cervix. Wash your hands, relax, squat or put one leg up, and gently reach in with your fingers to pull it out. Do not use sharp objects. If you cannot reach it, go to a health center where a nurse can remove it. Because semen may have leaked, think about emergency contraception and ask about HIV prevention.

0.69
  A PUB-0150 [puberty] Caring for the wound after circumcision: After circumcision, keep the dressing clean and dry and follow the health worker's instructions about when to remove it. Wear clean, loose-fitting underwear that supports the penis. Rest and avoid heavy work or sport for a few days. Wash your hands before touching the area. Go to all your follow-up visits. Healing usually takes about six weeks.
  B PUB-0151 [puberty] Waiting before sex after circumcision: After medical circumcision, you should not have sex or masturbate for at least six weeks, until the wound has fully healed. Sex before healing can open the wound and greatly increase the risk of getting or passing on HIV and other infections. Even after healing, keep using condoms, because circumcision only gives partial protection.

0.69
  A STI-0021 [stis] First stage of syphilis: painless sore: The first sign of syphilis is often a single sore, called a chancre. It usually appears around 3 weeks after infection where the bacteria entered, such as the penis, vagina, anus or mouth. It is usually firm, round and painless, so many people do not notice it. The sore heals on its own in a few weeks, but the infection stays in the body. Any genital sore should be checked at a health center.
  B STI-0023 [stis] Hidden and late stages of syphilis: After the early signs fade, syphilis can stay hidden in the body for many years with no symptoms. This is called latent syphilis. In some people who are never treated, it later causes late (tertiary) syphilis, which can seriously damage the brain, nerves, eyes, heart, blood vessels and bones. This damage can be life-threatening. Testing and treatment at any stage stops the infection from going further, so getting checked is always worth it.

0.69
  A PUB-0083 [puberty] What the vulva is: The vulva is the outside part of a girl's genitals, the part you can see between the legs. It includes the mons pubis (the soft area where pubic hair grows), the outer and inner lips (labia), the clitoris, the opening of the urethra where urine comes out, and the opening of the vagina. Many people call all of this the vagina, but the vagina is actually inside.
  B PUB-0087 [puberty] What the vagina is: The vagina is a stretchy, muscular tube inside a girl's body. It connects the vulva on the outside to the uterus (womb) inside. Menstrual blood flows out through it, and it is where a baby comes out during birth. It makes a little fluid, called discharge, which keeps it clean and healthy. The vagina is separate from the hole where urine comes out.

0.69
  A CSB-0038 [contraception] Getting the injection early: Yes. You can get your DMPA injection up to 2 weeks before your due date. This is useful if you will be travelling, going to boarding school or busy on the due date. Getting it a little early is safe and does not reduce protection. Talk to your provider and they will write the new date for your next injection on your card.
  B CSB-0048 [contraception] How soon the injection protects: If you get your first injection within 7 days after your period starts, it protects you right away. If you get it at another time, use condoms or avoid sex for the next 7 days. If you are switching from another hormonal method you used correctly, you may be protected right away. Your provider will tell you what applies to you.

0.69
  A STI-0019 [stis] What is syphilis: Syphilis is a sexually transmitted infection caused by bacteria. It develops in stages. It often starts with a painless sore, then may cause a rash, and then can hide in the body for years without signs. If it is never treated, it can later damage the brain, nerves, eyes and heart. Syphilis can be found with a simple blood test and cured with the right medicine from a health worker.
  B STI-0023 [stis] Hidden and late stages of syphilis: After the early signs fade, syphilis can stay hidden in the body for many years with no symptoms. This is called latent syphilis. In some people who are never treated, it later causes late (tertiary) syphilis, which can seriously damage the brain, nerves, eyes, heart, blood vessels and bones. This damage can be life-threatening. Testing and treatment at any stage stops the infection from going further, so getting checked is always worth it.

0.69
  A STI-0069 [stis] What is hepatitis B: Hepatitis B is an infection of the liver caused by a virus. It can cause a short illness, or it can stay in the body for life (chronic hepatitis B), slowly damaging the liver and sometimes leading to liver cancer. Many people have no signs for years. Hepatitis B can be prevented with a safe and effective vaccine, and chronic infection can be controlled with treatment from a health worker.
  B STI-0070 [stis] How hepatitis B spreads: Hepatitis B spreads through blood, semen and vaginal fluids. This can happen during sex without a condom, sharing razors, toothbrushes or needles, unsterile tattoos or piercing, and from a mother to her baby at birth. It does not spread through hugging, kissing on the cheek, sharing food or cups, coughing or breastfeeding. The vaccine, condoms and not sharing sharp objects protect you.

0.69
  A PRB-0002 [pregnancy] Why antenatal care matters: Antenatal care helps you and your baby stay healthy.
