Health workers can find problems early, like high blood pressure, anaemia, infections or a baby that is not growing well, and treat them before they become dangerous. You get vaccines, supplements and advice on food, rest and danger signs. Mothers who attend antenatal care regularly are more likely to have a safe birth and a healthy baby. Even if you feel fine, the check-ups are still important.
  B PRB-0022 [pregnancy] Keeping your antenatal care booklet: At antenatal care you usually receive a card or booklet where health workers write your test results, vaccines, blood pressure, the baby's growth and your due date. Keep it safe and dry, and bring it to every visit and when you go to give birth. It helps any health worker understand your pregnancy quickly, especially in an emergency. If you lose it, tell the health center so they can help replace the information.

0.69
  A STI-0092 [stis] What causes yeast infections: Yeast infections happen when the normal balance in the vagina changes and yeast grows too much. Things that can trigger this include taking antibiotics, pregnancy, uncontrolled diabetes, a weak immune system, hormonal changes, staying in wet or tight clothes, and washing inside the vagina or using scented soaps. Sometimes there is no clear reason. Yeast infections are not caused by poor morals or being unclean.
  B STI-0095 [stis] Preventing yeast infections: To lower the chance of yeast infections, wear clean cotton underwear, change out of wet or sweaty clothes soon, and avoid very tight clothes. Wash the outside of the genitals with clean water only and avoid scented soaps, sprays or douching inside the vagina. Wipe from front to back after using the toilet. Only take antibiotics when a health worker prescribes them. If infections keep coming back, get checked.

0.69
  A CON-0127 [consent_safety] The 3512 GBV hotline: 3512 is the toll-free gender-based violence hotline of the Rwanda National Police. Anyone can call it to report violence or abuse, including domestic violence, rape, child abuse, harassment or trafficking, or to ask for advice. You can call for yourself or to report something happening to someone else. In an emergency where someone is in danger right now, call 112.
  B SRV-0087 [services_rights] What the 3512 line is for: 3512 is a free line for reporting gender-based violence, such as rape, sexual assault, sexual harassment or violence from a partner or family member. You can call to report what happened and get guidance on where to get help. You can call for yourself or someone else. You do not need to wait to have proof. If someone is in danger right now, call 112.

0.69
  A CSA-0190 [contraception] Conditions for LAM to work: LAM only works when all three conditions are true: your monthly bleeding has not returned since birth; you are fully or nearly fully breastfeeding, often, day and night, without giving other foods or drinks regularly; and your baby is less than 6 months old. If any one of these changes, you need another method straight away.
  B CSA-0193 [contraception] Moving from LAM to another method: Plan your next method before LAM ends. Many methods are safe while breastfeeding, such as condoms, progestin-only pills, the implant, injections and IUDs. Combined pills are usually not used before 6 months while breastfeeding. Talk to a health worker during your baby's visits or at the health center so you are protected without a gap.

0.69
  A STI-0051 [stis] Why the HPV vaccine is given young: The HPV vaccine works best when given before a person is ever exposed to HPV, which means before they start any sexual activity. Young people's bodies also make a very strong immune response to the vaccine. Getting it at age 9 to 14 does not mean anyone expects the girl to have sex; it simply protects her for the future, like other childhood vaccines.
  B S-007 [stis] HPV vaccine: The HPV vaccine protects against the virus that can cause cervical cancer. Ask your school nurse or health center about the vaccine and who can get it.

0.69
  A HIV-0093 [hiv] Who PrEP is for: PrEP is medicine taken by HIV-negative people to prevent HIV. It is for people at higher risk, for example someone whose partner has HIV and is not virally suppressed, someone with several partners or who often has sex without condoms, someone who recently had an STI, or someone who sells sex or injects drugs. A health worker will talk with you to see if PrEP is right for you.
  B HIV-0111 [hiv] Telling a partner or family about PrEP: Whether you tell anyone about PrEP is your choice. Telling a partner can help you plan protection together and avoid misunderstandings if they see your pills. Telling a trusted family member may give you support to take it daily. Explain that PrEP is a prevention medicine for HIV-negative people. A counsellor can help you plan the conversation.

0.69
  A CSA-0182 [contraception] How effective fertility awareness is: Fertility awareness methods can work well for couples who use them carefully, but mistakes are common. For example, with the Standard Days Method about 12 out of 100 women get pregnant in a year with typical use, and about 5 with correct use. Other methods have similar or wider ranges. They are less effective than pills, injections, implants or IUDs.
  B CSA-0187 [contraception] Learning fertility awareness: To use fertility awareness methods well, it is best to learn from a trained health worker. They can check if a method suits your cycle, teach you how to track it, and show you how to use CycleBeads or record signs. Ask at your nearest health center or youth corner. Use condoms while you are learning.

0.69
  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B HIV-0058 [hiv] What an HIV self-test is: An HIV self-test lets you test yourself privately, using a kit with either a mouth swab (oral fluid) or a finger-prick blood drop. You do the test yourself and read the result in a short time, following the instructions. It is a screening test: a reactive result must be confirmed at a health facility, and a non-reactive result may need repeating if you had a recent risk. It is for people 16 and older in Rwanda.

0.69
  A CSB-0060 [contraception] Does IUD insertion hurt: Many people feel cramping or discomfort while the IUD is inserted, similar to period pain, and some feel more. It usually passes quickly. Breathing slowly and relaxing helps, and a provider can explain each step and stop if you need a moment. You can ask about pain relief. Some cramps for a day or two afterwards are normal. You can always ask questions before deciding.
  B CSB-0065 [contraception] Signs the IUD has come out: Sometimes an IUD partly or fully comes out, most often in the first months or during a period. Signs include not feeling the strings, feeling hard plastic at the cervix, strings longer than before, unusual cramping or bleeding, or seeing the IUD on a pad. If you notice any of these, use condoms and go to your health center so a provider can check and replace it if needed.

0.69
  A CSB-0006 [contraception] Bruising and soreness after implant insertion: It is common to have some bruising, swelling or tenderness where the implant was inserted. This usually gets better within a week or two without treatment. You can keep the area clean and avoid pressing on it. Bruising looks blue or purple and then fades to yellow, which is normal healing. If the pain gets worse instead of better, or you see signs like pus or the area becomes hot and red, see a nurse at your health center.
  B CSB-0007 [contraception] Infection signs at the implant site: Infection at the implant site is uncommon, but watch for these signs: redness that spreads, heat, pus or fluid coming out, pain that gets worse after the first few days, or fever. If you notice any of these, go back to your health center soon. Do not try to treat it yourself or remove the implant. A nurse can clean the area and give the right care. Getting help early usually solves the problem.

0.69
  A WEL-0001 [wellbeing] Worry after unprotected sex: Feeling scared after sex without a condom is very common, and worrying will not change what happened, but acting quickly can help. Within a few days, a health center can talk with you about emergency contraception, testing for HIV and other infections, and medicine that can prevent HIV if you go very soon. Try to slow your breathing and talk to someone you trust. You are not a bad person. You can also call 114 for free, private advice about what to do next.
  B PR-006 [pregnancy] Worried about pregnancy after sex without protection: Pregnancy is possible after sex without protection. Emergency contraceptive pills can lower the risk if taken as soon as possible, ideally within 3 days and up to 5 days. Go to a health center or pharmacy soon. If your period is more than 7 days late afterwards, take a pregnancy test.

0.69
  A PUB-0061 [puberty] Oily hair and scalp: During puberty the glands in your scalp make more oil, so your hair may look greasy faster or you may get dandruff. Washing your hair regularly with a mild shampoo or soap and rinsing well helps. Avoid putting heavy oils near your forehead, as they can cause pimples. If your scalp is very itchy, flaky, red or has sores, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0069 [puberty] Oily skin: Oily or shiny skin is common in puberty because hormones make oil glands more active. Wash your face gently with mild soap and water twice a day and after sport. Pat dry with a clean towel. Avoid putting thick oils or petroleum jelly on your face. Do not wash too often or scrub hard, because that can irritate the skin and make it produce even more oil.

0.69
  A PUB-0150 [puberty] Caring for the wound after circumcision: After circumcision, keep the dressing clean and dry and follow the health worker's instructions about when to remove it. Wear clean, loose-fitting underwear that supports the penis. Rest and avoid heavy work or sport for a few days. Wash your hands before touching the area. Go to all your follow-up visits. Healing usually takes about six weeks.
  B PUB-0152 [puberty] Danger signs after circumcision: Go back to the health facility immediately if, after circumcision, you have bleeding that soaks the dressing, pain that keeps getting worse, a lot of swelling, pus or a bad smell from the wound, fever, or difficulty passing urine. These can be signs of bleeding or infection that need quick treatment. If you cannot reach the clinic, call 112 or 912.

0.69
  A PRA-0051 [pregnancy] Washing the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy: Washing or rinsing inside the vagina after sex does not prevent pregnancy. Sperm can reach the cervix and womb within minutes, before you can wash. Putting water, soap, salt, lemon, soda or other liquids inside the vagina can also hurt the skin and cause infections. Just wash the outside gently with water. If you are worried about pregnancy, see a health worker.
  B PRB-0049 [pregnancy] Personal hygiene in pregnancy: Keeping clean helps prevent infections during pregnancy. Bathe daily with clean water and mild soap, and wash your genital area from front to back with plain water; do not wash inside the vagina or use strong soaps, sprays or herbs there. Wear clean, loose cotton underwear and change it daily. Wash your hands with soap after using the toilet and before eating or cooking. Tell a health worker about itching, smelly discharge or sores.

0.69
  A HIV-0006 [hiv] Why symptoms cannot tell you if you have HIV: You cannot know if you have HIV by checking how you feel. Early signs like fever, rash or sore throat are the same as many common illnesses such as flu or malaria, and many people with HIV have no signs at all for years. Feeling healthy does not mean you are HIV negative, and feeling sick does not mean you are positive. The only way to know is to take an HIV test at a health center or youth corner.
  B HIV-0008 [hiv] Signs of advanced HIV: When HIV is not treated for a long time, the immune system becomes weak. Then a person may have signs such as losing a lot of weight, long-lasting fever, night sweats, diarrhoea that does not stop, a cough for weeks, or repeated infections. These signs can also come from other illnesses like TB, so they do not prove HIV. Anyone with these signs should go to a health center soon to be checked and tested.

0.69
  A CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.
  B CSA-0151 [contraception] When you need EC: You may need emergency contraception after sex with no method, when a condom broke, slipped or leaked, after missing several pills, being late for an injection, if withdrawal failed, if you were forced to have sex, or if you made a mistake with any method. If you are not sure, ask a pharmacist or health worker quickly, or call 114.

0.69
  A PRB-0003 [pregnancy] When to start antenatal care: Start antenatal care as early as possible, ideally in the first 12 weeks (first three months) of pregnancy. Go as soon as you think you are pregnant or have a positive test. Starting early lets the health worker confirm how far along you are, check your health, offer important tests and give supplements that help the baby's early development. If you are already further along, it is never too late. Go to your nearest health center now.
  B PRB-0004 [pregnancy] How many antenatal care contacts are recommended: WHO recommends at least eight antenatal care contacts during pregnancy. The first should be in the first 12 weeks, then around 20 and 26 weeks, then more often near the end of pregnancy, at about 30, 34, 36, 38 and 40 weeks. Your health center may use its own schedule, so ask them when to come back. If you have a health problem, you may need extra visits. Write down each appointment date so you do not miss it.

0.69
  A HIV-0074 [hiv] Couples HIV testing: Couples HIV testing means you and your partner test together and receive your results together with a counsellor. It helps you both know your status, plan how to protect each other, and support each other whatever the results. It also builds trust. Each person still has the right to decide. Ask at your health center or youth corner about testing together.
  B HIV-0089 [hiv] Giving an HIV self-test kit to a partner: If you are 16 or older and got a self-test kit, you can offer one to your partner, if they are also 16 or older, so they can test privately. This can help partners who are shy about going to a clinic. Never force or trick someone into testing; it must be their choice. If their result is reactive, they should go to a health facility to confirm.

0.69
  A PUB-0127 [puberty] Getting hit in the testicles: Being hit or kicked in the testicles hurts a lot, and you may feel sick for a short time. Usually the pain fades within an hour. Lie down and use a cool cloth on the area. Go to a health facility if pain is severe or does not settle within an hour, if there is a lot of swelling or bruising, blood in the urine, or fever. Wear protective gear or supportive underwear during sport.
  B STI-0111 [stis] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle is an emergency. It can be testicular torsion, where the testicle twists and loses its blood supply, which can destroy the testicle within hours. Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.

0.69
  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B SRV-0071 [services_rights] HIV testing for children under 12: For children under 12, a parent or guardian usually needs to give consent for an HIV test. If you are younger than 12 and worried about HIV, talk to a trusted adult, such as a parent, relative, teacher or community health worker, who can go with you to the health centre. You can also call 114 or 116 for advice. Health workers will support you kindly.

0.69
  A CON-0066 [consent_safety] What is grooming: Grooming is when someone builds trust and an emotional connection with a child or young person, and sometimes their family, so they can later abuse or exploit them. A groomer may give gifts, attention, compliments, money or special favours, then slowly introduce secrecy, sexual talk or touching. It can happen in person or online and can take weeks or months. Grooming is a form of abuse. If something feels wrong, tell a trusted adult or call 116.
  B CON-0070 [consent_safety] Grooming the family: Groomers often also win the trust of parents and family. They may help with money or school fees, offer lifts, babysit, or become a 'family friend' so they can spend time alone with a child without suspicion. Families then may not believe the child if something happens. Parents can protect children by noticing adults who seek a lot of alone time with one child and by listening carefully. Concerns can be shared with 116 or 3512.

0.69
  A MEN-0099 [menstruation] Pain medicine for period cramps: Common pain medicines sold in pharmacies, such as ibuprofen or paracetamol, can ease period cramps for many people. Ask a pharmacist or health worker which one is right for you and how much to take for your age and weight, and always follow the instructions on the packet. Some work best taken when pain first starts, with food. Do not take medicines prescribed for someone else. If pain medicine does not help, see a health worker.
  B M-006 [menstruation] Period pain and cramps: Pain in the lower back or stomach during a period is common. To feel better you can move and stretch, do some exercise, put a hot compress on the painful area or have a warm bath. Ibuprofen may help if you have it. If the pain is very strong or stops you from doing your normal activities, see a health worker.

0.69
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0095 [hiv] Where to get PrEP in Rwanda: PrEP is offered at health facilities in Rwanda that provide HIV services. Ask a nurse at your health center or youth corner. They will talk with you about your risk, test you for HIV, and check if PrEP is right for you. Not every site may offer it, so if your health center does not, they can refer you. You can also call 114 to ask where PrEP is available.

0.69
  A CON-0135 [consent_safety] What happens when you report abuse: When abuse is reported, the person who reports is listened to, and the survivor's safety is checked first. They may be referred to Isange or a health facility for medical care and counselling. Police or RIB will take a statement and may investigate. For children, social workers or child protection staff help make sure the child is safe. Survivors can ask questions at every step and bring a trusted person. Reporting starts with 3512, 116, 3029 or 112.
  B CON-0138 [consent_safety] Reporting abuse for someone else: Yes, you can report abuse even if it is happening to someone else, such as a friend, sibling, classmate or neighbour's child. You do not need proof; you only need a genuine worry. Share what you saw or heard. It is best to tell the person you are getting help, if it is safe. Call 116 for children, 3512 for GBV, 3029 for Isange, or 112 if someone is in immediate danger.

0.69
  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B HIV-0141 [hiv] Is HIV treatment free in Rwanda: Yes. HIV treatment (ART), viral load tests and routine HIV care are provided free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda. You do not need to pay for your ARVs. If you face any problem getting your medicine, talk to the health facility or call 114.

0.69
  A CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.
  B C-004 [contraception] Male condoms: Condoms are worn on the penis and block sperm and infections. They are the only method that protects against both pregnancy and STIs, including HIV. Used correctly every time, about 2 in 100 women get pregnant in a year; as commonly used, about 13 in 100. Use a new condom each time and talk about it with your partner before sex. Using two condoms at once does not protect more and can make them break.

0.69
  A CON-0037 [consent_safety] Body privacy during medical exams: Sometimes a health worker needs to examine private parts for a health reason. A good health worker will explain what they are doing and why, ask for your permission, and allow a parent, carer or another staff member to be present. You can ask questions, ask for a female or male provider if possible, and say if something hurts or feels wrong. An exam should never be secret or involve sexual comments. If something felt wrong, tell a trusted adult or call 116 or 3512.
  B SRV-0039 [services_rights] Physical examinations and your consent: Not every SRH visit needs a physical examination. If the nurse or doctor thinks one is needed, they should explain why, what they will do and ask for your agreement first. You can ask for a female or male provider, ask for another person to be present, ask them to stop at any time, or say no. Many services, like condoms, pills and most counselling, do not need an exam at all.

0.69
  A HIV-0093 [hiv] Who PrEP is for: PrEP is medicine taken by HIV-negative people to prevent HIV. It is for people at higher risk, for example someone whose partner has HIV and is not virally suppressed, someone with several partners or who often has sex without condoms, someone who recently had an STI, or someone who sells sex or injects drugs. A health worker will talk with you to see if PrEP is right for you.
  B HIV-0107 [hiv] PrEP when your partner has HIV: If your partner has HIV, PrEP can help protect you, especially while your partner is starting treatment or if their viral load is not yet undetectable. When your partner takes treatment well and is undetectable, HIV is not passed through sex. Talk with a health worker together about the best plan, which may include PrEP, condoms, and regular testing.

0.69
  A CSA-0153 [contraception] Medicines that affect EC: Some medicines, such as certain medicines for epilepsy, TB (rifampicin) and some HIV medicines, can make emergency pills work less well. If you take any regular medicine, tell the pharmacist or health worker when you ask for EC. They may suggest a different approach or the copper IUD, which is not affected by medicines.
  B CSA-0155 [contraception] Ordinary pills as EC: Some ordinary combined pills can be used as emergency contraception in a special way, but the right number depends on the brand, and they cause more nausea than dedicated EC pills. Do not try this on your own. Ask a pharmacist or health worker; dedicated emergency pills or a copper IUD are better choices if available.

0.69
  A PRA-0046 [pregnancy] Wet dreams and pregnancy: A wet dream is when a boy ejaculates while sleeping. It is a normal part of puberty. A boy's wet dream cannot make anyone pregnant unless his fresh semen gets into or right at a girl's vagina, which does not happen when he is simply asleep in his own bed. A wet dream does mean his body is making sperm and could cause pregnancy during sex.
  B PU-003 [puberty] Wet dreams and sudden erections: A wet dream is when a boy ejaculates while he sleeps. Sudden erections are also common in teenage boys, even many times a day. Both are normal and harmless, and part of growing up. Erections go away by themselves. They do not mean a boy needs to have sex.

0.69
  A MEN-0010 [menstruation] Why first periods may be light or brown: Your very first periods are often light, short, or brown in colour rather than bright red. This is because your hormones are just starting to work together and the womb lining may be thin. Brown blood is simply older blood that took longer to leave the body. Over the next months your flow often becomes more regular. If you have questions or the bleeding is very heavy, you can talk to a nurse at a health center.
  B MEN-0012 [menstruation] Dark brown or black period blood: Period blood can be bright red, dark red, brown or almost black, and all of these can be normal. Dark blood is usually older blood that stayed in the womb or vagina a little longer before coming out, so it has changed colour. This is common at the start or the end of a period, or in the morning after lying down. See a health worker if dark bleeding comes with a bad smell, fever, belly pain or itching.

0.68
  A PRB-0067 [pregnancy] Vaginal discharge in pregnancy: It is normal to have more vaginal discharge during pregnancy. Healthy discharge is thin, clear or white and has little smell. Wear clean cotton underwear and avoid washing inside the vagina. See a health worker if the discharge is yellow, green or grey, smells bad, is thick like curds, or comes with itching, burning or pain, as it may be an infection. A sudden gush or steady leak of watery fluid needs care immediately.
  B M-007 [menstruation] Vaginal discharge: Clear, cloudy white or yellowish discharge between periods is normal. Changes in your discharge, a new bad smell, itching or a burning feeling can be signs of infection. If you notice these, please see a health worker.

0.68
  A CSA-0054 [contraception] Female condoms and HIV/STI protection: Yes. When used correctly every time, the female condom protects against HIV and other STIs, because it stops semen and body fluids passing between partners. It also covers part of the outer genitals. Like male condoms, it gives less protection against infections spread by skin contact outside the covered area. Use a new one every time.
  B STI-0047 [stis] Condoms and HPV: Condoms lower the chance of getting HPV and genital warts, but they do not give full protection, because HPV can live on skin that a condom does not cover, like the scrotum, vulva or thighs. Using condoms every time is still very worth it, because they also protect against many other STIs, HIV and pregnancy. Combining condoms with the HPV vaccine and fewer partners gives the best protection.

0.68
  A MEN-0068 [menstruation] Period underwear: Period underwear looks like normal underwear but has special absorbent layers in the crotch that soak up blood and keep you feeling dry. Some can be worn alone on light days, while others are used as backup with a pad or cup. They can be washed and reused many times. They cost more at first and can be hard to find, and you need enough pairs and time to wash and dry them between uses.
  B MEN-0088 [menstruation] How often to change underwear during a period: Change into clean underwear at least once a day during your period, and change it sooner if blood leaks onto it or it feels damp. Cotton underwear is best because it lets air through and is easy to wash. Wash used underwear in cold water first to remove blood, then with soap. Dry it in the sun. Having a spare pair in your bag is helpful in case of leaks.

0.68
  A CSB-0058 [contraception] Copper IUD vs hormonal IUD: Both are placed in the womb and are very effective. The copper IUD has no hormones and lasts up to 12 years, but may make periods heavier or more painful, especially at first. The hormonal IUD releases a small amount of progestin, lasts several years, and usually makes periods lighter, sometimes stopping them. Neither protects against STIs. A health worker can help you compare them for your needs.
  B C-008 [contraception] IUD (coil): The copper IUD is a small device placed in the womb by a trained provider. It protects against pregnancy for up to 12 years and can be removed at any time. Periods may become heavier with more cramps in the first 3 to 6 months. It does not protect against STIs. IUDs can come out more often in women who have never given birth, so check with a health worker.

0.68
  A CSA-0147 [contraception] Taking EC before sex: Emergency contraceptive pills are meant to be taken after unprotected sex, not before. Taking them before sex does not give reliable protection for sex in the coming days. If you know you will have sex, use a condom or a regular method. Keep EC only as a backup for accidents.
  B CSA-0150 [contraception] Keeping EC at home in advance: Some people choose to get emergency contraceptive pills before they need them and keep them at home. This is safe and means you can take them quickly if a condom breaks or you have unprotected sex, which makes them work better. Check the expiry date and store them in a cool, dry place. Ask a pharmacist or health worker.

0.68
  A CON-0057 [consent_safety] Boys can be sexually abused: Boys and young men can be sexually abused, by men or by women. It is just as serious as abuse of girls. Boys often stay silent because of shame, fear of being laughed at, or myths that 'boys can't be victims' or 'he should be happy'. These ideas are false and harmful. Being abused does not change a boy's manhood or sexuality. Boys deserve to be believed and helped. They can call 116, 3512 or go to Isange (3029).
  B CON-0160 [consent_safety] Myth: boys and men cannot be raped: It is a myth that boys and men cannot be raped or sexually abused. Boys and men can be assaulted by men or women. Some people think a male body reaction means he wanted it, or that real men can always fight back. These beliefs are wrong and keep male survivors silent. Male survivors deserve to be believed and supported. They can get care at Isange (3029) and call 3512 or 116.

0.68
  A CSA-0001 [contraception] How to put on a male condom: First check the expiry date and make sure the packet is not torn. Open it gently with your fingers, never with teeth, scissors or a blade. Put the condom on when the penis is hard and before it touches your partner's genitals. Check that the rim is on the outside so it rolls down easily. Pinch the tip to squeeze out air, then roll it all the way down to the base. If you start putting it on the wrong way, throw it away and use a new one.
  B CSA-0002 [contraception] How to take off a condom after sex: Right after ejaculation, while the penis is still hard, hold the rim of the condom against the base of the penis and pull out slowly so no semen leaks. Move away from your partner's genitals before you slide the condom off. Be careful not to spill anything on your partner. If you want to have sex again, always use a brand new condom. Wash your hands after handling a used condom.

0.68
  A HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.
  B HIV-0087 [hiv] Telling my parents I took an HIV test: You do not have to tell your parents that you tested for HIV; it is your choice and your result is private. Still, many young people find it helpful to have support from a trusted adult, especially if the result is positive. If you want to tell them, a counsellor can help you plan what to say or even be with you when you talk. Choose someone you feel safe with.

0.68
  A STI-0046 [stis] HPV testing: An HPV test checks for high-risk types of HPV in cells from the cervix. It is used for cervical cancer screening in adult women, not for teenagers, because HPV in young people usually goes away by itself. The sample can be taken by a health worker or, in some programmes, by the woman herself. There is no routine HPV test for men. Ask at a health center about screening when you reach the recommended age.
  B STI-0054 [stis] Screening after the HPV vaccine: Yes, women who had the HPV vaccine still need cervical cancer screening when they reach the recommended age. The vaccine protects against the types of HPV that cause most cervical cancer, but not every type. Screening finds early changes in the cervix so they can be treated before they become cancer. Vaccination when young plus screening as an adult gives the strongest protection.

0.68
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B SRV-0070 [services_rights] Age to test for HIV without a parent: According to Rwanda's HIV Guidelines, young people aged 12 and above can agree to an HIV test themselves, without needing a parent's permission. You will get counselling before and after the test, and your result is kept confidential. HIV testing is offered at health centres and some youth centres. Knowing your status is a smart, brave step for your health.

0.68
  A HIV-0058 [hiv] What an HIV self-test is: An HIV self-test lets you test yourself privately, using a kit with either a mouth swab (oral fluid) or a finger-prick blood drop. You do the test yourself and read the result in a short time, following the instructions. It is a screening test: a reactive result must be confirmed at a health facility, and a non-reactive result may need repeating if you had a recent risk. It is for people 16 and older in Rwanda.
  B H-004 [hiv] HIV testing: Testing is voluntary and private. In Rwanda, people aged 12 and above can agree to a test themselves, and for children aged 10 to 11 a parent or guardian agrees. A self-test kit is for people aged 16 and above and is sold at health facilities and pharmacies. A positive self-test must be confirmed by a health worker.

0.68
  A REL-0057 [relationships] When you think your partner is cheating: If you suspect your partner is cheating, try not to act on rumours alone or spy on their phone. Notice what is making you worried, then talk calmly and honestly with them about your feelings and listen to their answer. If trust is gone and you still feel unsure, think about whether the relationship gives you peace. If you have had sex, getting tested for HIV and STIs at a health center is a good idea.
  B REL-0058 [relationships] Your partner cheated on you: Learning that your partner cheated can be very painful. You may feel angry, sad or embarrassed; these feelings are normal, and it is not your fault. Take time before deciding what to do. Some couples try to rebuild trust; others decide to end the relationship. Both choices are valid. Talk to someone you trust. If you have had sex with this partner, visit a health center for HIV and STI testing.

0.68
  A STI-0111 [stis] Sudden testicle pain: Sudden, strong pain in a testicle is an emergency. It can be testicular torsion, where the testicle twists and loses its blood supply, which can destroy the testicle within hours. Go to a health facility immediately, or call 112 or 912, especially if there is swelling, nausea or vomiting. Do not wait to see if it goes away. Milder pain can be from an infection and also needs checking soon.
  B WEL-0136 [wellbeing] Injury to the testicles: Getting hit in the testicles, for example in football, is very painful, and mild pain usually settles within an hour. Rest, lie down and use a cool cloth wrapped around ice for short periods. Seek care quickly if pain is severe or does not improve, there is swelling, bruising, blood in urine, nausea or vomiting, as the testicle may be damaged. Wearing protection in contact sports helps prevent injury.

0.68
  A WEL-0021 [wellbeing] Feeling guilty after sex: Feeling guilty after sex is common, especially if it went against your values, happened quickly, or you worry what others would think. Guilt is a feeling, not a sign that you are a bad person. Think about what you want for yourself next time, and remember you can decide to wait. Make sure you are safe: if there was no condom, visit a health center soon. Talking to a trusted person or calling 114 can help.
  B WEL-0023 [wellbeing] Regret after first sex: Some young people regret their first sexual experience because it happened too soon, with the wrong person, or not how they imagined. Regret is painful, but it does not define you. Having had sex once does not mean you must continue; you can choose to wait until you feel ready. Look after your health: visit a health center if you had no protection. If it was not your choice, that is not your fault and you deserve support.

0.68
  A HIV-0125 [hiv] Does PEP always work: PEP greatly lowers the chance of getting HIV when it is started early and taken every day for all 28 days, but it is not 100 percent effective. It works best when started within hours, not days. That is why follow-up HIV tests are needed after finishing. Keep using condoms during and after PEP until your follow-up tests are done.
  B H-007 [hiv] PEP after possible exposure: PEP is a 28-day course of HIV medicine that lowers the chance of infection after possible exposure, for example after rape or a condom failing with a partner living with HIV. Start it as soon as possible, ideally within 72 hours. Go to a health facility right away.

0.68
  A HIV-0054 [hiv] Where to get an HIV test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get an HIV test at public health centers, hospitals and many health posts, including youth corners that are friendly to young people. Some places also offer self-test kits. A nurse or counsellor will explain the test and keep your result private. You can ask a community health worker where the nearest testing place is, or call the free line 114 for information.
  B HIV-0056 [hiv] Testing for HIV without a parent: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, young people aged 12 and older can agree to an HIV test by themselves, without needing permission from a parent or guardian. A counsellor will talk with you first to make sure you understand the test and are ready. Your result is confidential. You may still choose to bring a trusted adult for support if you want.

0.68
  A CSA-0113 [contraception] Who can use progestin-only pills: Almost all women and girls can use progestin-only pills, including those who are breastfeeding, those who smoke, those with high blood pressure that is controlled, and those who cannot take estrogen. A health worker will ask a few questions to check they are right for you, for example about current breast cancer or certain liver diseases.
  B CSA-0117 [contraception] How effective progestin-only pills are: For women who are not breastfeeding, progestin-only pills work about as well as combined pills: with typical use, about 7 in 100 women get pregnant in a year, and fewer than 1 in 100 with perfect use. For breastfeeding women they are even more effective. Taking every pill on time is the key.

0.68
  A HIV-0053 [hiv] Is being faithful enough to prevent HIV: Being faithful to one partner lowers HIV risk only if both of you tested negative and both of you stay faithful. If one partner had earlier partners and has not tested, or has sex with others, the risk remains. You cannot be sure of another person's choices. The safest plan is to test together, keep testing, and use condoms until you both know your status.
  B HIV-0076 [hiv] When one partner has HIV and the other does not: A couple where one partner has HIV and the other does not is called a discordant couple, and it is more common than people think. Such couples can stay together and have a healthy relationship. When the partner with HIV takes treatment and keeps an undetectable viral load, HIV is not passed through sex. The negative partner can also use PrEP and condoms, and test regularly. A counsellor can support you both.

0.68
  A WEL-0062 [wellbeing] Calling 114: 114 is a free phone line run by the Rwanda Biomedical Centre (RBC). You can call it to ask health questions, including about stress, mental health, sexual and reproductive health, HIV and where to find services. You do not need airtime. The people who answer are trained to listen and guide you. If you or someone else is in immediate danger, call 112 instead.
  B WEL-0069 [wellbeing] When to call 112 instead of 114: Call 112 when someone is in immediate danger and needs police or emergency help now, for example violence, someone unconscious, an overdose, severe injury or someone at risk of harming themselves. Call 114, the free RBC health line, for health questions, advice and support when it is not an emergency, such as stress, worries about sex or where to find services. If unsure and someone could be hurt, call 112.

0.68
  A PUB-0003 [puberty] Puberty starting very early: If a girl shows breast growth or pubic hair before about age 8, or a boy shows genital growth, pubic hair or a deep voice before about age 9, this is called early puberty. Sometimes it is just the child's natural timing, but sometimes there is a medical cause that can be treated. Please take the child to a health center so a nurse or doctor can check. It is not the child's fault.
  B PUB-0009 [puberty] First sign of puberty in girls: For most girls the first sign of puberty is breast budding: a small, firm bump under one or both nipples, which can feel a bit sore. Soon after, hair may start to grow around the genitals and under the arms. A growth spurt follows, and the first period usually comes about two years after breasts start growing. Some girls notice pubic hair first, which is also normal.

0.68
  A PUB-0038 [puberty] Breast growth in boys: Many boys get a little breast swelling during puberty, on one or both sides. It is caused by normal hormone changes and is common. It can feel tender. In most boys it goes away on its own within months to about two years. It does not mean you are turning into a girl. If it is very large, keeps growing, or you are worried, see a health worker.
  B PUB-0040 [puberty] Do breasts keep growing after puberty: Breasts usually finish most of their growth by the late teens, but they can keep changing a little after that. Breast size can change with weight gain or loss, during your monthly cycle, during pregnancy and breastfeeding, and with age. So it is normal if your breasts look a little different from year to year. Sudden changes in one breast only should be checked by a health worker.

0.68
  A CSB-0065 [contraception] Signs the IUD has come out: Sometimes an IUD partly or fully comes out, most often in the first months or during a period. Signs include not feeling the strings, feeling hard plastic at the cervix, strings longer than before, unusual cramping or bleeding, or seeing the IUD on a pad. If you notice any of these, use condoms and go to your health center so a provider can check and replace it if needed.
  B CSB-0072 [contraception] How an IUD is removed: A trained provider removes an IUD by gently pulling on the strings with a small instrument. It usually takes only a minute or two. You may feel a short cramp. You can ask for removal at any time and for any reason. Never try to remove it yourself. If you want to stay protected, you can start a new method the same day.

0.68
  A CSA-0156 [contraception] Keeping a copper IUD after using it as EC: If you get a copper IUD as emergency contraception, you can keep it as your regular method. It protects against pregnancy for many years and can be removed by a provider whenever you want. If you prefer another method, the IUD can be taken out at a later visit after the provider confirms you are not pregnant.
  B CSB-0056 [contraception] How long a copper IUD lasts: A copper IUD can protect you from pregnancy for up to 12 years. You do not need to keep it that long; you can have it removed whenever you want, for any reason. Your provider will write the date on your card. When it expires, it can be removed and replaced with a new one at the same visit if you wish.

0.68
  A CSA-0064 [contraception] Hormones in combined pills: Combined pills contain two hormones: an estrogen and a progestin. These are man-made versions of hormones the body makes naturally. Today's pills have low amounts of hormones and are safe for most women and girls. This is different from progestin-only pills (mini-pills), which contain only a progestin and are often used by breastfeeding women.
  B CSA-0112 [contraception] Difference between combined and progestin-only pills: Combined pills contain two hormones, estrogen and progestin; progestin-only pills contain only progestin. Progestin-only pills are taken every day with no break, and timing is stricter. They are suitable for breastfeeding women and for some people who cannot use estrogen. Combined pills often make bleeding more regular, while progestin-only pills more often cause irregular bleeding.

0.68
  A CSA-0116 [contraception] Bleeding changes on progestin-only pills: Changes in bleeding are common with progestin-only pills. You may have irregular bleeding, spotting, more frequent bleeding, or sometimes no bleeding at all. These are not harmful. Breastfeeding women often have no periods. If bleeding is very heavy or long, or you think you might be pregnant, see a health worker.
  B CSA-0120 [contraception] Side effects of progestin-only pills: Besides bleeding changes, some people on progestin-only pills have headaches, dizziness, mood changes, sore breasts or stomach pain. These are usually mild and not harmful. Severe stomach pain could be a sign of a pregnancy outside the womb, which is rare but serious; go to a health facility straight away if that happens.

0.68
  A CSA-0002 [contraception] How to take off a condom after sex: Right after ejaculation, while the penis is still hard, hold the rim of the condom against the base of the penis and pull out slowly so no semen leaks. Move away from your partner's genitals before you slide the condom off. Be careful not to spill anything on your partner. If you want to have sex again, always use a brand new condom. Wash your hands after handling a used condom.
  B CSA-0013 [contraception] When to put on a condom during sex: Put the condom on as soon as the penis is hard and before it touches the vagina, anus or mouth of your partner. Pre-ejaculate (pre-cum) that comes out before ejaculation can carry sperm and infections like HIV. Putting the condom on only just before ejaculating is not safe. Keep it on from start to finish for every act of sex.

0.68
  A CSA-0126 [contraception] Using EC more than once: Yes, you can use emergency contraceptive pills more than once, and every time you need them. Using EC again is safe and does not cause infertility, cancer or other long-term harm. But if you often need EC, a regular method like pills, an injection, an implant or an IUD will protect you better. A health worker can help you choose.
  B CSA-0128 [contraception] Why EC is not for regular use: Emergency contraception is safe, but it is meant as a backup, not an everyday method. It is less effective than regular methods like pills, injections, implants or IUDs, and using it often can make your bleeding irregular. If you have sex regularly, ask a health worker about a method that protects you all the time. Condoms also protect against STIs.

0.68
  A CSB-0080 [contraception] Hormonal IUD side effects: With the hormonal IUD, the most common changes are irregular spotting in the first months, then lighter or no periods. Some people have acne, headaches, breast tenderness or mood changes, usually mild. Most effects get better with time. If an effect bothers you, talk to your health worker. Severe belly pain or fever needs care quickly.
  B CSB-0094 [contraception] Common side effects of hormonal methods: Hormonal methods like the implant, injection and hormonal IUD can cause bleeding changes, headaches, breast tenderness, nausea, mood changes or acne. Not everyone gets these, and they are usually mild and not dangerous. Many improve within the first few months. Side effects are different from one person to another. Talk to your health worker about what to expect before you start.

0.68
  A SRV-0089 [services_rights] What the 112 line is for: 112 is Rwanda's emergency number for police help. Call it if you or someone else is in danger right now, for example if someone is being attacked, threatened or hurt, or if there is a serious emergency. Tell them clearly where you are and what is happening. For medical emergencies you can also call 912 for an ambulance.
  B WEL-0069 [wellbeing] When to call 112 instead of 114: Call 112 when someone is in immediate danger and needs police or emergency help now, for example violence, someone unconscious, an overdose, severe injury or someone at risk of harming themselves. Call 114, the free RBC health line, for health questions, advice and support when it is not an emergency, such as stress, worries about sex or where to find services. If unsure and someone could be hurt, call 112.

0.68
  A CSA-0136 [contraception] Copper IUD as emergency contraception: A copper IUD put into the womb within 5 days after unprotected sex is the most effective emergency contraception, preventing more than 99 out of 100 possible pregnancies. A trained provider at a health facility puts it in. It works even for people for whom pills may work less well. After that, it can stay in as a long-term method.
  B C-008 [contraception] IUD (coil): The copper IUD is a small device placed in the womb by a trained provider. It protects against pregnancy for up to 12 years and can be removed at any time. Periods may become heavier with more cramps in the first 3 to 6 months. It does not protect against STIs. IUDs can come out more often in women who have never given birth, so check with a health worker.

0.68
  A STI-0065 [stis] Trichomoniasis signs in girls and women: Many girls and women with trichomoniasis have no signs. When signs appear, they may include a frothy yellow-green or grey vaginal discharge, often with a strong or fishy smell, itching or soreness around the vagina, burning when urinating, and discomfort during sex. These signs can also come from other infections such as bacterial vaginosis or yeast, so only a test at a health center can tell the cause.
  B STI-0067 [stis] Testing and cure for trichomoniasis: Trichomoniasis can be found by testing a swab from the vagina or a urine sample, depending on what the health facility has. Sometimes the nurse treats based on signs. Trich is curable with medicine from a health worker. Take all the medicine, avoid alcohol if the nurse tells you to, avoid sex until you and your partner have both finished treatment, and go back if signs return.

0.68
  A HIV-0063 [hiv] How a rapid HIV test is done: A rapid HIV test at a health facility usually uses a small drop of blood from a finger prick. The health worker puts the blood on a test strip and you get the result in a short time, often during the same visit. Before and after the test, a counsellor talks with you, answers your questions, and explains what the result means. It is quick, safe and private.
  B HIV-0064 [hiv] How long HIV test results take: With a rapid HIV test at a health facility, results are usually ready in a short time, often within the same visit, so you can wait and get them with your counsellor. If more tests are needed to confirm a result, it may take a little longer, and the health worker will explain when to come back. Self-tests also give results in a short time; follow the waiting time in the instructions.

0.68
  A CSA-0095 [contraception] Where to get contraceptive pills in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get combined and progestin-only pills at public health centers and health posts, youth corners, and pharmacies. In many areas, community health workers can also give pills to continue a method. A health worker will ask some questions and may check your blood pressure. Call 114, the free RBC line, to find services near you.
  B PRA-0083 [pregnancy] Where to get a pregnancy test in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get a pregnancy test at a health center, and many health posts and youth corners can also help. Pharmacies sell urine pregnancy tests. A community health worker can tell you where to go near you. At a health center, a nurse can test you, explain the result and talk with you about next steps. You can also call 114 for free information.

0.68
  A HIV-0074 [hiv] Couples HIV testing: Couples HIV testing means you and your partner test together and receive your results together with a counsellor. It helps you both know your status, plan how to protect each other, and support each other whatever the results. It also builds trust. Each person still has the right to decide. Ask at your health center or youth corner about testing together.
  B HIV-0076 [hiv] When one partner has HIV and the other does not: A couple where one partner has HIV and the other does not is called a discordant couple, and it is more common than people think. Such couples can stay together and have a healthy relationship. When the partner with HIV takes treatment and keeps an undetectable viral load, HIV is not passed through sex. The negative partner can also use PrEP and condoms, and test regularly. A counsellor can support you both.

0.68
  A HIV-0072 [hiv] Can an HIV test be wrong: HIV tests are very accurate, but rarely a result can be wrong. A negative result may miss a very recent infection because of the window period. A first positive result is always confirmed with more tests to avoid mistakes. That is why health workers follow a set testing process. If you doubt your result, talk to the health worker about retesting rather than ignoring it.
  B HIV-0079 [hiv] Does a normal blood test check for HIV: No. A normal blood test for things like malaria, blood count or sugar does not automatically test for HIV. HIV testing is done only with your knowledge and agreement, and with counselling. If you want to know your HIV status, you need to ask specifically for an HIV test. Never assume you are negative because you had other blood tests.

0.68
  A PRB-0037 [pregnancy] Eating well on a small budget in pregnancy: You can eat well in pregnancy without expensive food. Beans, peas, groundnuts and small dried fish are cheap sources of protein and iron. Dark green leaves like amaranth and pumpkin or cassava leaves are nutritious and can be grown at home. Eggs, seasonal fruits, sweet potatoes and sorghum porridge are also good. If your family is struggling to get enough food, tell your community health worker or health center, as support programs may exist.
  B PRB-0039 [pregnancy] Vegetarian diet in pregnancy: If you do not eat meat or fish, you can still have a healthy pregnancy. Eat plenty of beans, peas, lentils, soybeans, groundnuts and other nuts for protein and iron. If you eat eggs and milk, they are excellent. Add dark green vegetables and fruits rich in vitamin C to help absorb iron. Take your iron and folic acid supplements as advised. Tell your health worker about your diet so they can check you have enough nutrients.

0.68
  A HIV-0023 [hiv] HIV risk in sex between men: Men who have sex with men can get HIV mainly through anal sex without a condom, which carries a high risk. Oral sex has a much lower risk. Using condoms with water-based lubricant every time, testing regularly, and asking about PrEP can greatly reduce the risk. Health services should treat everyone with respect and confidentiality. You can talk to a nurse at a health center or call 114 for advice without being judged.
  B HIV-0046 [hiv] Having many sexual partners and HIV risk: Having more than one sexual partner, especially at the same time, increases the chance of HIV, because each partner may have other partners too, creating a network where HIV can spread quickly. You often cannot know your partners' status. Fewer partners, using condoms every time, testing together, and PrEP if you are at high risk all lower your risk. This is about staying safe, not about judging anyone.

0.68
  A HIV-0070 [hiv] What an inconclusive HIV test result means: An inconclusive or indeterminate result means the test could not clearly show whether you have HIV or not. This can happen if you were infected very recently or for other technical reasons. It does not mean you are positive. The health worker will ask you to test again after some time, following national guidelines. Keep protecting yourself and your partners until you get a clear result.
  B HIV-0072 [hiv] Can an HIV test be wrong: HIV tests are very accurate, but rarely a result can be wrong. A negative result may miss a very recent infection because of the window period. A first positive result is always confirmed with more tests to avoid mistakes. That is why health workers follow a set testing process. If you doubt your result, talk to the health worker about retesting rather than ignoring it.

0.68
  A PUB-0010 [puberty] First sign of puberty in boys: For most boys the first sign of puberty is that the testicles get bigger and the skin of the scrotum becomes thinner, a bit darker and hangs lower. Many boys do not notice this at first. Next, pubic hair starts to grow and the penis gets longer and wider. The growth spurt, voice change and facial hair usually come later.
  B PU-001 [puberty] What is puberty: Puberty is the time when your body changes from a child's body to an adult's body. Your body, your feelings and your social life all change. It is normal and healthy. It does not start at the same age for everyone, and girls usually start earlier than boys.

0.68
  A PRB-0028 [pregnancy] Healthy eating in pregnancy: A healthy pregnancy diet includes a variety of foods every day: staples like sweet potatoes, cassava, maize or rice; beans, peas, groundnuts, eggs, fish or meat for protein; dark green vegetables like dodo (amaranth) and cassava leaves; fruits; and milk if you can. Eat an extra small meal or snack each day. Use iodised salt, limit sugary drinks and very salty food, and drink clean water. Good food helps your baby grow and keeps you strong.
  B PRB-0032 [pregnancy] Food taboos in pregnancy: In some families, pregnant women are told not to eat certain foods, like eggs, meat or some fruits, because of beliefs that they harm the baby or make birth difficult. Most of these beliefs are not true, and avoiding healthy foods can leave you and your baby without important nutrients. Eggs, meat, fish, milk and fruits are good in pregnancy if prepared safely. If you are unsure about a food, ask your health worker.

0.68
  A HIV-0006 [hiv] Why symptoms cannot tell you if you have HIV: You cannot know if you have HIV by checking how you feel. Early signs like fever, rash or sore throat are the same as many common illnesses such as flu or malaria, and many people with HIV have no signs at all for years. Feeling healthy does not mean you are HIV negative, and feeling sick does not mean you are positive. The only way to know is to take an HIV test at a health center or youth corner.
  B HIV-0007 [hiv] You cannot tell someone's HIV status by looking: There is no way to know if someone has HIV by looking at them. A person can look strong, healthy and beautiful and still have HIV. Many people living with HIV take treatment and look and feel well. Thin or sick-looking people may have other illnesses. Judging people by their appearance can give you a false feeling of safety. The only way to know anyone's status, including your own, is an HIV test.

0.68
  A HIV-0057 [hiv] Age for using an HIV self-test: According to the Rwanda HIV Guidelines 2022, HIV self-testing is for people aged 16 and older. If you are younger than 16, you can still get tested by a health worker at a health center or youth corner, where you will also get counselling and support. Anyone using a self-test should confirm a reactive result at a health facility.
  B HIV-0080 [hiv] Can someone test me for HIV without my consent: HIV testing should be voluntary. Health workers should explain the test and get your agreement before testing you, and young people 12 and older in Rwanda can give their own consent. No one, including a partner, school or employer, should force you to test or test you secretly. If you feel your rights were not respected, you can talk to the health facility management or call 114.

0.68
  A CSA-0122 [contraception] How long after sex EC pills work: Emergency contraceptive pills can be taken up to 5 days (120 hours) after unprotected sex. The sooner you take them, the better they work, so do not wait until the next morning if you can get them today. Even on day 4 or 5 they can still help. A copper IUD can also be fitted within 5 days.
  B CSA-0145 [contraception] EC after missed regular pills: If you missed several combined pills, or were late with a progestin-only pill, and had unprotected sex, emergency contraception may be needed. Talk to a pharmacist or health worker quickly, as it depends on how many pills you missed and when. Keep taking your regular pills, and use condoms until you are protected again.

0.68
  A HIV-0072 [hiv] Can an HIV test be wrong: HIV tests are very accurate, but rarely a result can be wrong. A negative result may miss a very recent infection because of the window period. A first positive result is always confirmed with more tests to avoid mistakes. That is why health workers follow a set testing process. If you doubt your result, talk to the health worker about retesting rather than ignoring it.
  B HIV-0092 [hiv] What an HIV test cannot tell you: A standard HIV test tells you whether you have HIV. It does not tell you when you got it, who gave it to you, how strong your immune system is, or whether you have AIDS. Other tests, such as viral load and CD4 count, help with that after a diagnosis. Try not to blame yourself or others based on a test; focus on getting care and support.

0.68
  A HIV-0115 [hiv] Where to get PEP in Rwanda: You can get PEP at health centers and hospitals in Rwanda. If it is night or a weekend, go to a hospital emergency service or the nearest facility that is open. After sexual assault, go to an Isange One Stop Centre or call 3029. Tell the staff you need PEP and what happened. You can also call 114 for information.
  B HIV-0124 [hiv] Is PEP free in Rwanda: PEP is provided free of charge at public health facilities in Rwanda, and care for survivors of sexual violence at Isange One Stop Centres is also free. Do not let worry about money stop you from going quickly. If you are asked to pay at a public facility, you can call 114 for information.

0.68
  A STI-0004 [stis] Chlamydia signs in boys and men: Many boys and men with chlamydia notice nothing. Some get a clear or cloudy discharge from the tip of the penis, burning or pain when urinating, itching inside the penis, or pain and swelling in one testicle. Infection in the anus or throat often has no signs. Other infections can cause the same problems, so the only way to know is a test at a health center. Testicle pain should always be checked quickly.
  B STI-0012 [stis] Gonorrhoea signs in boys and men: Boys and men with gonorrhoea often notice signs a few days after sex. These can include a thick white, yellow or green discharge from the penis, burning or pain when urinating, and sometimes pain or swelling in a testicle. Some men have no signs at all. Other infections can look the same, so only a test at a health center can tell. Please go soon, and avoid sex until you are checked.

0.68
  A PUB-0109 [puberty] One testicle hangs lower: It is very common and normal for one testicle to hang lower than the other, often the left one. This helps them not press against each other when you walk or sit. It is not a sign of a problem. However, if a testicle suddenly moves higher, becomes painful, swollen or hard, go to a health facility straight away.
  B PUB-0111 [puberty] Only one or no testicle in the scrotum: Sometimes a testicle does not move down into the scrotum before birth, or moves back up. If you can feel only one testicle, or none, in your scrotum, it is important to see a health worker. It is often treatable, and it is better to check early because an undescended testicle can affect fertility later. It is nothing to be ashamed of.

0.68
  A CSA-0136 [contraception] Copper IUD as emergency contraception: A copper IUD put into the womb within 5 days after unprotected sex is the most effective emergency contraception, preventing more than 99 out of 100 possible pregnancies. A trained provider at a health facility puts it in. It works even for people for whom pills may work less well. After that, it can stay in as a long-term method.
  B CSB-0055 [contraception] How the copper IUD prevents pregnancy: The copper IUD is a small, flexible T-shaped device placed inside the womb by a trained provider. The copper changes the way sperm move so they cannot reach and fertilise an egg. It has no hormones, so it does not change your natural cycle, though periods may become heavier. It works for many years and can be removed whenever you want.

0.68
  A CSA-0033 [contraception] Where to get free condoms in Rwanda: In Rwanda you can get free condoms at public health centers and health posts, and at youth corners or youth-friendly services. Some community health workers also give out condoms. You can buy condoms at pharmacies and many shops. You do not need to be married or to bring a parent. Call 114, the free RBC line, if you want to know the nearest place.
  B CSA-0132 [contraception] Where to get EC in Rwanda: In Rwanda, emergency contraceptive pills are available at health centers, youth corners and many pharmacies. Go as soon as possible after unprotected sex. A health center can also tell you about the copper IUD as emergency contraception. If you were forced to have sex, Isange One Stop Centre (3029) gives EC and other care for free. You can call 114 for advice.
